VELOCITY ARENA
Teachers Schedule Classroom Prep Running the Program Student Experience
NARRATIVE
Narrative One student · all 16 days · every activity

Maya's
Velocity Arena

A single student walks through every activity from Day 1 to Day 16. Every scene shows what she did and felt in the room; every exemplar box shows the actual artifact she produced — a worksheet row, a code snippet, a sentence on a sticky note. This is what the program looks like from inside one kid's hands.

Days 01020304 05060708 09101112 13141516
Meet Maya

Maya is fourteen. She just finished eighth grade and starts high school in September — a new school, bigger and louder than the middle school she just left, in a building she has never set foot in before. Her parents enrolled her in this summer program at the new school because her older sister told them she would have loved this at her age. Her sister is going to college in the fall as a physics major. Maya loves her sister. Maya is not her sister.

Maya does not like math. Something stopped clicking around fifth grade and never started clicking again. Her last report card had a C+ in pre-algebra and she felt good about the plus. Algebra 1 is on her schedule for the fall — she is dreading it. She has spent the last four years making sure adults know she is "not a math kid," because if she names it first, no one else gets to name it for her. She is not afraid of numbers. She is afraid of being asked to be wrong in front of people.

On Day 1 she walks into a classroom in the high school she will attend in the fall — a room that does not look like a classroom. Robots on every small group table. The walls have a few areas taped off and labeled but empty — Leaderboard, Bot Names, Rivalry Cards — as if the room is waiting for something to happen in it. Behind the teacher's desk, a printed PEMDAS reference poster is already up — the same one every middle-school math room has — along with a small Formula Reference card she'll learn to use later. Maya notices the poster, recognizes it from sixth grade, and looks away. She has no idea what any of the empty-labeled wall areas mean yet. The teacher waves everyone over and asks them to form a circle. Within five minutes Maya is standing in that circle, and the teacher is explaining how to play Circle Pass.

DAY 01

Speed · Welcome · The First Bot Drive

Week 1 · Play Lab + Boot Camp
Maya's arc today: Outsider in a room of strangers → has a team → has touched a robot → has done one piece of real math in front of people who didn't laugh.
Where Maya is nowDay 1 start. 14, going into 8th grade. Pre-algebra was rough. She's here because her mom signed her up for the summer enrichment slot at her new high school and she didn't push back. She does not expect to be good at this. She does not expect to like it. She is tired before anything has happened.
HOOK

Sound and Movement: Circle Pass + Mirror Pairs

When Maya's turn arrives she claps once and lifts her left foot. The girl to her left copies it exactly, then adds a snap. By the third lap around the circle the room is loud and uncoordinated and nobody is performing — they are just doing it. In Mirror Pairs she gets matched with a boy named Devon who moves slowly enough that she can keep up; she moves slower for him on the swap. Nobody mentions math. Nothing is graded. Maya notices that her shoulders are no longer at her ears.

She has not said her name yet, but she has been copied by another person three times. That counts as something.
HOOK

Name + One Word

The teacher asks everyone back into the circle. "Go around. Say your name and one word that describes how you feel right now." Maya can hear other kids saying things like "excited" and "good" and "ready." She does not feel those things. She is third from the end. By the time her turn comes she has decided to tell the truth.

What Maya said out loud — first time in the room
"Maya. Tired."

The teacher nods once and moves on without commenting. Nobody laughs. Nobody asks why. Maya realizes she just said her own name in front of nineteen strangers and survived.

CHALLENGE

Pre-Task Diagnostic — Prior Knowledge Assessment

The framing the teacher gives is the only reason Maya is still in her seat: "This is not a test. There is no grade. You are filling this out so I know what I already know." Maya answers what she knows, writes "not yet" on three of the eight questions, and moves on. She does not look up. Nobody else looks up either.

Diagnostic — partial transcript
QQuestion (paraphrased)Maya's answer
1If 3 apples cost $1.20, what does 1 apple cost?$0.40
2Plot (3, 5) on a coordinate grid.(plotted correctly)
3What is the slope of a line through (0, 0) and (2, 6)?not yet
4If a battery starts at 100% and drops 15% per minute, what is the charge at minute 3?not yet
5Find the distance between (1, 1) and (4, 5).not yet

Maya answers 4 of 8. The three she leaves are slope, linear decay, and the distance formula — every one of which she will be able to do by Day 5.

REVEAL

Driving Question Reveal + Team Formation

The teacher walks to one of the blank wall sections and unrolls a poster. Big block letters, large enough to read from the back of the room: "How can you use data, equations, and code to build a robot athlete that wins on math, not luck?" She reads it aloud once, slowly, and tapes it to the wall. Math, not luck. Maya reads it twice, the second time without meaning to. The teacher does not explain it. Then she points to the team rosters on the wall. Maya is on a team with Devon (from Mirror Pairs) and a girl named Iris who has bright orange shoelaces. The three of them walk over to their table together. Iris talks fast and decides faster; she carries a chewed-up Bic pen tucked behind one ear. Devon is quieter — moves and speaks at the same slow tempo he showed in Mirror Pairs. Their bot is sitting in the middle of the table, cute and small — not what Maya thinks a robot is supposed to look like. Iris pokes it. It does not move.

Team. She has a team. The word still feels strange in her head. So does "math, not luck."
CHALLENGE

Stat Card Distribution

Each student gets a paper card with four rows: Speed, Endurance, Turning, Power. All four start blank. Today they will only fill in the first one. The team has to land on one number. Iris (Coder this week) reads the budget rule off the teacher's board — "20 points total, all four stats combined. So whatever Speed we pick, we have 20 minus that left for the other three." Maya, who just watched her bot at Speed = 10 hit 50% motor power and miss the turn, says: "We saw 10 was too fast on the carpet — the bot overshot. And zero is dead. So somewhere in the middle." Devon, doing math in his head: "Six leaves us fourteen for Endurance, Turning, Power. That's enough for a real bot in three stats." Iris writes SPEED = 6 on her own card; Maya and Devon match. Not because anyone's certain. Because they have to commit to something to keep going, and 6 fits the test data they just saw.

Stat Card — Maya's, end of Day 1 
STAT CARD · Maya · Team: Iris/Devon/Maya
─────────────────────────────────────
SPEED       __6__   ← filled today
ENDURANCE   _____
TURNING     _____
POWER       _____
─────────────────────────────────────
Total / 20:  6 of 20 allocated
CHALLENGE

Speed Stat Challenge — What Does Motor Power Actually Mean?

The formula goes on the board: Motor Power % = Speed × 100 ÷ 20. The teacher asks the class what 100% means in everyday life. A boy says "phone battery." A girl says "health bar in a video game." The teacher nods and points to the formula. Before anyone calculates anything, every student writes a prediction for Speed = 10. Maya writes 50% — not because she did the formula, but because 10 is half of 20, and half of 100 is 50. She treats the answer like a guess. She is right and assumes she got lucky.

The teacher directs the students to a website and a webpage with the speed activity. They are told to work on their own devices, but that teams can work together.

Speed Stat Challenge worksheet — Maya's row
SpeedMaya's calcMotor Power %
11 × 100 ÷ 205%
33 × 100 ÷ 2015%
55 × 100 ÷ 2025%
66 × 100 ÷ 2030%
88 × 100 ÷ 2040%

Sentence at the bottom: "Every 1 of Speed adds 5%. I can just multiply by 5 instead of doing the whole formula every time. Easier."

MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

First Bot Drive — Speed in Real Life

The central question on the board and on the next webpage: does doubling your Speed double the distance the bot travels in 2 seconds? Before anyone touches a robot, Maya circles "yes — proportional" on her worksheet, because that's what the formula says. She does the same on the webpage. Then Iris uploads the code, Devon hits go, and they measure with masking tape and a tape measure — a starting line, a finish mark, and the actual cm read off the tape. At Speed = 3, the bot moves 18 cm in 3 seconds. At Speed = 6, it moves 44.6 cm. Maya wrote down 36 as the prediction. The actual is almost 9 cm farther. The formula was wrong. Or the bot was. Maya looks up from the worksheet and notices, against her will, that she wants to know which.

Data dashboard — Maya's team, Day 1 entries
SpeedPredicted (2s)Actual (cm)Ratio vs. Speed 3
318 cm18.0 cm1.00×
636 cm44.6 cm2.48×

Maya's written sentence at the bottom of the worksheet:

"Doubling Speed should have doubled the distance. It didn't — it went 2.48 times as far, not 2 times. The formula is close but not exact. The bot is doing more than the math said it would. I don't know why yet."
OPEN LAB

Free Code Time

For 45 minutes the teacher says nothing useful. She circles the room and asks one question per table: "What are you trying?" Maya's team tries to make the bot do a tight figure-eight, partly because Iris said it out loud first and Devon shrugged and Maya didn't want to be the one who killed the idea. It does not work. They try again with different numbers. It still does not work. Maya does not feel discouraged. She feels something closer to annoyed — annoyed in the way you get when a puzzle is right there and the answer is hiding. She has not felt that in school in a long time.

She leaves without finishing the figure-eight. She decides she will come back to it tomorrow. The decision feels like hers, not assigned. That is also new.
ENDS

Goals Wall — Personal + Team

Three minutes to write one personal goal and one team goal. Post them on the wall. Maya writes hers in small block letters.

Goals wall — Maya's two sticky notes
Personal: Talk in front of the team at least once this week without my voice shaking.
Team: Finish the figure-eight.
DAY 02

Endurance · Decay · Naming the Bot

Week 1 · Play Lab + Boot Camp
Maya's arc today: Makes a mistake on purpose in front of 19 people and gets cheered for it → graphs her first real linear decay → has a role (Analyst) → has a bot with a name.
Where Maya is nowDay 1 left her tired but not hating it. The team got chosen and the math part hadn't started yet. Today the math part starts. She still expects to be the worst one at her table.
HOOK

Failure Bow

The teacher goes first: she trips on purpose, takes an exaggerated bow, and the room erupts. Real or fake, big or small, doesn't matter. Maya's turn is fourth. She drops her pencil on purpose, bends to pick it up, and gives a very small bow. The room cheers anyway — loud, generous, not at her, with her. Something inside her unclenches one click.

She made a mistake on purpose and was rewarded for it. The shield she has been carrying for four years has a crack in it that wasn't there yesterday.
HOOK

Slow Down, Slow Down

The teacher calls a tempo and the room matches it. Slower. Slower. Slower than Maya thought a group of 18 teenagers could go. By the time they're moving in slow motion she can hear the fan.

The room is calmer than any classroom she has ever been in.
CHALLENGE

Endurance Stat Challenge — Drawing the Decay

Each student opens the Endurance Discovery interactive lesson on a device. Eight slides. The first one shows an animated power bar draining out of the lesson's sample bot — called Bot #14, with Endurance = 6 — and only the first three minutes of data. The mission card says one sentence: "Figure out what rule controls this battery." On the Notice slide the full data appears: the bot loses exactly 21% per minute for three minutes, then suddenly stops dropping at 20%, even though the pattern says it should keep falling. Maya types what she sees. By the Model slide the lesson has revealed the rule — both the linear drop and the floor. By the Explore slide she is plugging her team's E = 4 into the same formula on paper. On the Lock In slide she writes Endurance = 4 on her stat card and confirms.

Maya's typed observation — Slide 2 (Notice)
"It drops the same amount every minute — 21%. But then it just stops at 20%. Like the battery has a floor. Even the formula isn't allowed to go below it, because then the bot would stop moving completely."
Maya's paper table — Slide 6 (Explore), her own team's E = 4
t (min)(20−E) × 1.5 × tRaw P(t)After floor
00100%100%
12476%76%
24852%52%
37228%28%
4964%20% (floor)
5120−20%20% (floor)

Maya's sentence under the table:

"It loses the same 24% every minute — that's the slope. Our line is steeper than Bot #14's because our Endurance is lower. We also hit the floor a minute earlier. (I think the floor is the program saying *the bot still has to move even when it's tired*.)"
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Program Endurance Into Your Bot

Iris uploads the starter code with the formula plugged in. Devon places Patience on the masking-tape start line. Each press of Button B fires one burst — the bot drives for a fixed window of time at whatever power the decay formula says is left, then stops and waits for the next press. The room is loud. Half the teams are just hammering B because it is fun to press buttons. At Patience's table, Devon presses B four times in a row without pausing. The bot skids forward in a single messy lurch. Maya looks at the numbers in her notebook — 44.2, 30.1, 17.5, 5.0 — and realizes she has no idea which burst was which. She writes them in the table anyway, guessing at the order.

The teacher walks past one table and sees the same thing: kids spamming B and a column of numbers that don't match the decay graph. She calls a quick freeze. "Right now a lot of us are just making the bot move. That's fine. But this activity is secretly about one question: does each burst shrink by the same amount the formula says it should? You can only see that if you fire one burst at a time and label each one." She holds up a sample data table with B1, B2, B3, B4 written down the side. "Reset your bots. This time, say the burst number out loud before you press the button."

Devon resets Patience and presses B once. "Burst one." Maya measures 44.2 cm and writes it next to B1. Second press: 30.1 cm at B2. Third: 17.5 cm. Fourth: 5.0 cm. The numbers fall faster than the line on her Endurance graph predicted. Maya watches the bot slow down and feels the drop, one burst at a time, instead of as a blur.

4-Burst Endurance Test — Maya's data log (after restart)
BurstPredicted (cm)Actual (cm)Gap
144.644.20.4
233.930.13.8
323.217.55.7
412.55.07.5

Gap sentence at the bottom:

The formula said the bot would decay linearly. It did, but faster than the line. Maybe the motor loses more than 24 per round because of something the formula doesn't include — friction? heat? I don't know yet.
(Written after we re-ran the test one burst at a time like the teacher showed.)
At first, this block felt like chaos — everyone was just pressing B and writing down whatever numbers came out. When the teacher stopped the room and said out loud what the experiment was actually for, the whole thing slowed down. Maya still doesn't know why the actuals drop faster than the line, but she now knows what question she's really asking.

Common failure mode. Students spam the button and record unlabeled distances; the decay pattern and burst-by-burst comparison never surface.

In-the-moment save. If you see multiple teams with one long skid instead of four bursts, pause and (1) restate the goal ("Does each burst shrink by the same amount the formula predicts?"), (2) model labeling bursts B1–B4 aloud, and (3) have them re-run the test with one press per measurement.

TRANSITION

Role Rotation Briefing

Three roles on the board: Coder, Fabricator, Analyst. The teacher explains: "This week — Week 1 — every member of every team shares every role. You will take turns at the screen, take turns on the build, take turns with the data. By the end of this week, you will pick which role each of you commits to for Week 2 — and from there it rotates every Monday so every student holds every role at least once over the four weeks." Then she calls for the commitment now, on Day 2, so each team knows what's coming: "Talk for two minutes and write your Week 2 starting roles on the team card." Iris says it first: "I'll be Coder." Devon picks Fabricator without hesitation — he liked the cardboard yesterday. Maya gets Analyst; nobody argues. The teacher walks past and nods once. "Locked. Now go share every job this week so you know what the other two feel like."

Team role card — Iris, Devon, Maya · committed Day 2 
TEAM ROLES · 4-WEEK CAMP
──────────────────────────────────────────────
          Week 1      Week 2     Week 3     Week 4
──────────────────────────────────────────────
Iris      shared      Coder    → Fabric.  → Analyst
Devon     shared      Fabric.  → Analyst  → Coder
Maya      shared      Analyst  → Coder    → Fabric.   ← me
──────────────────────────────────────────────
Week 1 — every role, every member, every day.
Weeks 2–4 — committed role, shifts each Monday.

The Analyst's data tells the Coder what to change. The Fabricator's setup determines whether the data is accurate. Maya reads her column twice. She knows what is hers starting Monday.

MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Bot Naming Ceremony — Generated by the Math

The teacher opens the Robot Team Name Generator on the projector and says one sentence: "The math will generate your bot's name. Not random — determined." The I-Do takes four minutes. The We-Do walks the whole class through sample inputs x = 4, y = 3, z = 2, working three formulas in sequence — a = 2x + y − 1, b = (a + z) mod 12, n = 3z² − x — and landing on Kinetic Falcons #8. This is where Maya catches both gotchas: she almost writes 2x as 2 + x, then catches herself; and when the teacher writes 3z² on the board she has to remember it means 3 × (z²), not (3z)². Then it is the You-Do, and every student plugs in their own values. Maya works on paper before touching the tool: x = 4 (M-A-Y-A), y = 5 (May), z = 3 (their team number). While she is double-checking, she notices Devon writing (3 × 3)² = 81 next to her arm. She does not say anything. She just slides her paper toward him with the order of operations written out. He looks, says "oh," and re-does it. They post all three of the team's calculated names on the wall. Iris reads them aloud. Maya's name is "Patient Foxes #23". Iris says, "Patient Foxes. I like it. Fits the Endurance build." Devon shrugs and nods. They write the full name on the sticky note, then shorten it for everyday use: Patience.

Maya's Bot Naming worksheet — her own inputs 
INPUTS         x = 4   ← letters in "Maya"
               y = 5   ← her birth month, May, written as a number (Jan=1, Dec=12)
               z = 3   ← Team #3

ADJECTIVE      a = 2x + y − 1
                 = 2(4) + 5 − 1
                 = 8 + 5 − 1
                 = 12             → adjective[12] = "Patient"

ANIMAL         b = (a + z) mod 12
                 = (12 + 3) mod 12
                 = 15 mod 12
                 = 3              → animal[3]    = "Foxes"

TEAM NUMBER    n = 3z² − x
                 = 3(3²) − 4
                 = 3(9) − 4
                 = 27 − 4
                 = 23

GENERATED      Patient Foxes #23   ←  nickname: Patience

Sticky note on the Bot Names wall:

PATIENCE · Patient Foxes #23 · Team 3: Iris / Devon / Maya · Endurance focus

Yesterday Maya almost said "Patience" out loud as a guess. Today the math gave it back to her. She does not point this out. But she notices.

Exit Challenge — 3 minutes on paper, no tool

Inputs: x = 2, y = 5, z = 3. Compute n = 3z² − x.

Maya's work:
  n = 3(3²) − 2
    = 3(9) − 2
    = 27 − 2
    = 25

  Answer: n = 25.

She writes her name on the slip and hands it to the teacher on her way out. Correct. Three students at the next table turned in 79 — they computed (3z)² − x = (3·3)² − 2 = 81 − 2 = 79, treating 3z as one base instead of recognizing that the exponent applies only to z. The teacher notes their names. She will revisit the misconception with them on Day 3.

OPEN LAB

Free Code Time

Patience completes a figure-eight on the fourth try. Iris cheers. Devon high-fives Maya without thinking about it. Maya does not flinch.

Figure-eight done. Goal from Day 1: closed.
ENDS

Name One Math Concept — Write the Calculation, Translate to Behavior

Four minutes. Name one math concept you used today. Write the full calculation. Share it with a teammate. Then explain what it means for your bot — not in formula terms, in behavior terms. Maya picks linear decay because she has had it in her head all afternoon.

Maya's ENDS reflection

Concept: Linear decay.

CALCULATION       P(2) for Endurance = 4
                  P(t) = 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 × t
                  P(2) = 100 − (20 − 4) × 1.5 × 2
                       = 100 − 16 × 3
                       = 100 − 48
                       = 52%

Translation to bot behavior:

"By two minutes into any match, Patience is at 52% power. That means halfway through, she is already only half as strong as at the start. If we want her to win in the last minute, we either reallocate Endurance higher, or we use a strategy that only needs short bursts."

Iris's only response: "Yeah. That changes Season 1."

DAY 03

Turning · Coordinates · The Distance Formula

Week 1 · Play Lab + Boot Camp
Maya's arc today: Learns that natural language can't program a robot, learns the distance formula, walks an arc with her own feet, and watches Patience trace the pattern her math predicted.
Where Maya is nowDay 2 ended with Patience named and Maya labeled Analyst. She still doesn't know what an Analyst does. Today she gets one answer: the Analyst is the person who turns a stat number into a prediction about a real distance. She didn't know math could do that.
HOOK

Human Robot Programming Challenge

Pairs of students. One plays robot. The other gives instructions. Maya is the robot first; Devon directs her. "Move forward three steps. Turn 90 degrees left. Move forward two steps." She does — but each "step" she takes is a different size, and the 90-degree turn ends up at maybe 70 degrees, and she lands a foot off where Devon wanted her. They swap. Maya is more specific. "Move forward, heel-to-toe, four times. Pivot left until your shoulders face the door." Devon ends up closer. Still not exact.

Words are not precise enough to drive a robot. That is why formulas exist. Maya did not know that was a problem formulas solved.
CHALLENGE

Distance-Time Graphs + The Distance Formula

Two scatter plots side-by-side: Run A and Run B. The teacher says nothing — teams plot, then write one sentence about why the lines look different. Maya writes "Run B was slower." The teacher walks by, taps the paper, and says one word: "Why?" Maya looks again. The slope is shallower. She rewrites: "Run B's Speed allocation was lower — the slope is smaller, so it travels less distance per second." Then comes the distance formula on the arena grid. Maya catches herself adding |x₂ − x₁| + |y₂ − y₁| instead of using the quadratic. The teacher does not correct her. Iris does. Maya redoes it.

Distance worksheet — Maya's calculation 
Bot starts at (1, 1). Ball at (4, 5).
What is the straight-line distance?

First attempt (wrong):
  |4 − 1| + |5 − 1| = 3 + 4 = 7 squares

Iris: "It's not a city grid. Use the c² one."

Corrected:
  d = √((4 − 1)² + (5 − 1)²)
    = √(9 + 16)
    = √25
    = 5 squares ✓

Sentence underneath: "Walking around the block is not the same as going through it. The Pythagorean way is shorter. Always."

MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Coordinate Navigation — Arc Approximation

The activity opens with a launch sequence that brings the morning's distance formula straight into the room. The arena is an 8×4 grid with the ball at (4, 2) — dead center. Teams pick one of the two starting corners: (0, 4) or (8, 0). Maya's team picks (0, 4). She does the math on her worksheet — d = √((4−0)² + (2−4)²) = √(16 + 4) = √20 ≈ 4.47 squares × 27 cm/square ≈ 121 cm. But the bot doesn't drive in cm — at SPEED = 6 it drives at a constant PWM for whatever number of milliseconds you tell it. So Maya does a second conversion. She flips back to her Day 1 Speed Stat Challenge notes: at SPEED = 6, Patience covers 1 cm every 33 ms (her measured calibration). Multiply: 121 × 33 ≈ 4000 ms. Iris programs Patience to cruise at 30% PWM for 4000 ms, then stop — using the Launch Sequence Quick Help page since she has never written a Cutebot program from scratch before. Patience drives. They measure: 8 cm short of the ball. Maya logs the gap. Before they move on the teacher says it once: "Save your launch sequence. Name it so you can find it. You will reuse this on Day 5." Iris hits the Share button in MakeCode, copies the URL, and pastes it into the team's notes — labeled LAUNCH-V1 · (0,4)→(4,2) · 121 cm · 4000 ms @ SPEED 6.

Then the embodied piece. Maya walks a 2-segment "arc" to a target spot (it looks like a triangle, not a curve), then walks an 8-segment one (it looks like a curve, almost). She gets that more segments approximate a curve but never equal one.

Back at the worksheet — before anyone reveals the formula — teams calculate turnRatio for T = 5: 0.2 + 5 × 0.02 = 0.30. Maya draws three arc paths on the grid for T = 2, 5, and 10, predicting where each will land. Only then does the teacher turn to the board and write the formula they all just used: turnRatio = 0.2 + T × 0.02. Maya already had it on her paper.

Now the MakeCode part. The team opens the Turning Test program. The defaults all start at zero — SPEED, ENDURANCE, TURNING — and the teacher says one thing: "If any stat is zero, the bot won't move. Fill them in from your stat card before you flash." Iris is on the device today — Week 1 has everyone trading roles, and right now it is her turn. She types in SPEED = 6, ENDURANCE = 4. Then they cycle through the Coverage Challenge: TURNING = 2, flash, place Patience on the start, press Button A. The bot roams for 15 seconds tracing gentle, long arcs; the RGB strip on top alternates blue when arcing left and green when arcing right. Maya tallies squares covered on the taped grid. Reflash with TURNING = 5. Reflash with TURNING = 10. With each higher T, the arcs get sharper and shorter. At T = 10, Patience hits the white border — and Maya watches it brake, back up, turn 115°, and keep going without anyone touching it. It recovered itself.

Launch Sequence — Maya's distance calculation 
STARTING CORNER     (0, 4)
BALL POSITION       (4, 2)

STEP 1 — DISTANCE FORMULA
                    d = √((x₂−x₁)² + (y₂−y₁)²)
                    d = √((4−0)² + (2−4)²)
                    d = √(16 + 4)
                    d = √20
                    d ≈ 4.47 squares
                    d ≈ 4.47 × 27 cm/square ≈ 121 cm

STEP 2 — TIME CONVERSION  (the bot drives in ms, not cm)
                    Day 1 calibration: SPEED 6 → 33 ms per cm
                    ms = 121 × 33 = 3993 ≈ 4000 ms

PROGRAM             pwmCruiseControl(30, 30) for 4000 ms, then stop.
ACTUAL              Patience stopped 8 cm short of the ball.
GAP NOTE            Distance formula was correct.
                    The bot just doesn't always drive exactly what it's told —
                    calibration drift, wheel slip, or both.
Coverage Challenge — Patience at three Turning values
TturnRatioArc styleSquares covered (15s)
20.24Gentle, long arcs22
50.30Medium18
100.40Sharp, short11

Maya's synthesis sentence:

"Lower Turning covers more squares — bigger arcs. Higher Turning stays local — tighter, but doesn't go anywhere. I picked T = 5 to be middle for my stat allocation. And the bot recovers from the white border by itself — every time it hits the line it backs up, turns, and keeps going. We don't have to babysit it. That changes what I thought programming the bot meant."
OPEN LAB

Free Code Time

Maya pulls up the data dashboard and writes one full row by herself for the first time. She is not asking Iris if it's right. She just enters the number. On her way past the Goals wall she catches sight of her Day 1 sticky note and pulls a pen out of her pocket.

Maya's Day 1 goal note — annotated on Day 3
Personal: Talk in front of the team at least once this week without my voice shaking. ← done. Picked the bot's name on Day 2.
She put data in a spreadsheet without asking anyone to check it first. Goal closed quietly. Nobody saw her do it.
ENDS

Collaboration Rating — Hold Up Your Number

Each person silently rates today's team collaboration: 1 = rough, 2 = okay, 3 = solid. Hold up your number at the same time so everyone sees at once. If anyone holds up a 1, the team takes two minutes to talk about what specifically happened.

Team Patience — Day 3 collaboration ratings 
Iris    [3]      ← solid
Devon   [3]      ← solid
Maya    [3]      ← solid

No discussion needed.

Three threes. Nobody had to explain anything to anyone. Maya is the one who held hers up first. She did not check the others before she did.

DAY 04

Power · The Quadratic Surprise · Build a Scoop

Week 1 · Play Lab + Boot Camp
Maya's arc today: Discovers that not all formulas are lines — Power² breaks her intuition wide open. Then her hands build something that kicks a ball.
Where Maya is nowThree days in. She has graphed a line (Day 2) and walked an arc (Day 3). Today she finds out math has shapes other than lines, and she has to build something with her hands. The Fabricator usually does the hand part, but today they all do.
HOOK

Finger Speed-Sums — Faster ≠ Better

Students stand in a circle. The teacher gives the rules: hold up some number of fingers; the goal is to call the group total first. Each round speeds up. Round 1 is slow — Maya counts everyone's fingers carefully, gets seven, and someone else calls it. Round 2 she sums faster and wins clean. Round 3 the tempo doubles; Maya shouts "twelve" before she has actually counted — the real total is thirteen. She loses the round to someone who took half a second longer. By round 5, she has noticed the pattern: when she pushed for speed, her error rate went up. Iris laughs. Maya laughs. Then the teacher pulls the class into the debrief: "When did being fast help you? When did it hurt? Where else in your life have you seen this pattern?" The class names sports, video games, music practice. The teacher writes one sentence on the board and leaves it there for the rest of the day: faster ≠ better. Maya copies it onto the inside cover of her journal. "Save this thought," the teacher says. "Power is next. Watch for where this shows up."

Maya's journal — Finger Speed-Sums tally + reflection 
ROUND  PACE      MY CALL   REAL   RESULT
─────────────────────────────────────────
  1    slow         —        7    someone else won
  2    medium       9        9    ✓ won
  3    fast        12       13    ✗ rushed → wrong
  4    fast        11       11    ✓ won
  5    fastest      6        9    ✗ rushed → wrong

PATTERN  Rounds I rushed → wrong (3, 5).
         Rounds I let myself count → right (2, 4).
         Faster ≠ better. Cognitive load is real.

NEXT     Power Stat Challenge. Watch for where
         "more = more" stops being true.
She laughed in front of the team. With them, not at herself. And she has a sentence in her journal that she might come back to in an hour.
CHALLENGE

Power Stat Challenge — Ratio and Motor Speed

The formula goes on the board: Charge Speed % = Power². The teacher asks for a gut prediction for Power = 8 before anyone calculates. Maya writes 40 — she assumed it would be linear like Speed was, so eight times five. Then she does the math: 8² = 64. The teacher pushes the comparison: P = 4 gives 16. P = 8 gives 64. Doubling Power quadrupled charge speed. Maya looks down at the inside cover of her journal, at the sentence she copied moments ago — faster ≠ better — and realizes this is exactly where the teacher told her to watch for it. More Power doesn't just give you more kick. It gives you a lot more kick. Possibly more kick than the ball needs.

The Explore phase uses three tools at once. Teams open the Power Stat Challenge interactive lesson on a device, which walks them through the ratio table and the underlying formula chargePower = POWER². Maya fills in the worksheet column by column — Power, Power², resulting chargePower — then logs a prediction for each before any bot moves. Iris downloads the Power Test program to the bot — Week 1 still has the three of them trading off who's at the screen, and right now it's Iris's turn. They place the ball about 40 cm in front of the bot, set POWER = 2, and press Button A. The bot's ultrasonic sensor pings the ball, computes chargePower (4%), drives until it reaches the ball, strikes through it for 250 ms, then follows through for 190 ms. Maya measures the result. Reflash with POWER = 4. The chargePower jumps to 16 and the follow-through stretches to 260 ms — Patience arrives faster now, with a firm bump that pushes the ball forward maybe a foot before it stops. Reflash with POWER = 8. The chargePower jumps to 64 and the follow-through stretches to 400 ms. Patience races toward the ball at full PWM and slams into it. The bot stops exactly where the code told it to — at the ball — and the ball rolls forward just a couple of feet before stopping. Maya looks at the numbers: she just used a Power² of 64 to move the ball about twice as far as POWER = 4 (Power² = 16) did. Iris is impressed by the speed of the charge. Maya is more interested in the math: at POWER = 8 the bot moved 16× the Power² of POWER = 2, but the ball moved maybe 4× the distance. That's the diminishing return — without something to channel the impact, a bare bumper can only transfer so much energy to a ball. The formula's prediction is mathematically right; the bot just runs out of useful work to do.

Power Stat Challenge worksheet — Maya's row (predicted + bot trial)
PowerLinear guessPower² (actual)chargePower (SPEED=6)Bot trial result
1—1%25Crawls to ball. Ball doesn't move.
2104%26Soft bump. Ball rolls ~15 cm.
42016%34Firm bump. Ball rolls ~30 cm.
84064%134Hard hit. Ball rolls ~60 cm.

Maya's sentence under the table:

"Doubling Power makes Power² four times bigger. But when I doubled Power, the ball only went about 2× as far. So the formula isn't wrong — the bot just can't kick that hard with a bare bumper. Same as Finger Speed-Sums. Past a point, more doesn't help. POWER is whatever's left after the other three stats. For us: 20 − (6 + 4 + 5) = 5. Power 5 is between P=4 and P=8 on the curve. The scoop probably matters more than one extra point. (Building one next.)"
TRANSITION

Power Allocation — Stat Card Fill-In

All four rows on the stat card today, and there is no argument to have. Speed = 6 (Day 1), Endurance = 4 (Day 2), Turning = 5 (locked yesterday after the Coverage Challenge). The four stats must total exactly 20, so POWER = 20 − (6 + 4 + 5) = 5. The team doesn't pick it; the budget picks it. Maya does the arithmetic on her stat card and writes 5 in the POWER row. What she does now know — from the worksheet trials a half hour ago — is what Power = 5 actually means: chargePower = 25, follow-through = 295 ms, ball travel somewhere between her P = 4 and P = 8 measurements.

Stat Card — Maya, end of Day 4

Total / 20:  20 of 20 — locked.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Scoop Build + Ratio Testing

Devon does most of the cutting. Iris does the lego and tape. Maya holds the cardboard steady and takes the measurements. They attach the scoop to Patience's front bumper. Then three kick-distance trials at Power = 2, 4, and 8. Maya records.

"The formula said going from Power 4 to Power 8 would multiply the kick by 4×. The actual was 160 ÷ 44 = 3.6×. Close but not exact — like before. The scoop probably wobbles at high power. Devon wants to reinforce it with tape."
OPEN LAB

Free Code Time

Maya re-reads her notes from all four days laid out next to each other. Four formulas. Four shapes. She makes a small chart on the back of her stat card.

ENDS

Skill Bridge — Today's Skill, Tomorrow's Use

Three minutes. Write one specific skill you used today and one specific moment you'll use it tomorrow.

Maya's Skill Bridge entry
"Today I tested Power = 2, 4, and 8 on the bot and wrote down how far the ball went each time. Our Power = 5 is between 4 and 8, so I know roughly how far one kick will go. Tomorrow when we test loadouts I won't have to guess — I can predict from the numbers I already have."

She underlined predict. She has not used that word about her own math in a long time.

DAY 05

Budget · Cold Recall · First Simulation

Week 2 · Day 1 — Pre-Season Setup
Maya's arc today: Verifies her loadout. Gets 5/5 on a math check for the first time in four years. Watches Patience play a simulation match. And writes down something she has never said out loud before.
Where Maya is nowFour stats committed (Speed 6, Endurance 4, Turning 5, Power 5 — total 20). She has a graphed line, a walked arc, a kick. Today she puts numbers into a dashboard and watches the formulas compute themselves on her own screen. Math has never happened on a screen because of her before.
HOOK

Conditional Simon Says — IF your stats…, THEN…

Five rounds. Every condition is built on the four stats. The teacher's rule: "Right answer, right reason. Wrong and fast doesn't count." Maya pulls out her stat card and lines up the values she has filled in: Speed = 6, Endurance = 4, Turning = 5, Power = 5.

Round 1. "IF Speed = 5, THEN stand up." Maya checks her card. Her Speed is 6. She stays seated. Four students stand. The teacher asks one of them "why?" — "My Speed is 5." Simple equality. Maya gets the shape.

Round 2. "IF Power is greater than 6, THEN stomp twice." Maya's Power is 5. Stays still. Two students stomp. Devon (Power = 7) stomps. The teacher asks: "What values of Power count?" A student answers "seven, eight, nine, ten." The teacher pauses. "What about Power = 6?" The student catches it: "No — strictly greater. Not equal." Maya marks this in her head — strict inequality, the boundary doesn't count.

Round 3. "IF Speed plus Endurance equals 10, THEN clap four times." Maya does the math: 6 + 4 = 10. She claps four times. Iris does not (her Speed + Endurance ≠ 10). Maya is one of seven students who clapped. The teacher asks the whole room a question and does not call on anyone: "What would have to change about your allocation for your hand to go up next time?" She lets the question sit. Maya thinks: if my Endurance had been 3 instead of 4, my Speed would have to have been 7. If Endurance had been 5, Speed would have to have been 5. She has just informally solved for a variable.

Round 4. "IF Power plus Turning is less than 8, THEN wave both hands." Maya: 5 + 5 = 10. Stays still. She glances at Devon — he is starting to raise his hands. She catches him: his sum is at the boundary, and Round 2 just established that the boundary doesn't count. He drops his hands. The teacher catches it too: "What if Power plus Turning is exactly 8 — do you wave?" A student answers "no — less than is strictly less." Same shape as Round 2.

Round 5. "IF Speed plus Power plus Turning plus Endurance equals 20, THEN cheer." Maya adds: 6 + 5 + 5 + 4 = 20. So does everyone else. The whole room cheers. The teacher waits a beat, then quietly asks: "Why did everyone just cheer?" Maya is the one who raises her hand. She says it out loud — first time today: "Because every allocation in the room adds up to 20. The equation is true for all of us." The teacher writes on the board:

What the teacher wrote — and Maya's journal copy 
SPEED + POWER + TURNING + ENDURANCE = 20

Maya copies it into her journal. Underneath, in her own handwriting:

"This is what's been true all last week. We just proved it with our bodies. The teacher called it a budget constraint. It's a line in four variables, whatever that means."

This activity is the deliberate foundation for systems of inequalities on Days 11–14. Today's S + P + T + E = 20 is a single linear constraint in four variables — every student's allocation satisfies it by design. On Day 11, the Commissioner introduces a competing constraint (e.g. "Endurance ≤ 4") and students must find allocations that satisfy both at once. The graphing work on Day 13 builds directly on the strict-vs-non-strict inequality distinction students practiced in Rounds 2 and 4 today.

Maya does not have any of this perspective yet. She doesn't know about Day 11. She is correctly noting that a line in four variables is unfamiliar to her, and recording the term budget constraint for later. The metanarrative arc — single constraint → system → graphing two constraints simultaneously — is yours to hold, not hers.

She just said something out loud in front of nineteen people. About math. Voluntarily. And she generalized — went from "my numbers add to 20" to "every set of four numbers in this room adds to 20." That's algebra. Nobody told her it was. She figured it out by clapping.
WARM-UP

Two-Formula Warm-Up

Two numbers are already on the board when Maya walks in: Speed = 7 and Endurance = 5. Not her team's stats — a hypothetical bot. Three minutes, alone, no devices, no team. Use the formulas on the wall. Compute both outputs from memory. Maya picks up a pencil.

Q1 first. motorPower = SPEED × 100 ÷ 20 = 7 × 5 = 35%. Easy — she has been doing this all week. Q2: P(t) = 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 × t. With E = 5: (20 − 5) × 1.5 = 22.5, so P(t) = 100 − 22.5t. She plots two points in her head — at t = 1 → 77.5%, at t = 2 → 55%. By t ≈ 3.5 the bot hits the 20% floor. Then Q3 — the one with no right answer: "What do both numbers together reveal about how that bot performs?" Maya writes for the full minute.

Maya's warm-up sheet — board values, individual work 
BOARD INPUTS         Speed = 7,  Endurance = 5

Q1  SPEED OUTPUT     motorPower = SPEED × 100 ÷ 20
                                = 7 × 5
                                = 35%

Q2  ENDURANCE OUTPUT P(t) = 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 × t
                    P(t) = 100 − 22.5 × t

                    t = 0   →  100%
                    t = 1   →  77.5%
                    t = 2   →  55%
                    t = 3   →  32.5%
                    t = 3.5 →  ~20% (floor)

Q3 — "What do both numbers together reveal about that bot's performance?"

"Speed 7 is fast at the start — 35% motor power. But Endurance 5 means it loses 22.5% every minute. That's worse than ours. So this bot is fast early and tired fast. By 2 minutes it's at 55%. By 3.5 it's done. If I were on that team I'd try to score in the first minute and a half. After that the Speed doesn't help."

Open discussion. The teacher takes Q1 and Q2 in 30 seconds — easy, factual answers. Q3 takes ten minutes. Three students share. Maya is one of them. She does not raise her hand first, but when the teacher pauses long enough that the silence is going to break, Maya offers the line about the bot being "fast early and tired fast." Two other students build on it. No winner is picked. The teacher closes with: "Two numbers, four sentences of insight. That is what doing the math is for." Then hands out the Budget Constraint worksheet.

She offered a math observation in open discussion. Without being called on. Without being sure she was right. Three days ago she would have written it in her journal and never said it out loud. The journal still got the words. So did the room.
CHALLENGE

The Budget Constraint — Speed + Endurance + Turning + Power = 20

Two rival loadouts are revealed on the board— same 20 points, very different distributions:

The two loadouts on the board
TeamSPEEDENDURANCETURNINGPOWERTotal
Kinetic Cobras 145844420
Galactic Falcons 105484420

The teacher writes the budget equation underneath: S + E + T + P = 20. Then, in red marker: "What are you giving up if Speed = 8?"

Teams open the Budget Constraint interactive lesson and the paper worksheet at the same time. The instructions: design three hypothetical loadouts on the worksheet, then test them in the interactive lesson — calculate all four formula outputs for each. Maya picks three deliberately extreme loadouts so the trade-offs show up clearly. While she's working, she watches Devon adjust his draft — he is about to bump his Speed to 8 without dropping anything else. Sum: 22. She slides her pencil across his paper. "You added 2. You owe 2." He drops his Endurance from 6 to 4. Sum: 20. He nods.

Maya's worksheet — three hypothetical loadouts, all four formula outputs each
LoadoutSETPmotorPowerchargePowerdecay slopeturnRatio
A · Speed-heavy844440%16−240.28
B · Endurance-heavy484420%16−180.28
C · Power-heavy444820%64−240.28
(ours, for reference)645530%25−240.30

Maya's answer to the red-marker question, at the bottom of the worksheet:

"What are you giving up if Speed = 8? 12 points for everything else. If Speed = 8, then E + T + P = 12. That's only 4 each on average. So you're fast (40% motor power) but your kick is weak (16), your decay is fast (−24/min), and your turning is meh. You're fast and that's it. Every + has to come from a −."

Maya finishes the three loadouts and the answer sentence with ten minutes to spare. The teacher walks past her table, sees the worksheet flipped over, and points to the projector: "Crack the Bot. Try it." Maya opens Crack the Bot on her device. The puzzle: a mystery bot's five formula outputs are given, and the student has to reverse-engineer the four stat values that produced them. She has been doing forward calculations all week. This is the inverse problem. Her first bot: THE MARATHON — flavor text reads "Built to last. High endurance, balanced everywhere else."

Crack the Bot — Maya solves THE MARATHON 
GIVEN OUTPUTS (THE MARATHON)
  Motor Power        =  20%
  Power at t = 3 min =  46%
  Turn Ratio         =  0.28
  Charge Speed       =  16%
  Follow-Through     =  260 ms

Maya works each formula backward:

  Motor Power = S × 5
  20 = S × 5                        →  SPEED = 4

  Power at t = 3:  100 − (20 − E) × 4.5  = 46
  100 − 46 = 54
  54 ÷ 4.5 = 12
  (20 − E) = 12                     →  ENDURANCE = 8

  Turn Ratio = 0.2 + T × 0.02 = 0.28
  T × 0.02 = 0.08                   →  TURNING = 4

  Charge Speed = P² = 16
  P = √16                           →  POWER = 4

  Follow-Through check:
  120 + P × 35 = 120 + 4 × 35 = 260 ✓

BUDGET CHECK   4 + 8 + 4 + 4 = 20 ✓
ANSWER         (S, E, T, P) = (4, 8, 4, 4)   "THE MARATHON"

Underneath, on the worksheet margin:

"THE MARATHON is the opposite of Patience. They put 8 in Endurance and 4 everywhere else. Their decay is −18/min, way slower than our −24. By minute 3 they're still at 46% — we'd be at 28%. If we play them, we have to score in the first 60 seconds. They beat us late."

At minute 45 the teacher pauses the room for the Misconception Correction. She does not name names — she just puts a wrong loadout on the board: Speed = 9, Endurance = 5, Turning = 5, Power = 4. Total: 23. "What's wrong here?" Three hands. The teacher takes one: "They added points without subtracting any." She writes underneath: "Every + needs a − somewhere else. The equation has to balance." Then she picks two team loadouts for whole-class discussion. Team Patience's loadout C is one of them. Maya stands up and explains the Power = 8 trade-off without looking at her paper.

She just used the word trade-off out loud in front of the room. She used it correctly. She used it like it had always been her word.

Watch for the equals-sign error during the Explore phase — students adding to one stat without subtracting from another — but do not correct individual students when you see it. Save it for the public Misconception Correction at minute 45. The error caught publicly and anonymously becomes a teaching moment; corrected privately, it becomes a shaming one. This is the same misconception that will block algebraic balance work on Day 13.

CHALLENGE

Cold Recall Check — Five Problems, Individual, No Grade

Five math problems on paper. Individual. Ten minutes. The teacher says again: "This is not a grade. It is what I look at to know where the room is." Maya picks up the pencil. The first question makes her pause for a second — not because she can't do it, but because none of the problems are about bots. They are about recipes, cars, lines, budgets, and grid distances. Then she sees it: these are all the same things from this week, just wearing different clothes. She works the problems in seven minutes. Checks twice. Hands it in.

Cold Recall Check — Maya's answers
#QuestionMaya's answerCorrect?
1A line passes through (0, 4) and (3, 10). What is the slope?m = (10 − 4) ÷ (3 − 0) = 6 ÷ 3 = 2✓
2A recipe scales proportionally: 3 cups of flour makes 9 servings. How many cups are needed for 15 servings?3 / 9 = x / 15 → x = 5 cups✓
3A car drives at 75% of its maximum speed. The maximum speed is 120 km/h. What is the car's actual speed?0.75 × 120 = 90 km/h✓
4A 20-point budget assigns 7 points to one category and 6 to another. What percentage of the total budget is still unallocated?20 − 7 − 6 = 7 left; 7 / 20 = 0.35 → 35%✓
5Calculate the distance from (1, 1) to (4, 5). Show every step.d = √((4−1)² + (5−1)²) = √(9 + 16) = √25 ≈ 5.2✗

Score: 4 / 5. Question 5: Maya wrote √25 ≈ 5.2. The teacher circles the squiggle and writes underneath: "√25 is exactly 5. No rounding."

Maya looks at it for a long second. I rushed. I didn't even check that one. √25 is the easiest square root in the question. She'd written ≈ out of habit, like she'd been doing for the messy ones all week.

In the margin, in pencil, very small, where the teacher won't read it unless she looks for it:

"Slope = decay rate. Recipe = proportional. 75% = endurance left. Budget = budget. Distance = distance formula. These are the same five things from this week. Algebra was here the whole time. Next time, check if √x is a clean number before writing ≈."
4 out of 5. She has not gotten 4 right on a math anything since fifth grade. The one she missed she missed because she rushed, not because she didn't know. She does not say this to anyone. She knows the score is not the point. The score told her she can do this stuff. The mistake told her how she'll lose points when she does.

The Cold Recall problems are deliberately written in non-bot contexts. This is the assessment that tells you whether students transferred the algebra from bot form to other contexts. A student who can compute P(t) for an Endurance value but cannot find a slope from two points has memorized a bot procedure, not learned algebra. A 4/5 with a rounding error — student got every concept but rushed on one arithmetic check — is a normal-good result for a student who arrived shaky on algebra a week ago. Don't overcorrect "Maya got 4/5" as a problem; the missed point is a real student behavior, not a scaffold failure.

CHALLENGE

Dashboard Introduction

The teacher pulls up the Velocity Arena Team Data Dashboard on the projector and walks the room through it using the Pre-Season Setup guide as her own teaching script. The dashboard has two panels stacked vertically. The top is STAT LOADOUT — four big stat cards (Speed, Power, Turning, Endurance), each with its formula written underneath and the computed output in red that updates live as you type a number into the stat. At the bottom of that panel, a budget bar tracks how many of the 20 points have been spent. Next to the budget there's a button: "Set as underdog (+2 budget for Season 2)" — the teacher tells them to ignore it for now; it will matter on Day 11. The bottom panel is SIMULATION LAB: a table with columns Round · Formula · Predicted · Observed · Gap and a form below to add new rows. One row per round per formula. The teacher demonstrates entering one example loadout on the projector, then sets the room loose: each team's Analyst opens the dashboard on their own screen and enters their team's actual stats. Maya types in Patient Foxes #23's numbers and watches the formula outputs compute themselves on her screen. "Wait, what's a row?" a student at the next table asks. The teacher gave a longer answer earlier — one row per round per formula — but the student missed it the first time. The teacher repeats it. Two other tables look up because they had the same question and didn't ask. The instruction wasn't bad. It just needed to be said twice.

Maya types her team's numbers into a dashboard and watches the formulas compute themselves in red on her screen. It's the first time math has happened on a screen because of her, not in a textbook in spite of her. She does not say this aloud. She does notice that she has not looked at the clock since the dashboard opened.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Simulation Matches: Your Loadout vs. Reality

Two minutes to commit. The teacher says it twice: pick one of the three hypothetical loadouts you designed this morning. No new designs. No deliberation. Team Patience confers fast. Maya argues for keeping their actual locked allocation (6 / 4 / 5 / 5) instead of swapping in one of the worksheet hypotheticals — "if we're going to test something, test what we're actually about to run in Season 1." Iris and Devon agree.

MakeCode setup — copy the full game code, plug in your stats. The teacher shares the canonical Velocity Arena game program — the same code that will run in every Season 1 match. Teams don't write it from scratch; they copy the full program and plug in their own stat values. Iris (Coder, this week) opens the share link, makes her own copy, and fills in the four stat slots from Maya's stat card: SPEED, ENDURANCE, TURNING, POWER. The four formulas are already wired in. The four behaviors — motorPower, decay curve, chargePower, turnRatio — all derive from the four numbers Iris just typed.

Three simulation rounds, three minutes each. No opponent. Just Patience running her own program, with Maya (Analyst) logging Simulation Lab rows in real time while Devon (Fabricator) places and resets Patience between rounds. Before each round, Maya enters the predicted column for every formula they plan to track. During the round, she watches; after it ends, she enters the observed column and writes the gap as a plain-English sentence.

Simulation Lab — Maya's log, Rounds 1–3
RFormulaPredictedObservedGap
R1SPEED30% motor power · reach line in 4 sec4.4 sec0.4 sec slower
R1ENDURANCE100% power at t = 0; 76% at t = 60s68% at t = 60spredicted 76, got 68 — off by 8
R1TURNINGturn ratio 0.30 → gentle arcsarcs as predictedmatched

Maya's note in the dashboard's Analyst observations field after Round 3:

"Round 2 showed us something we missed in Round 1. The kick formula doesn't drop over time, so we predicted the same kick distance at 0 seconds and 90 seconds. But the bot kicked WAY less far at 90 seconds. We were wrong.

Took me a minute to figure out why. The kick formula doesn't get tired, but the motor that's doing the kicking does. Patience is tired at 90 seconds, so even though the kick is the same NUMBER on paper, the motors don't have enough power left to actually make it happen.

For Round 3 we predicted differently — kick distance = chargePower times whatever endurance is left. That matched the bot. The formula wasn't lying. It just wasn't telling us the whole story."

Between Round 2 and Round 3, Devon stops resetting Patience for a second to do some quick math out loud. "Round 1 we got three kicks. Round 2 we got two. Round 3, maybe two again." Maya checks her dashboard and confirms — 3, 2, and yeah, probably 2 in the last round. Iris looks up. "And that's with NO opponent. In a real match with another team's bot racing us to the ball, we maybe get half of that. Two real chances per match." Devon nods slow. "Launch sequence has to win the race to the ball. Every time. If they beat us there, we're chasing them all match and we lose." Maya writes a new page in her dashboard notes — How Patience actually scores:

Maya's strategy list for real matches (starting Day 7)

To score a goal, Patience has to do one of these — every time the ball is loose:

  1. Beat the opponent to the ball — fastest, cleanest launch wins the race.
  2. Have a clear kick — don't shoot with the opponent's bot in the way.
  3. Push the ball away from the opponent — if you can't shoot cleanly, push it where they can't reach.
  4. Block the opponent's path to the ball — defense buys time and steals their chances.

None of this matters in today's solo simulation. All of it matters starting Day 7. The launch sequence is the part we control most.

Post-match debrief — the teacher walks the class through the stats in order: Endurance, Turning, Power. Endurance first, because the decay caught most of the room off guard. Turning second, because that one mostly went as expected. Power last, because Power is the stat where the bot did something the formula didn't predict, and figuring out why took the most work. When the teacher gets to Power she asks Iris to share what Team Patience figured out, and Iris explains the kick-distance drop. Then the teacher turns to Maya: "Say back what Iris just said in your own words, then add to it." Maya does: "The kick formula doesn't get tired. The motor that has to carry the kick out does. Same number on paper. Different reality on the floor." The teacher writes the last sentence on the board.

Before anyone moves onto the next activity, the teacher hands out a half-sheet with two questions on it — the only piece of paper she will read tonight. "Two minutes. Individual. Hand it to me when you're done." Maya writes.

Exit ticket — Maya's two answers

Q1. Your bot's Speed is 5 and Endurance is 3. Predict: is your bot moving faster in minute 1 or minute 3 of a match? Explain why using what you know about how Endurance works.

"Minute 1, way faster. Endurance = 3 means the decay rate is (20 − 3) × 1.5 = 25.5 each minute. At minute 1 the bot is still at about 74 (out of 100). At minute 3 it's down to about 23. Speed = 5 is the same number in both minutes, but the motor running that Speed gets weaker every minute. By minute 3 the bot is barely crawling."

Q2. Name one thing you observed about your bot's behavior today. What stat do you think caused it? What change would you make, and what would you expect to see differently?

"Patience kicked the ball about 30 cm fresh and only 25 cm at 90 seconds, even though our Power didn't change. I think Endurance caused it — the kick formula stays the same, but the motors making the kick happen get tired. If I could re-allocate one point I'd move it from Speed to Endurance. We'd lose a little cruise speed (motor power drops from 30 to 25) but our decay rate slows from 24 per minute to 22.5 per minute. I'd expect our kicks at 90 seconds to look more like our kicks at 0 seconds. Less drop-off."
Maya did not say much during the debrief. But the sentence the teacher wrote on the board was hers — and the two paragraphs she just handed in on the way out are also hers. Three days ago she could not have written either one. Today both happened in about ten minutes total, and she has not noticed.

Teams reflash MakeCode with each of their three morning loadouts in turn and re-run the simulation, with Maya (Analyst) logging predicted vs. actual for every formula. Iris (Coder) handles the reflashing. Devon (Fabricator) places and resets Patience.

Off by 2. Loadout B — we predicted 18, she did 18. Off by zero. B matched what we predicted. A just moved more." Iris pauses, then nods. "OK. Yeah." ENDS

Name One Thing You Are Good At That Most People Do Not Know About

"What is one thing you are good at that most people do not know about? Be specific. Name the thing. Give a real example from your own life." Then she sits down at the back of the room with her own journal and writes alongside the students for five minutes of full silence. Maya sits very still for two minutes. Then she writes.

Maya's reflection — five minutes, in pencil
"I notice when stuff is about to break. Or when it is already broken and nobody knows yet. The toaster started smelling weird last fall and my dad was about to throw it out. I pulled the crumb tray and dumped it. Worked fine. My mom gave up on the bathroom sink draining for almost a year. Last spring I unscrewed the cover and pulled out a hairball big enough to fill a coffee mug. The drain has worked since. Nobody asked me to do either of these. I just noticed. I do not say I am good at this because then people will ask me to fix THEIR stuff and I do not want to fix their stuff. I want to fix MY stuff, when I want to. But I am good at it."

The teacher stops writing at the five-minute mark and opens the room. Volunteers first, one sentence each. Three hands. "I can identify any bird call in my neighborhood." "I can juggle four bean bags." "I can fall asleep anywhere — buses, classrooms, the dentist." Laughter, finally. Then the teacher reads from a list of two students who haven't shared today. They each give one sentence. Iris shares she can imitate her grandma's accent perfectly. Devon gets called and says he can name every starting lineup of the 2014 Detroit Pistons. Maya does not get called. The teacher built her shortlist during the silent writing minutes — she had been watching the room — and left Maya off it on purpose. Something about how long Maya sat still before she started writing told the teacher this reflection was a private one.

She does not share it with anyone. She does not have to. She wrote it down. And the teacher, did not force her to say it out loud.
DAY 06

Field Geometry · The First Residual · The Loop Closes

Week 2 · Day 2 — Final Pre-Season Prep
Maya's arc today: Second day as Analyst. Solves the Speed formula running backwards before the teacher names it as algebra. Runs the distance formula for Field Geometry, logs the first residual of the camp (+4 cm), tells the Coder what to change, and watches the second trial land within 1 cm. The loop she didn't know was the job — observe, propose, verify — closes for the first time. Without anyone telling her that was what she just did.
Where Maya is nowDays 1–5 got her in the room and on a team. Day 6 puts her in front of a feedback loop with real consequences (the launch sequence has to hit a target). For the first time she observes a gap, names it as a number, and tells the Coder what to change. The loop closes. She doesn't yet know it has a name.
HOOK

Equation Relay Race

Teams of 3 at whiteboards. On the board the teacher has written the Speed formula with a question mark where the value goes: motor power = (? / 20) × 100. Three steps, three people: Person 1 substitutes the given value, Person 2 does the division, Person 3 multiplies by 100 and calls the answer out loud as a percent. Wrong answer = team is not done. The teacher does the launch script slowly and refuses to give the Speed value until the last sentence. Then: "Round 1: Speed = 8. Marker goes to Person 1. Go."

Round 1 — Speed = 8 — Answer 40%. Iris is Person 1 for Team Patience. She writes (8 / 20) × 100, slaps the marker down, taps Maya on the shoulder. Maya is Person 2. She writes 0.4 × 100 — not 40 — and taps Devon. Devon calls out "forty percent!" They place second. From the table to their right, a different team yells "four thousand percent!" and laughter ripples across the room. Maya understands instantly — they wrote 8/20 = 40 instead of 0.4. She does not say anything. The teacher does not say anything either. The room teaches itself.

Round 2 — Speed = 5 — rotated lineup — Answer 25%. Maya goes first this round (the teacher cycled positions). She writes (5 / 20) × 100. Devon does the division and writes 0.25 × 100. Iris closes and calls "twenty-five percent!" They place third out of four teams running. The top two advance to the Final. Team Patience is out. The teacher hands them Card A on her way past: "Speed = 17. Find motor power."

Maya does Card A in about twenty seconds. 17 / 20 = 0.85. × 100 = 85%. She slides it across to Iris and Devon. They both nod. The teacher walks by again and drops Card B on their table. This one is different — "Motor power = 35%. Find Speed." Iris reads it, raises her eyebrows: "It's running backwards." Maya stares at the formula for a second, then writes.

Card B — Maya's solution, written before the final ends 
GIVEN     motor power = 35%
FORMULA   motor power = (Speed / 20) × 100
SOLVE     35 = (Speed / 20) × 100

          Divide both sides by 100:
          35 / 100 = Speed / 20
          0.35 = Speed / 20

          Multiply both sides by 20:
          0.35 × 20 = Speed
          7 = Speed

ANSWER    Speed = 7

She did not plug 35 into the formula like Speed. She worked it backwards. She did not think about it as algebra. She thought about it as "I know the answer; what was the input?"

The final ends. The teacher calls time. She holds up Card B in front of the room: "Someone who worked Card B — what did you have to do differently to find Speed instead of motor power?" A student says: "You had to undo the formula." The teacher nods. "Card B answer: Speed = 7. Here's how it runs backwards: 35 = (Speed / 20) × 100. Divide both sides by 100. Multiply both sides by 20. Speed = 7." She pauses. "That is algebra. You just did it."

Algebra. The word she has been dreading for September. Her schedule has it as the class she is most nervous about. She just did it on a whiteboard in twenty seconds without noticing. Nobody made a big deal out of it. Nobody told her she was a math kid now. They moved on to the next activity. Maya did not move on quite as fast.
CHALLENGE

Official Stat Allocation — Interleaved Problem Set

Individual silent work. No team huddling. No formula sheet visible anywhere in the room. The worksheet itself has the six concept tags printed at the top with their definitions, problems below — Maya reads the definitions once before starting. The teacher reads the task once aloud: "Twelve problems. Before you calculate any of them, write the concept tag in the tag column. No tagging is an incomplete answer. If you have a question, write it on your paper — I will not be answering questions during the work period. Begin." Then she walks the room slowly with her clipboard, deliberately unhelpful. Hands go up; she does not meet eyes. After about four minutes, the room stops waiting for her.

Worksheet header — concept tag reference printed on every sheet
TagDefinition (as printed)
unit rateA fixed amount of output per 1 unit of input
linear decayOutput that decreases at a constant rate over time
linear functionOutput that increases at a constant rate with a non-zero starting value
nonlinear functionOutput that grows by a changing amount as input increases
proportional scalingOutput that scales in direct proportion to input from zero
ratioA part-to-whole relationship within a fixed total

Maya works in order. She gets the Speed problems first because she has done that formula a hundred times this week, and she tags them unit rate — the ÷20 step is the giveaway. She notices the worksheet writes the formula (S × 100) ÷ 20 — multiply first, then divide. The Relay this morning had it (8 / 20) × 100 — divide first, then multiply. Same formula. Sequenced multiply-first here so MakeCode doesn't trip on the decimal. And the shortcut she wrote on her Day 1 worksheet still applies: 100 ÷ 20 = 5, so (S × 100) ÷ 20 simplifies to S × 5. She uses the multiply-by-5 trick on the rest of the Speed problems and writes the long form once in the margin so the teacher can see she knows the formula too. She gets the Power square problems next and tags them nonlinear function, glancing at the definition ("output grows by a changing amount as input increases") to confirm. The Endurance decay problems take a little longer — she has to remember to use (20 − E), not just E — and she tags them linear decay. The trap waits at the Turn Ratio problems. Her instinct is to tag them unit rate, because of the × 0.02. Then she stops and re-reads the definitions printed across the top of her sheet. Unit rate: fixed amount per 1 unit, from zero. Linear function: constant rate, non-zero starting value. She writes 0.2 + (0 × 0.02) = 0.2 in the margin. Not zero. Linear function, not unit rate. She erases unit rate and writes linear function. When she gets to the Follow-Through problem a few rows later — 120 + (P × 35) — she catches the same shape immediately. Constant plus variable times multiplier. Starting value 120 ms, not zero. Same structure as Turn Ratio. Linear function on the first try. She just finishes when the teacher calls time. Two students at the next table finished the main twelve minutes ago and have been working through a digital version of the problem set. This version has immediate feedback on each tag and answer. Maya is not in that group. She is in the larger group that finished the paper version right when time is called.

Extra credit. The bottom of the worksheet has two chaining problems that reach back into the main twelve — find Question 6's motor speed %, and find Question 12's charge power. Each one asks the student to take a stat value from an earlier problem and run it through a second formula. Maya does not get to them. The teacher calls time as she's writing the last tag on Problem 12.

Interleaved Problem Set — Maya's last four answers
#ProblemTagAnswer
9Turn Ratio if T = 2?linear function0.2 + (2 × 0.02) = 0.24
