Verification walk. After pencils down, the teacher picks one problem per formula type. She asks Maya: "Walk me through how you knew which formula to use for Problem 9." Maya explains it out loud — "if T is zero the ratio is still 0.2, so it can't be a unit rate. The printed definition says unit rate goes from zero. This one doesn't." The teacher initials Maya's sheet and moves on. Three students at the next table do not get their initial today; the teacher hands them a formula practice sheet for the Open Lab block instead of letting them into MakeCode. No initial = no MakeCode. Maya is in the MakeCode-ready stack. Maya puts the worksheet in her folder with the extra credit blank, the way the page tells her to: Keep this sheet — it goes in your data log.

Prove Your Build — official Season 1 lock-in. The Analyst on each team opens Prove Your Build on their device with their teammates at their shoulder. The header reads Day 6 · Lock In. The opener prompts her: "We are going with Loadout ___." Maya types Patient Foxes #23. Then the four stats from her card — Speed 6, Endurance 4, Turning 5, Power 5 — and the budget bar fills as she types: 6, 10, 15, 20. ✓ Budget balanced lights green.

Step 2 unlocks. Six formula rows appear, each one with its computed value live on the right and the prompt "This formula is a —" with six tag chips below it. The team works through them together. Iris reads the first row off the screen — Motor Power = S × 5 = 30% — and elbows Maya: "that's your Day 1 trick. Multiply by 5." Maya taps unit rate. The row turns green and a short explanation slides in underneath. They go through the rest the same way — Iris calling each formula from her growing code-side familiarity, Devon adding what he's seen the bot actually do, Maya making the tag call. Endurance: linear decay. Turn ratio: linear function. Charge: nonlinear function. Follow-through: Devon points at the screen — "same shape as turn ratio, right?" — and Maya nods. Linear function. Budget: ratio, all three of them say at once. Six green rows. The Lock In Season 1 button at the bottom of the page turns yellow.

Maya clicks it. A loadout card reveals: Patient Foxes #23 in big yellow letters, ✓ Locked In, all four stats stacked across the top, the five computed outputs in a grid, and the six confirmed tag pills lined up under Math Confirmed. The save fires off to the facilitator's sheet behind the scenes — invisible to Maya, but it's the receipt the teacher will pull tomorrow morning. Devon reads the card off the screen: "We're locked." Maya nods. Season 1 starts tomorrow.

Exit ticket. Two problems. Two minutes. Slips dropped on the desk before they start the next activity.

Exit ticket — Maya's answers

Q1. Which Velocity Arena formula is nonlinear? Write it out in full.

"Charge% = P². The gaps get bigger each time P goes up by 1. P = 4 → 16. P = 5 → 25. P = 6 → 36. Gap from 4 to 5 is 9. Gap from 5 to 6 is 11. Bigger every step. That's the nonlinear part."

Q2. A bot has Endurance = 6. The match lasts 4 minutes. What is P(t)? Show your work.

"P(t) = 100 − (20 − 6) × 1.5 × 4 = 100 − 14 × 1.5 × 4 = 100 − 84 = 16%. The bot is at 16% power by minute 4. Almost done."
She just spent fifty minutes naming whether functions are proportional, linear, nonlinear — the exact vocabulary her September Algebra 1 teacher will use. She did not realize it was that vocabulary while she was doing it. She just thought of it as "is this thing a straight line, and does it go through zero?" Two days ago that was a question she would have avoided. Today it is just the question.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Field Geometry + Approach Planning

The arena is taped out on the floor — an 8×4 grid with the ball A at (4, 2), opponent's goal G at (8, 2), their own goal at (0, 2). Before they pick a starting corner, the team has to do the Field Geometry worksheet's distance analysis. Part 3 asks Maya to calculate the distance from each of the four corners to the ball. She runs the formula four times and finds the answer she didn't expect: every single corner is √20 ≈ 4.47 units from the ball. Same distance from all four. The ball sits at center; corner choice doesn't change the launch distance at all.

Part 4 asks for two specific candidate positions to compare. Both have to be on Team Patience's own half (x = 0 to 4 — soccer doesn't let you start in the opponent's territory). Maya picks P1 = (0, 4) and P2 = (0, 0) — both left-side corners, just at opposite ends of the y-axis. Same distance to ball, same distance to goal, both miror images of each other. So what actually distinguishes them is the launch path: from P1, the bot's L-path goes through (0, 2) — which is their own goal location, meaning the bot rolls right past its own goal during launch. From P2, same situation — the bot also passes (0, 2). Either corner makes the bot brush past its own goal on the way to the ball. Maya notes it: the launch path interferes with the team's own goal area no matter which left-side corner they pick. That's a setup constraint the team has to work with, not a strategic choice.

The team picks P1 = (0, 4) because that's where Devon already taped the bot during simulation matches and the launchSequence is already tuned for that direction (left turn → 2 blocks sideways → right turn → 4 blocks forward). Re-tuning for P2 would mean swapping every "left" for "right" and re-running every trial. Geometry doesn't pick; the existing tuning does. Maya writes that on the reflection line and moves on.

Maya's Field Geometry worksheet — Part 3 + Part 4 + Data Log 
PART 3 — Distance from each corner to the ball A(4, 2)
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
(0, 0) → A:  d = √((4)² + (2)²)   = √(16+4) = √20 ≈ 4.47
(8, 0) → A:  d = √((-4)² + (2)²)  = √(16+4) = √20 ≈ 4.47
(0, 4) → A:  d = √((4)² + (-2)²)  = √(16+4) = √20 ≈ 4.47
(8, 4) → A:  d = √((-4)² + (-2)²) = √(16+4) = √20 ≈ 4.47

  ALL FOUR EQUAL. Ball is centered; corner doesn't change
  the launch distance. (And we can only legally start on
  our own half anyway — left side, x = 0 to 4.)

PART 4 — Compare P1 vs P2 (both on our own half)
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
P1 = (0, 4)        d to A = 4.47    d to G = √68 ≈ 8.25
P2 = (0, 0)        d to A = 4.47    d to G = √68 ≈ 8.25

  Closer to ball:  Same.
  Closer to goal:  Same.
  Geometrically equivalent — mirror images.

NOTE — Launch-path constraint
  Both P1 and P2 have the bot passing through (0, 2),
  which is our own goal location. Either corner forces
  the launch L-path to brush past our own goal on the
  way to the ball. That's a setup we work around.

REFLECTION — Why P1
  Geometry doesn't decide between P1 and P2. We pick P1
  because that's where Devon taped the bot during Day 5
  simulation matches and our launchSequence is already
  tuned for that direction. Swapping to P2 would mean
  flipping every "left" turn to "right" and re-tuning.
  Lock in P1 — keep the existing trials.

DATA LOG
  Starting position (x, y):     (0, 4)
  Distance to ball A:           4.47 units
  Distance to goal G:           8.25 units
  Motor power = Speed × 5 =     30%       Speed stat: 6

Now the launch sequence itself. Maya starts to run the distance formula again — d = √20 ≈ 121 cm — then stops a second time. A straight diagonal from (0, 4) to (4, 2) would hit the ball at an angle and scatter it sideways instead of pushing it toward the goal. Patience has to hit the ball head-on, moving along the goal direction at the moment of contact.

The launchSequence in the game code from Day 5 already handles this. Five steps mapped to the grid:

launchSequence — the L-path Maya inherits 
Step 1   Set block size: 1 block = 27 cm  (= 1 grid square)
Step 2   Turn 90° left   — now facing the center line
Step 3   Drive 2 blocks  → 2 × 27 = 54 cm
         (0, 4) → (0, 2) on the center line
Step 4   Turn 90° right  — now facing the goal
Step 5   Drive 4 blocks  → 4 × 27 = 108 cm
         (0, 2) → (4, 2) head-on at the ball, then kick

TOTAL    54 + 108 = 162 cm  (vs the 121 cm straight diagonal)
         Longer path, but the ball gets hit toward the goal.

Maya walks through the geometry: from (0, 4), turning left points the bot at the center line; 2 blocks sideways lands at (0, 2); a 90° right turn squares it up with the goal axis; 4 blocks forward reach the ball at (4, 2) head-on. The block counts are fixed in the code. The ms each block takes is whatever the bot's actual speed is at SPEED 6. Four blocks is four blocks. The number of milliseconds it takes is whatever the bot does on the floor.

But today isn't just "does the default work." Yesterday's simulation showed the team they only get two real scoring chances per match against an opponent — and the launch sequence is the part they control most. This is their chance to make the code better than it was yesterday. The Launch Sequence Design worksheet says it plainly across the top: Precision gets it there. Reliability gets it there every time. And it makes them design two strategies before testing either one. A and B. Predict which is more consistent. Test both. Decide.

Maya sketches both on her worksheet. Strategy A — Direct Hit: the L-path she just worked out. 90° left, 2 blocks, 90° right, 4 blocks, kick. 162 cm total. Sharp turns, predictable, head-on contact. Strategy B — Buffered Approach: 45° left turn, diagonal cruise to (2, 2), 45° right back to square, 2 blocks forward, kick. 130 cm total. Shorter path, smoother angles, harder to tune because the 45° turns have to land exactly. In the Prediction box, Maya checks Strategy A — Direct Hit. Her one reason: "Two 90° turns are easier for the bot to repeat than two 45° turns at exact angles. A is more reliable, even if it's a longer drive." She passes the worksheet to Iris.

MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Launch Sequence Programming + Testing

Three trials of Strategy A. Iris codes the L-path tunables, downloads, hands the bot to Devon. Devon places Patience to (0, 4). Maya stands near the ball with the worksheet's trial table. Trial A1: Patience runs the L-path, stops 4 cm past the ball — kick still fires clean toward the goal. Hit ball? Y. Good kick? Y. Maya circles both, writes "+4 cm overshoot — shave ms" in the notes column. Iris tunes. Trial A2: 3 cm short of the ball, kick fires anyway because the bot is touching the ball when it triggers — but the ball goes off-line by 5°. Y / N. Maya tunes the ms again. Trial A3: dead-on contact, ball goes straight at the goal. Y / Y. Strategy A: 3 hits out of 3. 2 clean kicks, 1 off-line.

Three trials of Strategy B. Iris changes the tunables — 45° turns and a shorter forward leg. Trial B1: the 45° diagonal lands the bot at (2.2, 2.3) instead of (2, 2); the 45° right turn squares up, but the forward leg hits the ball at a shallow angle and the ball squirts sideways. Y / N. Trial B2: same problem, slightly worse — the bot ends up at (2.3, 2.1) and grazes the ball. Y / N. Trial B3: Iris adjusts the diagonal time down by 200 ms; Patience lands closer to (2, 2) this time and hits the ball cleanly. Y / Y. Strategy B: 3 hits, 1 clean kick out of 3.

Maya's worksheet — Part 2 trial results 
STRATEGY A — Direct Hit (L-path, 90° turns)
─────────────────────────────────────────────
TRIAL  HIT BALL?  GOOD KICK?  NOTES
A1     Y          Y           +4 cm past ball — clean kick toward goal
A2     Y          N           −3 cm short — ball goes off-line by 5°
A3     Y          Y           Dead-on — ball straight at goal

STRATEGY B — Buffered Approach (45° diagonal)
─────────────────────────────────────────────
TRIAL  HIT BALL?  GOOD KICK?  NOTES
B1     Y          N           Diagonal landed long; ball goes sideways
B2     Y          N           Same problem — angle is off
B3     Y          Y           Shaved 200 ms — finally clean

Strategy A hit the ball  3  out of 3.   (clean kicks: 2)
Strategy B hit the ball  3  out of 3.   (clean kicks: 1)
More consistent:  A  ✓

Faster approach:   B  (shorter path)
More accurate:     A  ✓
More reliable:     A  ✓

Post-kick landing — Phase 2. The worksheet asks one more question before the decision. Where does the bot END UP after the kick? Because the match doesn't stop when the kick fires — Phase 2 starts, and the bot roams autonomously, bouncing off walls and reacting to the ball. Where Patience lands when the kick is over decides what arc of the field she covers next. After Strategy A, Patience ends near the center of the field, around (5, 2) — 360° of arena to react to wherever the ball goes. After Strategy B, the angled approach leaves her near the right wall at about (4, 1.5) — her roaming arcs get cut short by the wall. Strategy A wins this dimension too.

Decision. Maya circles Strategy A on her worksheet and fills in the sentence: "I chose Strategy A because it hit the ball 3 out of 3 times. It is more reliable. After the kick, my robot ends near center field, which helps roaming because she has all four directions to react and the wall isn't blocking her arcs." Her Day 5 prediction held — the 90° turns ARE easier for the bot to repeat than the 45° turns. The simpler strategy was the better one.

Revise one variable. The worksheet's last instruction: change exactly one thing in your chosen strategy, run it again. One change at a time — that's how you know what actually worked. Maya picks the forward leg's ms. "A1 was +4 cm, A2 was −3 cm, A3 was dead-on. The ms is locked. But what about Turn 1? It's a 90° in-place turn — what if we shaved 20 ms off the turn-rest pause? Faster off the corner without changing the geometry." Iris shaves 20 ms from the post-turn pause. Trial A4: Patience runs the same path, same hit, same clean kick, but 40 ms faster overall (the change applies to both turns). Maya logs the new total launch time in the data log.

Data Log entry — Team Patience launch sequence (locked) 
FINAL STRATEGY        A — Direct Hit (L-path, 90° turns)
HIT RATE              3 of 3   (clean kicks: 2 of 3 → 3 of 3 after Trial A4)
TURN 1                90° left in place,   post-turn pause 100 ms
TURN 2                90° right in place,  post-turn pause 100 ms
POST-KICK LANDING     center field (~5, 2)
PHASE 2 READINESS     Good — 360° coverage, no wall blocking arcs
TOTAL LAUNCH TIME     ~40 ms faster than default after Trial A4 revision

Big Idea
"A good launch is not just about hitting the ball once. Ours worked
3 out of 3 tries, and it left Patience near the center of the field —
which means she can react to the ball no matter which way it goes."
She has been in role for one day. She just ran A versus B like an actual engineer — predicted, tested, compared, decided, revised. Nobody told her that was the whole job. She did it without being told. And her prediction was right.

She showed her work in front of nineteen people. She did not flinch. She did not have to.
OPEN LAB

Free Code or Final Verification

Maya pulls up the dashboard and runs back through every trial Iris flashed today. She enters the residual column for the first time — predicted distance vs. actual, with the sign — and notices it: every trial went 3–5 cm past where we wanted it to stop. She writes in the dashboard's Analyst observations field: "Every trial today went 3–5 cm past where we wanted. Same gap every time. I think the motor keeps going a little after the code says stop. Try taking 4 cm off all the launch distances next time and see if that fixes it." Iris reads over her shoulder, says "true, that," and nods.

She just did something an Analyst does. She found a pattern in the residuals and named a code change to test. Iris will implement it tomorrow because Maya named it tonight. That is the loop.

DAY 07

First Real Match · Slope-Intercept · The Bot Maya Programmed Plays

HOOK

Trashketball Warm-Up Round

A recycling bin sits about four meters from the seats — far enough that even a seated underhand shot has real miss probability. Every table has a stack of scrap paper. The scoreboard on the board has three columns — Round 1 · Round 2 · Total — and the teacher writes each team's name in the rows while they watch. "Yesterday you built your stat card. Today that card decides who earns the shot." Then the four rules: (1) calculate independently on your own paper, then agree as a team. (2) All three stand together — one person standing doesn't count. (3) The teacher checks the answer before the shot. Wrong answer, sit back down and keep working. (4) Shooter rotates: Round 1 = Person 1, Round 2 = Person 2, tiebreaker = Person 3. Iris is Person 1. Devon is Person 2. Maya is Person 3 — she only shoots if it goes to a tiebreaker.

Scoring goes on the board next to the scoreboard: 1 point for the first team standing with the correct answer. +1 bonus point if your shooter — from their seat, not the aisle, not standing — sinks it. Same in Round 2. Tiebreaker is 1 point for the correct answer if you need to run one. Max total: 4. Then the teacher writes a sentence underneath in smaller letters: "Score is a pretext. Post it anyway."

Round 1. "Turning = 6. What is the turning ratio?" Maya knows this one cold from yesterday's Interleaved Problem Set — Turn Ratio is a linear function, multiply first, then add the 0.2 starting value. She writes 6 × 0.02 = 0.12, then 0.2 + 0.12 = 0.32. Iris matches. Devon matches. All three stand. The teacher walks over. "What's your answer?" "Zero point three two." "Earned. One point. Iris, shoot from your seat." Iris stays in her chair, crumples her paper, lobs it underhand across four meters of air. The shot bounces off the rim and into the bin anyway. Bonus point. Round 1: Patience earns 2 points — 1 for the correct answer + 1 for the made shot. Across the room, two teams stand with 0.124 — they added 0.2 + 6 first, then multiplied. The teacher hands their papers back without saying which step broke. They sit and rework it.

Debrief 1. The teacher calls Iris to walk through her steps. Iris does: "6 times 0.02 first because of the parentheses — that's 0.12. Then 0.2 plus 0.12 is 0.32." The teacher says it back to the class in her own words: "So the parentheses mean 6 × 0.02 gets computed first, giving 0.12, and then you add 0.2." Then she names the practice on the board: "Working systematically with the formula. Same move you'll need this afternoon when you sit down for the Pre-Match Prediction Huddle and compute your opponent's turn ratio from their stat card."

Round 2. "Endurance = 3. Power at minute 3." This one's harder. Maya works the formula fast from her stat card. (20 − 3) = 17. 17 × 1.5 = 25.5. 25.5 × 3 = 76.5. Steady Hawks #17 scrapes their chairs to stand before Maya is done with the last step. Maya rushes — rounds 76.5 to 77 in her head — and writes 100 − 77 = 23%. She glances at Iris and Devon. Iris has 23.5%. Devon has 23.5%. Maya frowns: "I got 23." Devon points at his paper: "100 minus 76 point five — not 77. The decimal carries through. It's 23.5." Maya stares for a second, redoes the subtraction in her head. He's right. She crosses out 23, writes 23.5. All three stand — but Steady Hawks #17 is already standing, and the teacher walks to them first. "What's your answer?" "23.5%." "Earned. Person 2, shoot." Steady Hawks #17's Person 2 lobs from her seat. Goes in clean. Round 2: Steady Hawks #17 earns 2 points — same way Patience earned R1. Standings after Round 2: Patience 2, Steady Hawks #17 2, everyone else 0. Patience earned nothing in Round 2 — they had the right answer, but the script's rule is firm: the point goes to the first team standing with the right answer. Standing second with the right answer earns nothing.

Debrief 2. The teacher pulls another team's paper that wrote 20 × 1.5 × 3 = 90 → P(3) = 10 — they skipped the first subtraction. She names the second practice: "Respecting mathematical structure. Skip the (20 − E) step, and for an opponent with Endurance 8 you'll predict they end the match at 64% — when they actually end at 46%. Order is not optional." Half the room nods like they get it. The other half is still looking at the wrong-paper example trying to figure out which step the team skipped. Maya is in the second half. She doesn't ask, because she doesn't want to be the kid who didn't follow. She writes "come back to this" in the margin of her journal. The debrief moves on without her.

Tiebreaker. The scoreboard reads Patience 2 — Steady Hawks #17 2. Tied. "Different formula. Check your stat card. Person 3 shoots if you earn it." The problem: Speed = 7. What is motor power %? Maya doesn't even reach for paper — Speed × 5, her own Day 1 shortcut. 7 × 5 = 35%. Iris and Devon write it out and confirm. All three stand. Patience first. The teacher checks. "35%. Earned. One point. Maya, shoot." Maya picks up the crumpled paper. Four meters across the room. She has been Person 3 the whole activity — hasn't thrown a single shot. She lobs it underhand, too short. The paper lands a foot in front of the bin. No bonus point — but Patience earned the tiebreaker point for the correct answer. Patience: 3. Steady Hawks #17: 2.

Exit ticket. One problem on the board, individual, ninety seconds. Endurance = 5. Power at minute 2? Maya works it on her scrap: (20 − 5) × 1.5 × 2 = 15 × 3 = 45. 100 − 45 = 55%. Writes 55%, name on top, drops it on the table. The teacher reads the final score aloud — just the numbers, no commentary: "Patience 3. Steady Hawks #17 2. Everyone else 0. Challenge Block starts now."

Final scoreboard

──────────────────────────────────────────────────

Steady Hawks #17              0    2    0    2

Score is a pretext. The teacher posted it anyway.
Devon caught Maya's rounding error in real time. He did not gloat; he just pointed at the decimal. The point five matters. Three days ago Maya would have either dug in (wrong but proud) or shut down (wrong and ashamed). Today she stared at her paper for one second, accepted it, fixed it, and stood up with her team — too late to win Round 2, but right in time to earn the tiebreaker. Then she shot, four meters across the room from her seat, and missed. She didn't make a face. Iris elbowed her: "You got the math. That's the part." Maya nodded. The team won by a point. And Devon — the Fabricator who barely touches the device — turned out to be the one with the cleaner arithmetic on the round that mattered. The team won because they checked each other in time, because the kid who was wrong didn't make it a thing, and because the kid who finally got to shoot took the shot even though it would probably miss.
CHALLENGE

Endurance Decay Equations in Slope-Intercept Form

The teacher pulls up the Endurance Decay Equations lesson on the projector. Three numbered tabs across the top: 1. From Formula to Equation · 2. Two Times on Your Graph · 3. Your Personalized Equation. Progress bar at 0%. "Your bot starts at full power, and every minute power drops. Today you're going to find exactly when that matters — and there are TWO different moments on that line we care about, not just one." The teacher say sbefore anyone clicks into Section 2: write your E and compute r. Maya writes: E = 4, so r = (20 − 4) × 1.5 = 24.

Section 1 — From Formula to Equation. The page breaks the formula into four colored parts: P(t) (cyan: "Power right now"), 100 (green: "Starting power — every bot starts here"), (20 − E) × 1.5 (yellow: "Power lost per minute — this is your slope"), and t (purple: "Time"). Then a red misconception block flashes onto the screen: STOP — most students get this wrong. Higher Endurance does NOT mean more decay. The subtraction (20 − E) flips it: a bigger E makes the decay rate smaller. Maya already knows this from yesterday's Interleaved Problem Set — linear decay, not direct proportion. She nods at the warning and scrolls past. The slope-intercept mapping section maps P(t) = 100 − 24t onto y = mx + b for her: y = −24x + 100. Slope = −24% per minute. Y-intercept = 100% at t = 0. Tab 1 turns green.

Section 2 — Two Times on Your Graph. Two input boxes. Box 1: 20% power floor time. Maya sets P = 20: 20 = 100 − 24t → 24t = 80 → t = 80/24 ≈ 3.33 min. Box 2: true x-intercept. She sets P = 0: 0 = 100 − 24t → 24t = 100 → t = 100/24 ≈ 4.17 min. Two different numbers. She enters both. Iris looks at the screen: "So the math line flattens at 20% at 3.33 minutes — but the line doesn't hit zero until 4.17. Why are there two different answers?" Maya: "Because the power-budget line holds at 20%. The bot's power on paper never goes below 20 — the math line just flattens out." Tab 2 turns green.

Section 3 — Your Personalized Equation. A big card appears at the top with Patience's actual equation: y = −24x + 100. Below it, a graph draws itself — Patience's decay line going from (0, 100) down through the t-axis. Two markers light up on the line: a yellow dot at (3.33, 20) labeled "power-budget floor — math line flattens", and a red dot at (4.17, 0) labeled "x-intercept (math only)". A vertical band shaded across the graph marks the 3-minute match window. A yellow callout right under the chart catches Maya's eye: "Two different floors — don't mix them up. P(t) is your power budget, and the math line holds at 20%. But your bot's wheels also have a hard minimum speed set by your SPEED stat — and because that minimum is higher than 20% of your motor power, the wheels bottom out and the lights turn red before you hit 20%." Maya re-reads it. "So the 20% point is where the math line flattens — not the moment Patience first feels slow. The lights turn red earlier than that." Devon does the arithmetic out loud: "3-minute match. 3.33 is bigger than 3. So we DON'T hit the math floor during a match." Maya checks: at t = 3, P(3) = 100 − 24(3) = 100 − 72 = 28%. The graph confirms it — the curve at the right edge of the match window sits at 28%, four points above the yellow floor dot. They end the match at 28% on the power-budget line. The wheels probably went red somewhere before that, but the budget never flattens during the match. Their E = 4 allocation from Day 5 is exactly enough to clear a 3-minute match — no margin to spare, but enough.

Maya's worksheet — Section 2 both boxes, Section 3 verification 
SLOPE-INTERCEPT FORM
─────────────────────────────────────────────
P(t) = 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 × t           (original)
P(t) = −1.5(20 − E) × t + 100             (factored)
y    =        m         × x  +  b          (matched to y = mx + b)

For Patience (E = 4):
  m = −1.5 × (20 − 4) = −1.5 × 16 = −24
  b = 100
  → y = −24x + 100

BOX 1 — 20% POWER FLOOR TIME (P = 20)
─────────────────────────────────────────────
  20 = 100 − 24t
  24t = 80
  t = 80 / 24  ≈  3.33 min    ← power-budget floor (math line flattens)

BOX 2 — TRUE X-INTERCEPT (P = 0)
─────────────────────────────────────────────
  0 = 100 − 24t
  24t = 100
  t = 100 / 24  ≈  4.17 min   ← line crosses t-axis (math only)

CHECK — proportional relationship
─────────────────────────────────────────────
  t_xi / t_floor = 4.17 / 3.33 = 1.25
  (= 100/80, always. Doesn't depend on r.)

MATCH ANSWER — Does Patience hit the floor?
─────────────────────────────────────────────
  Match is 3 minutes. Floor is at 3.33 min.
  3.33 > 3 → NO floor hit.
  P(3) = 100 − 24(3) = 28%.
  Patience ends at 28%, 8 points above the floor.

Summarize. The teacher calls two teams: highest E in the room (Iron Ravens #25 , E = 8 → r = 18, floor at 80/18 ≈ 4.44 min, never hits floor in a 3-min match) and lowest E in the room (Steady Hawks #17, E = 2 → r = 27, floor at 80/27 ≈ 2.96 min, hits the floor with about 2 seconds left). The contrast is on the board. "What changed? What stayed the same?" A student notices: "The 1.25 ratio is the same for both." The teacher draws the line under it: t_xi is always 1.25 times t_floor — because 100/80 = 1.25 for any r. That ratio doesn't care what your E stat is.

Exit ticket. One problem, individual. "A bot has Endurance E = 4. Write the equation, find the floor time, find the x-intercept, and tell me if it hits the floor in a 3-minute match." Maya looks at the problem and almost laughs out loud — it's our bot. She writes everything she just figured out twenty minutes ago, drops the slip on the desk. Then in her journal she logs both critical times next to the slope-intercept equation:

Data Log — before today's first match 
EQUATION             y = −24x + 100
POWER-BUDGET FLOOR   3.33 min     (math line flattens at 20%)
TRUE X-INTERCEPT     4.17 min     (math only — bot never actually reaches)
3-MIN MATCH RESULT   P(3) = 28%   Math line never flattens. 8-point buffer.

Note on wheel speed (separate from the math line):
- minWheelSpeed (set by SPEED) bottoms out before P drops to 20%.
- The lights turn red EARLIER than the 3.33-min floor time.
- The 3.33 number is when the math line flattens, not when Patience
  first feels slow on the floor.

Notes for the match:
- Patience's endurance is exactly enough for 3-minute matches.
- If a match ever runs overtime, we hit the floor at 3:20.
- High-E teams (E ≥ 7) never hit the floor in standard matches.
- Low-E teams (E ≤ 3) hit the floor before the match ends.
She just spent twenty minutes finding two specific times on her bot's decay curve. Both ended up being numbers she can use in a real match this afternoon — not just answers to fill in a box. Her Day 5 stat allocation (E = 4) had been a guess. Today the math proved it was the smallest E value that still clears a 3-minute match. Not by much — 28% at match end is a thin buffer — but enough. The team got their endurance allocation exactly right by accident.

We are 12 lower by halftime. We have to score in the first minute or lose." Devon writes the prediction down word-for-word.

Pre-match prediction card — Patience vs. Iron Ravens #25 
OPPONENT      Iron Ravens #25
              S = 4, E = 8, T = 5, P = 3

OUR DECAY     y = −24x + 100
THEIR DECAY   y = −18x + 100

CROSSOVER     At t = 0 both 100%
              At t = 1   we 76, them 82
              At t = 2   we 52, them 64  ← 12-pt gap

MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Dashboard + Match Rules

Devices closed. The teacher waits until every screen is dark. Then she opens: "Before we do anything today, think back to the simulation rounds. You logged a gap — the difference between what the formula predicted and what your bot actually did. Turn and tell your partners one gap your team found. One sentence." Maya, Iris, and Devon huddle. Maya names it instantly — the Round 2 endurance gap from Day 5, the one where the kick formula didn't predict the motor's decay. Iris adds the Day 6 launch overshoot (+4 cm). Devon nods. The teacher takes two responses from the room, says each one back in her own words without judging, then frames the shift: "Those gaps were practice data. This week, the data gets real. Every match you play counts toward the leaderboard. Every result goes into the dashboard. And every Note you write — or skip — will matter when you decide whether to change your stat allocation in Season 2."

Devices open. Dashboard walkthrough. Two things look different from the Pre-Season setup Maya's been using since Day 5. (1) The View control in the top corner shows "Full dashboard" instead of "Pre-trial." (2) A new section is visible at the bottom of the page: Match Log. The teacher walks the whole class through a practice row together — every team adds it at the same time. Round = 1. Day = 7. Opponent = Practice. Source = Endurance formula. Predicted = 2. "Now click Add row." Every screen ticks. The row appears in the table, dimmed grey. "See the dim? That is Week 1. Practice. Starting today, your rows are clear — they count."

The Notes field. The teacher pulls the form back up. Two text boxes underneath the row fields — "What happened?" and "What are you testing next round?" "The Notes field is not optional," she says. "'We lost' is not a note. A note tells you something useful in Season 2. Here is the test: could you read this note in 7 days and know what to change? If not, rewrite it." Then she types an example on the projector: "Bot slowed near the 2-minute mark — Endurance = 4, possibly too low. Opponent's Turning kept them in the center zone." Maya doesn't have to be told this is what a useful note looks like — she's been writing Simulation Lab observations and dashboard residual notes since Day 5. For her, the Notes requirement is just the camp formalizing what she's been doing as Analyst all along. Iris and Devon read the example and nod. Maya writes the placeholder note for the practice row: "first walkthrough — does not count." Click Save. The top right corner shows Saved ✓. Load Team demo flashes by. Delete the practice row by clicking the X — "this cannot be undone, so be careful."

Match rules briefing. The teacher walks to the arena. The rules go up on the board exactly as written, no improvising:

Match rules — memorize before Round 1 
OBJECTIVE
  Push the ball across your opponent's goal line.
  The goal spans the middle section of each short end.

SCORING
  A goal counts the moment the ball fully crosses the
  line — even if it bounces back.
  Either goal line counts for that net's attacker —
  OWN GOALS COUNT.
  After each goal: ball returns to center, robots keep
  running, clock keeps running.

MATCH LENGTH
  3 minutes. Most goals wins. Tied at 3:00 = draw.

STARTING THE MATCH
  Press Button A once. No touching your robot during
  the match unless the referee says so.

BALL OUT OF BOUNDS
  Referee returns the ball to center.
  Clock keeps running.

ROBOT OFF THE FIELD
  Referee places the robot at the nearest edge, at
  the center line, facing inward. Referee presses
  Button B to try to reset matchLoop. Robot must
  recover autonomously. Clock keeps running.

ROBOT-TO-ROBOT CONTACT
  Part of the game. Your bots will collide. That is
  not a foul.

NO MID-MATCH CODE CHANGES
  The bot is programmed before the match. If your
  bot gets stuck, it stays stuck. That is your data.

The teacher reads each line aloud. Maya marks two on her notes: own goals count (she had not considered this — if Patience pushes the ball the wrong way after a hit, Cobalt Wolves get the point) and after a goal, robots keep running (she had assumed there would be a reset window — there isn't). She updates her prediction model in her head: own-goal risk goes up against teams whose Power can knock Patience off-line. Then the last sentence stays with her as the script writer intended: "if your bot gets stuck, it stays stuck. That is your data." Stuck isn't a failure. It's evidence to log.

Post-match protocol — four steps in order. The teacher writes them on the board:

Post-match protocol (memorize the order) 
STEP 1   Score goes into the dashboard. Immediately.
         No exceptions. Score first, everything else second.

STEP 2   Each team gives ONE data acknowledgment sentence.
         Not "good game." One sentence with a stat or a
         number. Winning team goes first.
         Example: "Their Turning kept them in the center zone
                   — we couldn't push through."

STEP 3   30-second dispute window. Any scoring concern raised
         now. No evidence, no dispute. After 30 seconds the
         result locks.

STEP 4   Reset. Both teams return bots to their starting
         positions.

Ready Check. The teacher asks, "What's the difference between a Pre-Trial row and a Full row in the dashboard?" Iron Ravens #25 answers: "Pre-trial rows are dimmed. They don't count toward the leaderboard. Full rows are live." The teacher says it back in her own words to confirm. "A match ends. Your bot got stuck at the 90-second mark. What do you write in the Notes field?" Magnetic Raptors #22 answers: "Bot stuck at 90 sec. Power was around 64% at that time. Probably a turning issue — check Turn Ratio next." Teacher presses: "Why that specific detail rather than just 'bot got stuck'? What decision does that help you make?" Devon answers from Team Patience's table without being called on: "Because. In. Season 2. You. Can't. Change. Anything. If you don't know what to change." The teacher writes Devon's sentence on the board. "That's the move engineers make when they debug a prototype. It's really useful."

Closing consolidation: "The dashboard is live now — every match, every note, every row. The rules are fixed — 3-minute match, no touching your bot mid-match, 30-second dispute window, post-match data first. Your job is not just to play well. It is to capture what happened so you can make better decisions in Season 2. The teams that improve across the tournament are the ones with the best notes. Today we start collecting that evidence." Then: "First match in five minutes."

Maya has been keeping Analyst-style notes since Day 5 — every Simulation Lab row, every dashboard observation, every residual. What the teacher just walked the whole class through, Maya was already doing. The difference now is that it counts toward the leaderboard, and the Notes she writes today are the data Iris will use to argue for or against a Season 2 stat change. Three days ago her notes were practice. By the end of today they will be evidence. Devon's voice carrying his unprompted answer across the room — "you can't change anything if you don't know what to change" — sounded like the kind of thing a Coder says, not a Fabricator. The roles are bleeding into each other in a useful way. The team is becoming a team.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 1 Round-Robin Matches, Day 1

Three rounds across 90 minutes. Patience runs the Velocity Arena game code Iris flashed last night, with the locked stat values from the Day 6 Prove Your Build commit. Maya (Analyst) sits at her device with the dashboard open. Devon (Fabricator) resets Patience between matches. Iris (Coder) watches the bot run with her hands off the keyboard — no mid-match code changes, no between-match changes either; the code is locked for all of Season 1. What Iris CAN do is observe Patience closely so that when Maya reads off the gaps after each match, Iris has her own visual data to compare against. The teacher walks the match cycle out loud before Round 1: "Before the match: open your match data log and read your prediction number out loud. Your opponent hears it. Then both bots go on the field. After the match: record your goals and win or loss immediately. Not later. Right then. If you lost, I will come to you before the next round starts." Each match is three minutes.

Round 1. Iron Ravens #25. Pre-match: Maya reads Patience's prediction off her device — "Two goals. Win if we score by sixty seconds; else it comes down to endurance and we drop." Iron Ravens reads theirs — "One for us, two for them." Both numbers on paper before either bot moves. Three minutes. Patience scores at 0:52 and again at 2:14. 2 − 1. Maya enters Goals For = 2, Goals Against = 1, W/L = W before anything else. Then the post-match data acknowledgment — Patience goes first because they won: "We scored before our own decay caught us. Our slope is steeper than theirs — we needed to score early before they outlasted us, and we did." Iron Ravens' Analyst: "You predicted you'd score by 60s and you did at 52s. Our Endurance was higher than yours (E = 8 to your 4), but you didn't give us time to use it." Thirty-second dispute window passes — no claims. Maya's Notes: "Iron Ravens E = 8 → slope −18 (vs our slope −24). Higher endurance, but lower Speed. They couldn't catch us once we were up 1−0. Prediction at 60s held; gap on our goals = 0."

Round 2. Kinetic Falcons #8. Maya: "One goal each. Even decay slopes — comes down to who finds the ball first." Falcons: "Two for us, none for them. Our Power is 7." Three minutes. Falcons reaches the ball first at 1:08 and pushes at chargePower 49 — both wheels driving the ball forward at 49% of max. The ball rolls clean over Patience's goal line. After the reset, both bots run back toward center. Patience reaches the ball some of the time but at chargePower 25 her push can't drive the ball past Falcons' bot. Falcons re-acquires and scores again at 2:34. Patience never gets a clean push toward Falcons' goal. 0 − 2. Maya enters it: GF = 0, GA = 2, W/L = L. Falcons' acknowledgment: "Our Power was higher than yours. We predicted that would decide it. It did." Iris does Patience's, quieter: "Their Power was 7 to our 5. We expected even decay would matter more than the chargePower gap. It didn't."

Loss recovery — between Round 2 and Round 3. The teacher walks to Patience's table. Step 1, verbatim: "That result was frustrating. That's a real feeling and it makes sense." She waits thirty seconds. Devon stares at the table. Maya looks up. Step 2: "Your model predicted 1 goal for you, 1 for them. Actual was 0 for you, 2 for them. Open the dashboard. One stat only. Which stat explains why you scored zero?" Maya thinks for a few seconds. "Their Power. They got the ball both times when the bots hit each other. We never got it back." Step 3: "What's one thing you'd change about your PREDICTION — not your code — for Round 3?" Maya hesitates. "If they have way more Power than us… predict we lose, I guess? Even if our Endurance is good?" The teacher: "Say that with more conviction. That's a rule. Write it down." Maya writes in her data log: "When opponent Power is much bigger than ours, predict we lose. Endurance doesn't fix Power." Step 4: "Walk to one other team. Tell them: 'We're changing _____ because _____.' One sentence." Maya walks to Iron Ravens #25. "We're changing our prediction. When the other team's Power is way higher, we predict we lose. Because Falcons got the ball both times and we never got it back." Iron Ravens nods. Maya comes back to her seat. The rule she just wrote uses the words "way more" and "much bigger" — not a specific number. That's part of why she'll get Round 3 wrong: she didn't write down what "way more" meant.

Round 3. Cobalt Wolves #14. Maya looks at the stat card: their Power = 9. Patience's Power = 5. "They have way more Power than us," she says. "That's the rule. We lose." But she also wants to score. She looks at Patience's Endurance — 4 — and Cobalt's — 5. "Our Endurance is almost the same as theirs, though. Maybe we still get goals." Devon, quieter than usual: "The rule said the Endurance thing doesn't fix Power." Maya: "I know. But like — what if we score one and they score one? Tie?" She writes "2 − 2 tie" in the prediction column. Cobalt: "Two for us. Once we win first contact, chargePower 81 keeps sending the ball back toward your goal." Three minutes. Both bots sweep toward center. Cobalt's launch is quick, but Patience's bot is in the path between Cobalt and the goal — twice in the first minute, Patience's body blocks Cobalt's push. Then Patience tries to attack and Cobalt's chargePower 81 push knocks her off-angle every time she aligns. Neither bot gets a clean push to a goal line. The clock hits 3:00 with the ball still loose at center field. 0 − 0 draw. Maya enters it: GF = 0, GA = 0, W/L = D.

Maya looks at the dashboard. Predicted 2 − 2. Actual 0 − 0. Gap of 4 goals total. The biggest miss of her day. The rule she wrote 20 minutes ago said predict a loss. She predicted a tie. "I should have just listened to my own rule," she mutters. Devon doesn't say anything. Iris doesn't either. The teacher doesn't come — Patience didn't lose. Maya writes in her data log on her own: "Rule said predict a loss. I didn't. Next time: when the rule says we lose, predict we lose." Then she adds a smaller note underneath: "Maybe at really big Power gaps neither bot scores at all? Watch for this tomorrow." She's not sure if that's a new rule or if she's just trying to make herself feel better about being wrong.

Leaderboard reveal. The teacher posts the leaderboard on the wall — three columns: Wins · Goals Scored · Best Prediction. She names the Best Prediction column first, before pointing at Wins. Maya looks at her own data log — total gap of 3 — and doesn't expect to be called. She isn't. "Steady Hawks #17 — total gap of one goal across three rounds. Tell us about Round 3." The Hawks Analyst stands up. Her explanation is shorter than Maya expected. She just says "we had the same rule everyone else had after Round 2 — when their Power is higher than ours, predict we lose. We did. We predicted 0-2. We lost 0-1." She sits back down. Maya stares at her own journal. The Hawks Analyst did exactly what the teacher told them to do. Maya didn't. Then the teacher names the practice: "Looking at the gap between what you predicted and what happened — that's called residual analysis. You'll do it formally in Season 2. You all just did it for real today." Maya writes residual analysis in her journal with quotes around it. Not because she did it. Because next time she's going to.

Exit ticket. One sentence in the data log, no calculator. Prompt on the board: "My prediction across today's matches averaged ___ goals. My actual average was ___ goals. The gap was ___. I think this happened because ___." Maya writes: "Predicted 5/3 = 1.67 goals. Actual 2/3 ≈ 0.67. Gap = 1 per match. I think this happened because I had the rule from Round 2 and I didn't follow it in Round 3. I predicted a tie when the rule said predict a loss." She drops the log on the table. The teacher's closing line: "Today your math met reality. The gap between predicted and actual — that's a residual. Starting Day 8, every prediction needs a formula, and every formula will be tested against what actually happens."

Patience: 1 win, 1 draw, 1 loss. Maya finishes fourth on Best Prediction. Steady Hawks wins. Maya doesn't get called up. She walks to her bag at the end of the day and her stomach is tight. Not because of the losses. Because she had the rule in her own handwriting — Power gap of 2+ means we lose — and predicted a tie instead. Three days ago she would not have cared. Math was for other kids. Now she does care. That's the worst part.

ENDS

Name One Math Concept — Write the Calculation, Translate to Behavior

Same prompt as Day 2. Today the concept Maya picks is different.

Maya's ENDS reflection

Concept: Slope-intercept form, applied to predict a match outcome.

CALCULATION       y = −24x + 100   (us, E = 4)
                  y = −18x + 100   (Iron Ravens, E = 8)

                  Their slope is shallower — they hold power longer.
                  Our Speed is higher. So we have to score EARLY
                  before their endurance advantage takes over.

                  At t = 0.87 (52 s):  us 79%, them 84%.  Gap = 5 pts.
                  At t = 2 min:        us 52%, them 64%.  Gap = 12 pts.

                  Window: score before t = 1 min, while we can still
                  outrun them. Past 1 min, the gap grows fast and
                  their power advantage decides it.

OUTCOME           Scored at 0:52. Won 2 − 1.

BEHAVIOR          The slope of a line told us when to make our move.
                  Not 'go fast.' Not 'try harder.' The number told us
                  WHEN. We picked the timing from the algebra, and
                  the algebra picked the win.
DAY 08

Scavenger Hunt · Matching · Match Day 2 · The Rivalry Card

Week 2 · Season 1 Match Day 2
Maya's arc today: Runs the leaderboard chase scavenger hunt — Devon catches her PEMDAS slip from his seat before the pile would. Matching Situations: Iris catches her scatter-point misread. Maya writes the class linear equation from real data. Match Day 2 afternoon: equation overestimates Patience by 3 in Round 1; loss recovery walks her through the gap; her revised Round 2 prediction matches exactly. Posts a Meets-level rivalry card. Open Lab: stuck on "what would a Patience-specific equation look like?" — files the question for Season 2.
Where Maya is nowDay 7 left her with the knot — she had a rule and didn't trust it. Today she watches Devon catch her Scavenger Hunt PEMDAS slip and Iris catch her scatter-plot misread. She is sloppier on inputs than she'd like to be, and it costs her. But the afternoon's bias-correction insight is real, and her revised Round 2 prediction matches exactly. The arc is no longer "wishes she did math." It is "does math imperfectly and notices when she gets it wrong."
HOOK

Leaderboard Chase — Scavenger Hunt

The teacher built the chain last night with the Leaderboard Chase Builder — eight cards posted around the room, each one showing a real team's actual stat allocation from yesterday's leaderboard. On the back of every card is one of the six Velocity Arena formulas to compute. The answer cards sit face-down at the central table, sorted lowest to highest by value. Individual work. No calculators — per the policy poster, this is a PEMDAS-practice activity, and the pile catches the order-of-operations error. A calculator would hide the error and the activity would lose its teaching purpose. The teacher gestures at the policy poster without saying anything as students pull out their pencils. The hard rule, posted on the board: "If your answer is not in the pile, your calculation is wrong. Go back and recalculate. Don't ask me whether your answer is right — the pile tells you."

Maya starts at Card 2 — Kinetic Falcons #8, Power = 7. The card on the back asks: "What is Kinetic Falcons' charge speed?" Formula: P². 7 × 7 = 49%. She walks to the central table, finds 49 in the pile, flips the back. "Go to Card 3 — Cobalt Wolves #14 by the trash can." Maya crosses the room. Card 3: Cobalt Wolves, Endurance = 5. "What is Cobalt Wolves' power output at minute 3?" Formula: 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 x 3. She computes: (20 − 5) × 4.5 = 67.5; 100 − 67.5 = 32.5%. Pile. Card 4 is Magnetic Raptors #22 with three stats listed — S = 4, P = 4, T = 6 — and asks "how many points did Magnetic Raptors put into Endurance?" Maya: 20 − 4 − 4 − 6 = 6. Pile. Card 5 is Patient Foxes #23 — that's us, S = 6 — "What is Patience's motor power percentage?" 6 × 5 = 30%. Pile. Card 6 is Steady Hawks #17, T = 6 — Turn Ratio: 0.2 + (6 × 0.02) = 0.32. Pile. Five cards in eight minutes.

Card 7 — Iron Ravens #25, Power = 3. "What is Iron Ravens' follow-through time in milliseconds?" Formula: 120 + P × 35. Maya rushes the calculation in her head: (120 + 3) × 35 = 4305 ms. She mutters it under her breath as she walks toward the central table. Devon is at his own seat working Card 4 — he doesn't look up, but he hears her say "forty-three hundred" and says, without looking up, "Multiply first."

Maya stops mid-step. Re-reads her own work on the index card. Oh. The formula is 120 + (P × 35) — implicit parens around P × 35, multiply before add. Not (120 + P) × 35. She glances at the PEMDAS poster on the wall — it's been there since Day 1, the same poster every middle-school math room has — to confirm what Devon's two-word correction was pointing at. The pile would have caught it. Devon caught it first. She erases 4305 and rewrites: 3 × 35 = 105. 120 + 105 = 225 ms. Walks to the table — 225 is in the pile. The back tells her: "Go to Card 8 — Iron Ravens #25 near the door." Just before she gets there, the teacher calls time. Six of eight cards completed. One of those six only got finished because Devon was sitting close enough to hear her mutter the wrong answer.

Maya's answer sheet — six of eight cards completed in 13 minutes
CardTeam · statFormulaMaya's workIn pile?
2Kinetic Falcons #8 · P = 7P² (Charge Speed)7 × 7 = 49%✓ first try
3Cobalt Wolves #14 · E = 5100 − (20 − E) × 4.5 (Power at t = 3)(20 − 5) × 4.5 = 67.5 · 100 − 67.5 = 32.5%✓ first try
4Magnetic Raptors #22 · S = 4, P = 4, T = 6E = 20 − S − P − T (Find Endurance)20 − 4 − 4 − 6 = 6✓ first try
5Patient Foxes #23 · S = 6 (us)S × 5 (Motor Power)6 × 5 = 30%✓ first try
6Steady Hawks #17 · T = 60.2 + T × 0.02 (Turn Ratio)0.2 + (6 × 0.02) = 0.32✓ first try
7Iron Ravens #25 · P = 3120 + P × 35 (Follow-Through)1st: (120+3)×35 = 4305 ✗ — caught by Devon overhearing
2nd: 120 + (3×35) = 225 ms ✓		second try

Six cards (2–7) in thirteen minutes. One PEMDAS slip on Card 7 — Devon caught it from the next seat before the pile would have. No teacher consulted. Did not reach Card 8 (Decay Rate) or Card 1 (Speed % of Budget) before time was called.

Wrap. The teacher pulls her monitoring sheet. The most common error she logged across the room was on Card 7 — students writing (120 + P) × 35 instead of 120 + (P × 35). She names it without naming anyone: "Follow-Through. The most common move was distributing the multiplication across both terms. The formula is 120 + (P × 35) — multiply Power by 35 first, then add 120. For P = 3: 3 × 35 = 105, plus 120 = 225 ms. The implicit parentheses around P × 35 bind tighter than the addition." Maya doesn't look up. She nods at her answer sheet. Devon glances at her sideways and doesn't say anything.

Exit ticket. Ninety seconds on the back of her answer sheet, no calculator allowed — exit tickets are calculator-free across all activities. "Which formula gave you the most trouble today? Write it out. Then write the step where you made an error and why that step is easy to miss." Maya writes: "Follow-Through. 120 + P × 35. I rushed and wrote (120 + 3) × 35 instead of 120 + (3 × 35). I didn't catch it. Devon did, from his table. The step that's easy to miss is the implicit parentheses around P × 35 — you have to multiply before you add, even when there are no visible parens. I'll watch for that next time."

Devon caught the mistake. Maya didn't. Three weeks ago Maya would have been embarrassed. Today she's relieved and a little annoyed at herself. She wrote it on the exit ticket because she didn't want to pretend she figured it out alone. "I didn't catch it. Devon did." The Analyst missed it. The Fabricator caught it. She's going to think about that on the bus.
CHALLENGE

Matching Situations, Graphs, and Linear Equations

Scatter plot build. Worksheet on every desk. The wall has a blank coordinate grid — Speed 0–8 on the x-axis, Goals 0–20 on the y-axis. The teacher hands each team a pre-labeled dot sticker with the team name written on it. Maya checks Patience's stat card: Speed = 6. She opens her Day 7 data log and sums their goals across the three matches: 2 + 0 + 0 = 2 goals. Patience's data point: (6, 2). Devon takes the sticker up to the wall grid and places it. Maya stays at the table and copies every other team's sticker coordinates onto the scatter plot on her worksheet as they go up. Iron Ravens lands at (4, 3). Cobalt Wolves at (3, 6). Kinetic Falcons at (5, 7). Steady Hawks at (5, 5). Magnetic Raptors at (4, 0). Maya writes Magnetic Raptors as (4, 1) because the dot looks like it's between 0 and 2 from her angle. Iris glances at her sheet a minute later: "Raptors went zero-zero-zero on Day 7. Their dot is on the x-axis." Maya re-reads the dot from the wall — Iris is right. She crosses out the 1 and writes 0. "Thanks." Six dots, copied correctly the second time.

Step 3 — prediction before any math. "Before we do any math — if Speed doubles from 4 to 8, what do you think happens to goals? Circle one." Maya circles "increase but NOT double" without hesitating — she has been living the answer for forty-eight hours. Her Step 3 explanation: "Speed doubling doesn't double goals. Speed only sets how much of the field you can cover. The number of goals also depends on Power (how hard our kicks send the ball when we make contact), Endurance (whether we last through the match), and strategy. We had Speed = 6 yesterday and still scored zero against Cobalt because their Power was almost four times ours."

Matching cards — V/T/G. Three card types. V (verbal data point), T (data table), G (graph). Five sets to match — one V, one T, and one G describe the same linear relationship. Maya works with Iris and Devon. The trap the script warns about surfaces quickly: T-C and T-A both have slope = 0.5. Devon thinks they're a pair. Maya catches the difference: "T-C starts at Speed = 2, Goals = 1 — extend the line back and it passes through the origin. T-A starts at Speed = 0, Goals = 1 — y-intercept = 1. Same slope, different starting value. T-C goes with G-E (the line through the origin). T-A goes with G-B (the line that hits the y-axis at 1)." Iris re-reads it once, nods, marks the matching record. Five sets in eleven minutes. "Goals = 0.5 × Speed" (V1·T-C·G-E) vs. "Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1" (V2·T-A·G-B) — the y-intercept is what distinguishes them.

Finger Speed-Sums. Six minutes of movement before the cognitive load goes back up. Both partners hold up 0–5 fingers simultaneously; first to call the correct sum wins the round. Rotate every minute. Maya wins three rounds against three different partners. By the time she sits back down, the math part of her brain has rebooted.

Equation building — Steps 4 through 9. Maya picks two points from the class scatter plot. She does NOT pick Patience's own point — at (6, 2) Patience is below the trend, and she wants to write the equation that describes the cloud of data, not their specific outlier. She picks (4, 3) (Iron Ravens) and (8, 5) (Kinetic Falcons). Step 5 — slope: m = (5 − 3) ÷ (8 − 4) = 2/4 = 0.5. Step 6 — solve for b: 3 = 0.5 × 4 + b → b = 1. Her equation: Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1. Step 7 — interpret: "When Speed increases by 1, goals increase by 0.5. If Speed = 0, this model predicts 1 goal." She circles "not realistic" on b — a bot with Speed = 0 literally can't move to the ball, so 1 goal at Speed = 0 isn't a physical claim. It's a math artifact of where the line happens to cross the y-axis. The model lives within the domain of the data we have (Speed ≈ 3–8). Outside that range, it breaks down. Step 8 — test it on the doubling question: 0.5 × 4 + 1 = 3; 0.5 × 8 + 1 = 5. 3 to 5 is not doubling; it's a 67% increase. Her Step 3 prediction confirmed by her own equation.

Maya's worksheet — Steps 4–9 + Reflect 
STEP 4 — Two points: (4, 3) Iron Ravens · (8, 5) Kinetic Falcons
STEP 5 — m = (5 − 3) ÷ (8 − 4) = 2 ÷ 4 = 0.5
STEP 6 — b = 3 − 0.5(4) = 1
         EQUATION:   Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1

STEP 7 — Per +1 Speed, goals +0.5.
         At Speed = 0, model predicts 1 goal.    b: NOT realistic ✗
         A bot with Speed = 0 literally can't move to the ball.
         The model lives in Speed ≈ 3–8 (where our data lives).
         Outside that range, it breaks down. The y-intercept is
         a math artifact of WHERE the line crosses the y-axis,
         not a real-world prediction.

STEP 8 — Speed = 4 → 3 goals.   Speed = 8 → 5 goals.
         Doubling Speed: NO. 3 → 5 is +67%, not ×2.

STEP 9 — "Speed doesn't fully control goals because the line
          doesn't go through zero — the y-intercept is 1, and a
          bot with Speed = 0 can't even move, so the 1 isn't a
          real prediction, it just tells me other stuff matters
          too (Power, Endurance, strategy). I think the equation
          only works for Speeds we actually have data for
          (about 3 to 8). Outside that I'm guessing."

PATIENCE CHECK — at Speed = 6, model predicts 0.5(6) + 1 = 4 goals.
         Patience actual: 2 goals. Residual = −2.
         We are BELOW the trend. Why? Round 3 0–0 draw vs Cobalt
         (Power gap of 4). Speed couldn't compensate.

REFLECT Q1 — Two teams got different equations. Both correct? YES.
         Both equations come from real data. They differ because
         teams picked different point pairs from the same scatter
         plot. There is no single "right" equation — only better
         or worse fits. I picked two points spread far apart
         (Speed = 4 and Speed = 8 — an x-gap of 4), so my slope
         is more reliable than Magnetic Raptors', who picked points
         closer together. Both equations describe the same cloud
         of data; mine is just sampled with a wider x-gap.

REFLECT Q2 — What matters MORE than Speed? POWER.
         Patience has Speed = 6 (above class average) but scored
         only 2 goals because Cobalt's Power = 9 prevented clean
         shots in Round 3. Speed determines field coverage;
         Power determines how hard your kicks send the ball when
         you make contact — and whether your kick reaches the
         goal area. The non-zero y-intercept is exactly where
         Power, Endurance, and strategy LIVE in the equation.

Summarize. The teacher calls Maya up first — the clear-case team. Maya walks to the board and writes Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1 in her own handwriting. Then the teacher calls Magnetic Raptors #22, who picked different points and got Goals = 0.75 × Speed − 1. Two equations side by side. Different slopes. Different y-intercepts. Same scatter plot. "Can two different equations both correctly describe the same data? Talk to your partner." Maya to Devon, quietly: "Yes. Different points → different sample → different line. Neither pair is 'the' right pair. They're both valid samples of the cloud." The teacher draws that exact answer out of the room across two minutes of talk moves. Then she names the practice: "What you just did — taking two data points, calculating a rate of change, and using it to describe the behavior of the entire dataset — is called building a linear model. It's how scientists, economists, and coaches make predictions from real data."

Maya gets called up first. Two days ago she would have hated it. Today she walks to the board, writes Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1 in her own handwriting, and sits back down. By the time her chair stops scraping she's already started Reflect Q2. The teacher says what Maya did is called building a linear model. Maya recognizes the phrase. She'd clicked on a Khan Academy video by accident over the summer because her cousin had left a tab open at home. She knew the words before she knew what they meant. Now she knows both.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 1 Match Day 2 — Two Rounds

Two rounds across 70 minutes. The cycle is familiar from Day 7 — prediction before, results after, loss recovery if you lose. One thing changed: the prediction source. Today Maya uses the scatter plot equation from this morning's Matching Situations activity: Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1. Patience's Speed = 6 plugs in directly: 0.5(6) + 1 = 4. Maya does the arithmetic in her head and writes 4 in the prediction column of the data log. At the next table over, a student on Magnetic Raptors writes the equation by hand, then taps the calculator on her stat-card sleeve to compute 0.5(4) + 1 = 3. The teacher walks past, glances at the calculator, doesn't say anything — match predictions are the calculator-OK column on the policy poster. The Raptors student writes 3, no shame in it. The bot is still locked from Day 6. Iris's hands are still off the keyboard.

Round 1. Steady Hawks #17 (2 − 1 − 0 from Day 7, strong with high Power). Pre-match: Maya reads Patience's equation prediction off the data log — "Equation says four. Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1, Speed = 6, prediction = 4." Steady Hawks' Analyst reads theirs — "Three. Our equation gives 0.6 × 5 + 0.5 = 3.5, we rounded down." Three minutes. Steady Hawks reaches the ball first at 1:08 and pushes at her high chargePower. The ball drives clean over Patience's goal line. Hawks gets there again at 2:15 — pushes, scores. Patience scores once at 2:48, just before the 3-minute mark. Final: 1 − 3. Maya enters it: GF = 1, GA = 3, W/L = L. Equation predicted 4, actual was 1. Gap = 3 goals. Biggest residual she's recorded in two days.

90-second slope reflection — all class, before loss recovery. Before the teacher walks anywhere, she calls a quick reflection across the room: "Look at your Round 1 gap. Was your equation too high or too low? Talk to your partner — what does that tell you about your slope or your y-intercept?" Maya turns to Devon. "Equation gave us 4. We scored 1. Gap of 3 — equation was high." Devon, fast: "So either the slope is too steep, or the y-intercept is too high — or both. Same effect on the prediction." Maya: "Could be either. Can't tell from one match. But the direction is clear — we overestimate Patience. Same as the scatter plot residual this morning." Sixty seconds, then the teacher calls time. Maya's diagnosis is already in motion before the loss recovery starts.

Loss recovery — between Round 1 and Round 2 (Day 8 version). The teacher walks straight to Patience's table. She says, "That result was frustrating. That's a real feeling and it makes sense." Then, "Your equation predicted 4 goals. You scored 1. Open your equation. What Speed number did you put in? What Speed number did you actually run with in the match?" Maya: "Six in both. Speed input matches the bot. The equation just overestimated Patience — same way the class equation overestimated us on the scatter plot at (6, 2). The class equation doesn't see the Power gap; it only sees Speed." Teacher: "Your equation is as accurate as your data. You now have one more data point. Save the slope change for Open Lab — two data points aren't enough to revise a slope. What is one number you would change in your PREDICTION for Round 2?" Maya writes: "Round 2 prediction: 2 goals. The class equation overestimates Patience by ~2. Until I have enough data to revise the equation in Open Lab, I subtract 2 from the equation output to get a Patience-specific prediction. I'm not changing the equation. I'm correcting for what I know." Maya walks to Iron Ravens #25 — the same team she walked to in Day 7's loss recovery, callback by accident. "We're changing our Round 2 prediction from 4 to 2 because the class equation overestimates Patience by 2 goals — same direction as our slope reflection 90 seconds ago, same direction as Day 7's residuals against high-Power opponents."

Round 2. Magnetic Raptors #22 (mid-tier, Power = 4 → chargePower 16). Pre-match: Maya reads her revised prediction aloud, not the raw equation output: "We're revising. Equation gives four, but Patience-specific prediction is two." Teacher acknowledges it publicly to the room: "Patience revised their prediction based on Round 1 data. That is exactly how a model improves. Watch whether it works." Raptors: "Three for us." Three minutes. Both bots sweep toward center. When Patience pushes the ball, chargePower 25 drives it further than Raptors' chargePower 16 — Patience's pushes reach the goal area; Raptors' tend to fall short. Patience reaches the ball first at 0:52 and scores. Raptors finds the ball at 1:24, pushes toward Patience's goal, scores once. Patience reaches the ball first again at 2:38, scores. Final: 2 − 1. Patience wins. Maya enters it: GF = 2, GA = 1, W/L = W. Revised prediction was 2. Actual = 2. Gap = 0. Her revision was exact.

Team Patience — Day 8 dashboard, two rows + revision audit
ROpponentEquation predictionMaya's predictionActualGap (Maya's)
1Steady Hawks #174 (raw equation)4 (used raw)1 − 3 loss3
2Magnetic Raptors #224 (raw equation)2 (revised after R1)2 − 1 win0

Day 8 record: 1 win — 1 loss. Combined Season 1: 2 wins — 1 draw — 2 losses. Total gap on Maya's predictions today: 3 goals. Same metric using the raw equation both rounds would have been 5 goals. The revision cut today's gap by 40%.

Maya's loss-recovery hypothesis in Notes (Round 1 → Round 2 update):

"R1: equation predicted 4, actual 1, gap 3. The class equation fits the cloud of all teams' data — but Patience sits below the cloud at (6, 2). Same below-trend pattern as Day 7. Rule for Round 2 prediction (NOT for the equation): subtract 2 from raw equation output to correct for the Patience-specific Power gap. I'm not changing the slope. I'm correcting for known bias. Slope revision waits for Open Lab when I have at least 5 data points to fit a Patience-only line."

Leaderboard reveal. The teacher pulls her prediction-accuracy chart. Best Prediction today goes to Steady Hawks #17 — total gap of 1 across both rounds, equation was already well-fit. But the teacher does something specific before naming Hawks. She points at Patience: "Patience's equation overestimated their R1 result by 3 goals. They revised their Round 2 prediction based on that gap and predicted 2. They scored exactly 2. Their revision cut their gap from 3 to 0. That is how a model improves — not by throwing out the equation, but by correcting for known bias while you gather more data." Then she names Steady Hawks as Best Prediction. Maya is not Best Prediction today. She is not even in the top three. But she got named for the move that mattered to her. Then the teacher names what they did, plain: "What Patience did — using one real result to make a better next prediction — is how models get more accurate. Not by throwing out the equation. By checking it against what actually happens, and adjusting."

Exit ticket — sixty seconds, written in the journal. Prompt on the board: "My scatter plot equation predicted ___ goals today. My actual average was ___. My equation was (too high / too low / accurate). I think this happened because ___." Maya writes: "Equation predicted 4 goals per match. Actual average: (1 + 2) / 2 = 1.5 goals per match. Equation was: too high. I think this happened because the class equation fits a cloud of all teams' data — Patience sits below the cloud's trend line because Power = 5 isn't enough to convert Speed-6 opportunities into goals against high-Power opponents. The Speed-only equation can't see the Power gap. The fix is more data points, not a different equation." Drops the data log on the table. Closing line from the teacher, then immediate transition to Rivalry Cards: "Leaderboard is up. Now you tell each other what you think about it."

Two days ago Maya wouldn't have known revising the prediction and revising the equation were two different things. Today she does. The teacher said so out loud with Maya's name attached. Her revised prediction was exact: 2 and 2. Steady Hawks got Best Prediction with a tighter equation to start with — but the teacher pointed at Patience when she named the move. Patience is 2−1−2 in Season 1. Across both days they're still in the top three on Best Prediction. The matches are messy. The math is sharper than the matches.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Rivalry Cards

The one rule, on the board. Index card on every team's table. Markers staged. The teacher writes one sentence — that is the entire launch script — and then steps back: "Your card must cite a specific number from the dashboard. Not 'you're slow.' Specific: 'Your Speed = 4 means your bot runs at 20% motor power.' If I cannot find that number on the dashboard, the card comes back to you." Twenty minutes. One card. One rival. Pick your rival, write their team name at the top, go.

Data lookup — six minutes, hands off the marker. Maya picks Iron Ravens #25 — the team Patience beat in Round 1 yesterday, and the team whose stat profile she's been carrying around in her head since the scavenger hunt. She does NOT write yet. She opens the dashboard to Iron Ravens' row first and copies the numbers onto her draft: Power = 3, Endurance = 8. The teacher walks past and nods — dashboard is open, two numbers on paper before any words. Strong foundation. She moves on. Iris pulls up Patience's own row: Power = 5, Endurance = 4. Devon: "So they have endurance but we hit harder." Maya already knew. She's been the analyst on this rivalry for two days.

Write the card — five minutes. Maya holds the marker. Two sentences. State the stat. State what it means in the match. Make a prediction match results can prove right or wrong. She drafts on a scrap first. Two stats. Two formulas. One non-obvious insight — the kind of move the Exceeds exemplar pulls. She writes the final card slow enough for Iris and Devon to follow, then signs — Patient Foxes #23 at the bottom.

Patience's Rivalry Card — Day 8, ready to post
To: Iron Ravens #25

"Your Power = 3 means your chargePower is 9 (P² = 3 × 3). Patience's Power = 5 → chargePower = 25. We hit the ball almost 3× harder per kick. When the match ends, the scoreboard will show how many extra clean shots that bought us."

— Patient Foxes #23

Card audit (Meets level): One dashboard number (P = 3). One correct formula application (P² for chargePower). One verifiable claim (more clean shots). Two-sentence card, readable from a standing distance. Maya drafted a longer version with a second formula on Endurance decay, but Iris read it and said "too much. Pick one." Maya cut the Endurance paragraph. The card is shorter and stronger.

Facilitator review pass. The teacher walks the room. She picks Patience's card up, reads it once, looks for the three checkpoints: (1) specific dashboard number — P = 3, there. (2) Correct formula application — P² = 9, correct. (3) Verifiable claim — more clean shots, match-checkable. She hands the card back without saying anything except: "Post it." Patience moves to the wall.

Read aloud + post + practice naming. Maya reads it aloud — flat tone, single breath, doesn't milk the closing line. She tapes the card to the wall next to Iron Ravens' team area. The teacher names the practice for the whole room — using a different team's longer two-formula card as the example, not Maya's: "What [other team] just did — starting from two stat values, applying formulas to both, and making a prediction tomorrow's verification round can prove right or wrong — is mathematical modeling. A prediction with a number behind it is not trash talk. It is a claim." Maya listens and notes the difference: the other team's card uses two formulas. Mine uses one. That's the difference between Meets and Exceeds.

Three days ago Maya could not have written two sentences with that much math in them. Today she wrote them, signed her name at the bottom, and let Iris cut a third sentence. The card she posted is shorter than the one she drafted. She watches another team's longer card get named as the practice example. Two formulas instead of one. Next time, Maya thinks, that's the difference. She doesn't feel embarrassed. Two weeks ago she would not have put her name on a math claim in front of a class. Now hers is on a wall for the rest of camp.
OPEN LAB

Day 8 Open Lab

Scatter plot review. The teacher directs every team back to the morning's linear equation and asks them to compare it against today's match results. "Your linear equation predicted X. Today you scored Y. Does today's result support that model, or contradict it?" Patience pulls up their equation — Goals = 0.5 × Speed + 1 — and lays today's data next to it. R1: equation predicted 4, actual was 1, gap of 3. R2: equation predicted 4, actual was 2, gap of 2. Maya, Iris, and Devon work the question together. Maya says it first: "It holds for the cloud. The class equation describes the average team well. For Patience, it consistently overestimates." Iris adds: "And the gap matches our scatter plot residual from this morning — we were at (6, 2) on the cloud, two below the line. Today we're scoring 1 and 2 against an equation that predicts 4. The line hasn't moved; we've always been below it." Devon: "So the model holds — for the cloud. It doesn't hold for us. Same thing all day."

The teacher circulates with one question per team. When she reaches Patience, Maya gives the answer ready: "Equation supports the model as a class average. Contradicts it for Patience — we're below the prediction every match. Same direction as our scatter plot residual." She trails off — she's not sure how to finish the thought. The teacher doesn't push. "That's the right instinct. Hold onto it for Day 11 — that's when you can change the bot's Speed and get a new data point." Maya nods. The teacher moves to the next table. Maya turns to Iris: "What would 'at different Speeds' even mean if our bot is locked?" Iris shrugs: "Day 11 reallocation. Then we test." Maya writes "Day 11 — change Speed, take a new data point, see if the equation gets sharper" in the margin of her data log. The question is filed. It has a date attached.

Iris double-checks each one against the Formula Reference Card on the wall. Maya writes the example calculations in the corner of each card — one real team's stat values plugged in, with the worked arithmetic next to it. They stack the cards in formula-type order and clip them with a rubber band. Tomorrow morning's relay will go fast.

She is doing math after the bell — but with her team, not alone at a screen. Devon prints the formulas in block letters. Iris double-checks them against the wall reference. Maya writes the worked examples in the corners. Three roles, one stack of cards. The bias correction stays in her head as a vague thing she can't say out loud yet — the equation runs high for us — and the teacher's question ("what would you need?") left her stuck. Maya doesn't know what she doesn't know yet. She has a feeling. The answer waits for Day 11.

DAY 09

Rivalry Card Response · Formula Relay · Match Day 3

Week 3 · Season 1 Day 3

They cannot change their stats. They have to plan around the numbers they have. Maya writes a plan on the board for the second time in two days. She doesn't get nervous for it.
Where Maya is nowDay 8 ended with Maya sloppier on inputs than she wanted to be — Devon caught her PEMDAS, Iris caught her scatter-point misread.
