Checking is a different muscle than writing.
HOOK

Rivalry Card Response

Every team finds a Rivalry Card face-down on their table when they walk in. Not the card they wrote — the card someone else wrote about them. The teacher writes the rule on the board in one line: Open the dashboard. Find the number they cited. THEN respond. Underneath, the two outcomes: (1) wrong number → write the correction on a sticky note with your team name and post it on their card on the wall. (2) right number → you can't change your stats this season. Find one stat where the matchup favors you and write a one-sentence plan for Match Day 3 on the board. Ten minutes. Flip.

Patience flips. The card on their table is from Iron Ravens #25 — the same team Maya wrote her own card to yesterday. Maya reads it out loud:

The card Iron Ravens #25 sent Patience
To: Patient Foxes #23

"Your Endurance = 4. By minute 3, your bot is at 28% power. Ours is at 46%. You'll be slow at the end while we still have full kick. Match Day 3, we win late."

— Iron Ravens #25

Maya opens the dashboard before she says anything. She pulls up Patience's row first — E = 4 — and reads it across to Devon. Devon runs the decay rate on his scrap paper: (20 − 4) × 1.5 = 24. Power at minute 3: 100 − 24 × 3 = 100 − 72 = 28%. Devon: "Twenty-eight percent. That's right." Maya pulls Iron Ravens' row: E = 8. Devon runs the decay: (20 − 8) × 1.5 = 18. At t = 3: 100 − 18 × 3 = 46%. Devon: "Forty-six. That's right too." Iris leans over: "They did their math right." Maya, checking Devon's two numbers against the dashboard's auto-computed values: "Both numbers match."

The teacher walks past. "Did you find that number in the dashboard?" Maya nods. "Both of them. Card's accurate." The teacher moves on. The teacher's check confirmed the dashboard was open and a number was recorded. It didn't confirm Devon got the decay rate right on his scrap paper. (He had to redo the Iron Ravens line twice before it matched what Maya was reading off the dashboard. Maya saw the redo and didn't say anything.)

No sticky note for Patience — the card is right. That means they have to scout back. Iron Ravens' stat line goes next to theirs on the dashboard:

Stat-line comparison — Patience vs Iron Ravens 
STAT       PATIENCE   IRON RAVENS   WHO HAS THE EDGE
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
SPEED        6          4            Patience (faster)
ENDURANCE    4          8            Iron Ravens (lasts longer)
TURNING      5          5            tied
POWER        5          3            Patience (chargePower 25 vs 9)

Maya stares at the comparison. The teacher's instruction was one stat. She has two — Speed and Power. Devon, quietly: "Use both? Or pick one?" Maya: "The plan is one sentence. But the sentence can name both stats if they work together." Iris: "They do. Faster off the line, harder kick. That's the same play we ran against them on Day 7." Maya nods. She picks up the marker and walks to the board, where teams are writing plans in a column. She writes:

Patience's plan on the board — one sentence
"Patient Foxes #23: score in the first 60 seconds — Speed 6 vs their 4 and chargePower 25 vs their 9 — before their Endurance starts to matter. Same play as Day 7 R1."

— Patient Foxes #23

Wrap. The teacher pulls her monitoring chart and picks one finding. She doesn't pick Patience. You don't argue with someone's claim. You check the number first. If it's wrong, you correct it. If it's right, you plan around it."

She glances at the board where the plan sentences are. "Twelve plans, twelve teams. Every one of these names a stat. That's the right shape. Now we make sure your formulas are accurate enough to trust the next set of claims. Formula Relay starts in two minutes."

Maya wrote a plan on the board. That's the second time in two days her math has gone up where the whole room can read it. This time she didn't think about it before she walked over. She just walked over and wrote. She and Iris and Devon picked the plan together. Iron Ravens did their math right. The card was accurate. Patience can't change their stats, but they can pick the fight they win. The teacher said it like it was obvious. Maya thinks of Day 7 Round 3, when she had the rule and didn't follow it. Today she's actually going to follow the plan.
CHALLENGE

Formula Relay: All Five Types in One Race

Each team gets a stack of 20 cards face-down on their table. "One person flips the top card. Your team solves it together. When you have the answer AND the work — formula, substitution, answer — one person brings the card and your paper to me at the stamp station. If it's right, I stamp it. If it's wrong, I give it back. I will not tell you what's wrong. I will say 'check your work.' One more attempt. After the second attempt, you move on to the next card whether it's right or not. Race ends at twenty-two minutes. First team to finish all twenty with fewer than three errors wins. After the race, you sort your cards by type and count your errors. That count tells you something you need this afternoon." Comprehension check: Iris restates the rule to Devon and Maya in fifteen seconds. Two attempts. After two, move on. Got it.

Race. The teacher goes to the stamp station with the answer key and a tally chart. She does not circulate. The team divides the work without talking about it: Devon flips, Maya does the math out loud, Iris writes the work on their paper and double-checks before anyone walks anywhere. Card 1 is THE BLITZ, Speed = 7. 7 × 5 = 35%. Stamped. Card 2 is MARATHON, Endurance = 6, t = 3. Maya: "20 minus 6 is 14. 14 times 1.5 is 21. Times 3 is 63. 100 minus 63 is 37%." Iris writes every step. Stamped. They get a rhythm. By card 7 they have six stamps and one card still in their hand.

First return. Card 16 is GRINDER — E = 8, t = 3. Same Iron Ravens formula they ran twice this morning. Maya goes fast: "100 minus 8 times 1.5 times 3 — that's 100 minus 36 — 64." Iris writes it. They take it up. Teacher hands it back: "Check your work." Devon, on the walk back: "What's wrong with it?" Maya re-reads. Oh. She forgot the parens. (20 − 8), not 8. "It's 20 minus E, not just E." Iris draws a slash through the 8 and writes: (20 − 8) × 1.5 × 3 = 12 × 4.5 = 54. 100 − 54 = 46%. Stamped on the second try. The same mistake she'd been catching other people on for two weeks. She made it because she rushed.

Second return. Three cards later, ZERO WASTE — Budget two-step. The card lists S = 4 · T = 3 · P = 6 · Budget = 20 · t = 2 minute and asks for power output at minute 2. Maya does the budget math fast: "20 minus 4 minus 3 minus 6 is 7. E = 7." Iris writes it. They take the card up. Teacher hands it back. "Check your work." Devon, on the walk back: "E equals 7. That's right, isn't it?" Iris re-reads the card aloud — slowly this time — and stops at the last clause. "It says power output at minute 2. We just found E. We haven't run the decay formula yet. That was step 1. There's a step 2." Maya re-reads. The card didn't ask for E. It asked for power at minute 2. She does step 2: P(2) = 100 − (20 − 7) × 1.5 × 2 = 100 − 13 × 3 = 100 − 39 = 61%. Iris writes the full work underneath. Stamped on the second try.

End of race. At twenty-two minutes the teacher calls time. Patience has finished seventeen cards. Two errors corrected (cards 16 and 20). One card sitting in their hand they didn't get to. They were not the first team done. They were not the last. They sit down with their pile.

Error sort. The teacher sets out six labeled piles around the room: Speed · Endurance · Turning · Power · Follow-Through · Budget. Teams sort and count their unstamped cards. Maya does the math: Endurance pile — one correction (the rushed Card 16). Budget pile — one correction (the ZERO WASTE step-2 miss). Everything else — zero. Iris writes the counts on the sorting labels. Endurance and Budget are tied at one each. The team has to pick one as their target. Maya, after a second: "Endurance. The bracket mistake is on a formula I should know cold. Budget I just rushed past the question — that's a reading mistake, not a math mistake." Iris nods and draws a star and the team name on the Endurance label and tapes it on the wall.

Summarize. The teacher pulls her tally chart from the stamp station. Two columns ran high: Power (eleven returns across all teams) and Budget (nine returns). Patience had zero of each. The teacher names them and asks the room: "Why those two? Talk for thirty seconds." Maya turns to her team. Iris: "Power is P². Most teams probably did P times 2." Devon nods. Maya remembers Day 4 — doubling Power quadruples charge speed — and almost says it, but the teacher calls on a different team first. A kid at the Magnetic Raptors #22 table says "we kept writing P × 2" and the teacher does the talk-move sequence — rephrase, restate, press, wait. The discussion gets to "squared is bigger than doubled" but it doesn't quite get to the structural why before the teacher steps in: "Every formula except Power is linear. Double the stat, roughly double the output. Power is the only one that grows faster than that. P goes from 4 to 8 — doubles — charge speed goes from 16 to 64. Four times, not two times. That's why Power surprises people." Maya copies the sentence into her journal.

Exit ticket — Match Day 3 prediction. Seven minutes. The teacher writes the three requirements on the board: (1) Formula stated with variable names. (2) Substitution shown with your team's actual stat values. (3) One sentence interpreting what the answer means in the context of the match. Then a footnote underneath: "Budget-target teams — use the Endurance formula with the E you calculated from the budget constraint, not the budget equation itself." Patience's target is Endurance, so the footnote isn't for them — but Maya re-reads it twice anyway, because she got Card 20 wrong by forgetting that the budget equation isn't where the answer lives. Iris pulls out a clean exit ticket sheet. Maya does the math on her scrap first. Same Patience numbers she has now run three times today. Iris writes the clean version onto the sheet.

Patience's Match Day 3 prediction — Endurance target 
TARGET FORMULA     Endurance — P(t) = 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 × t

PATIENCE           E = 4 · 3-minute match

SUBSTITUTION       P(3) = 100 − (20 − 4) × 1.5 × 3
                       = 100 − 16 × 1.5 × 3
                       = 100 − 72
                       = 28%

INTERPRETATION     Our power at the end of Match Day 3 will be 28%.
                   Same answer as Iron Ravens' rivalry card said this
                   morning. We need to score before minute 2 — our
                   motor will be too tired to chase down a fresh ball
                   at the end.
Two corrections in twenty-two minutes. Both on cards Maya should have caught — the parens she's been pointing out to other people for two weeks, and the Budget step-2 trap, which is just reading the card. One was a math mistake. The other was a reading mistake. Both came back with "check your work" and no other information. The teacher didn't tell her what was wrong. Iris caught the reading one. Maya caught the math one, after Devon asked the question. If she'd been working alone she'd have been stuck on both. She thinks about that during break. The team is the part she doesn't have when she's doing her September homework alone.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 1 Match Day 3 — Three Matches + The 20% Gap Rule

The teacher says to open the interactive lesson and use it during this activity. There is one new rule. Same cycle as every match day: prediction before, results after, loss recovery if you lose. New today: the 20% gap rule. The teacher writes it on the board. "After each match, calculate the gap between your prediction and your actual goals. Formula:" |prediction − actual| ÷ prediction × 100. "If the gap is over 20%, you write one sentence in the Notes column: 'The variable my model missed was _____ because _____.' One sentence. One specific variable from your formula. Not 'we scored fewer goals.' Not 'our bot was slow.' One number from your formula that didn't match what the bot actually did." Patience's target formula from this morning is still on the wall: Endurance — P(t) = 100 − (20 − E) × 1.5 × t. Maya's Round 1 prediction is already written from the Formula Relay exit ticket: 2 goals. Same plan as the Rivalry Card Response board this morning. Score before our 28% kicks in.

Round 1 — rematch with Iron Ravens #25. Pre-match: Maya reads Patience's prediction. "One goal. Score by minute 2." Iron Ravens reads theirs — "One for us. We hold until your Endurance drops." Three minutes. Patience reaches the ball first at 0:34, aligns, and pushes at chargePower 25 — the ball rolls steadily across the field. Patience scores at 1:18 — Maya's planned window. Iron Ravens tries to push late but at 2:48 her bot loses contact with the ball before the goal line. Final: 1 − 0. Maya enters it. Gap: |1 − 1| ÷ 1 × 100 = 0%. No sentence required. Iris, watching the Notes column stay blank: "Plan worked." Devon nods. The morning plan carried into the afternoon.

Round 2 — rematch with Kinetic Falcons #8. The matchup Patience lost on Day 7. Maya's prediction this time: 1 goal. She's using the Endurance formula even though Power is the real issue — that's the rule. "At minute 1 we're at 76% power. We get one clean chance at full power. After that, Falcons' Power decides it." Falcons: "One for us. Same as last time." Three minutes. Falcons reaches the ball first at 0:54 and pushes at chargePower 49 — same as Day 7. The ball drives over Patience's goal line. Maya's chance at minute 1 never comes; Patience is near the ball for stretches but can't drive it past Falcons' bot at chargePower 25. Neither team scores again. Final: 0 − 1. Maya enters it: |1 − 0| ÷ 1 × 100 = 100%. Sentence required.

Loss recovery + the 100% sentence. The teacher walks to Patience's table. Step 1: "That result was frustrating. That's a real feeling and it makes sense." Thirty seconds. Step 2: "Your prediction used Endurance. Your formula said 76% power at minute 1, which you said meant one goal. You scored zero. Look at the Endurance formula — did the decay rate match the actual battery behavior today?" Maya thinks. "The decay rate was fine. We just never had the ball. Endurance didn't matter because we never used our motor." Teacher: "That's your sentence. Write it down." Devon writes it in the Notes column, copying down what Maya said:

Patience's R2 sentence — first attempt + revised

First attempt (Devon, copying Maya):

"We scored fewer goals than predicted because Kinetic Falcons got the ball first."

Teacher reads it and stops at her table. "Which number in the Endurance formula was wrong?" Devon re-reads what he wrote. Outcome, not input. He crosses it out. Maya, looking at the formula on the dashboard: "The decay rate is right. The assumption that we'd HAVE the ball is what was wrong." Devon nods and rewrites.

Revised:

"The variable my model missed was the decay rate. I assumed our Endurance 4 would give us 76% power at minute 1 and that we'd get one clean chance. But the formula assumes we have the ball. Falcons got the ball first and we spent the whole match chasing — our power level at minute 1 didn't matter because we never had a kick lined up. Endurance is a power-budget formula, not a who-gets-the-ball formula."

Three sentences instead of one. The teacher reads it, nods, and writes "see Open Lab Track A" at the top of the page.

Round 3 — rematch with Steady Hawks #17. Hawks beat Patience on Day 8 with Power. Maya's prediction this time leans on the Endurance lens: Steady Hawks' Endurance is the lowest in the league (E = 2 → decay rate 27%/min). By minute 3 they hit the power floor. Maya: "They fade hard. We hold up. Prediction: 2 goals — one early before they're tired, one late after they're crawling." Hawks: "One for us. We do our damage early." Three minutes. Patience scores at 1:14 — Maya's early window. Hawks miss a kick at 2:18 (their motor is visibly slow). Patience's late chance at 2:42 goes wide — Hawks' defender bot is in the way even at 19% power. Final: 1 − 0. Maya enters it on the dashboard: |2 − 1| ÷ 2 × 100 = 50%. Over 20%. Sentence required again. Devon writes faster this time — Maya has been narrating the insight while watching the late chance go wide:

Patience's R3 sentence — written without teacher prompting
"My model missed the decay rate — for Hawks, not for us. I thought their decay 27 would drop their power so low by minute 2 that they couldn't defend. But Power 7 still kicked the ball away even when their motor was slow. The decay number was right. I just assumed low power meant no defense — that part was wrong. Devon said it: "Power and Endurance aren't the same kind of stat." I think I was supposed to see that before I wrote the prediction."

The teacher reads it on her circulation pass, doesn't say anything, and moves on. Maya watches her go. The lack of stopping is its own confirmation.

Gap entry review. Nine minutes. The teacher walks the room checking that every row over 20% has a sentence. Patience has two sentences in their Notes column — Devon's handwriting, Maya's framing. Maya re-reads both before the teacher gets to her. The R2 sentence feels more honest. The R3 one feels more careful — they wrote it knowing the teacher would read it. She doesn't know if that means they wrote what she actually thought, or what they thought the teacher wanted. She leaves both as written.

Standings after Match Day 3. The teacher posts the running Season 1 leaderboard on the wall. Best Prediction first, before Wins. Across all three match days so far, lowest average gap goes to Steady Hawks #17 again — they've led that column every day of the season. Then Wins: Kinetic Falcons #8 is undefeated. Patience is at 4 − 1 − 3 across 8 matches. Middle of the table. "Three more matches tomorrow afternoon — Final Round — and then Season 1 closes for real," the teacher says. "Today we found that a prediction is only as good as the accuracy of its inputs. When the gap is over 20%, the model didn't fail — one number in the model was wrong. Naming that number is the first step in improving the model. The gap sentences you wrote today are the evidence base for the reallocation decision after Final Round."

Patience is at 4 − 1 − 3 after 8 matches. Not last. Not first. Middle. Maya wrote two sentences today she wouldn't have known how to write a week ago. The first one she wrote wrong and the teacher made her fix it. The second one she wrote right on the first try. Both of them name a number from a formula. Neither of them says "we scored fewer goals." She is looking at her Notes column before Open Lab and noticing the column is no longer just numbers. It's an argument with herself about what her formulas can and can't see. Three more matches tomorrow afternoon, then Season 1 closes for real and Season 2 begins. She can change the bot after that. She has been writing down what she would change for nine days. Two days from now she gets to find out if she was right.
OPEN LAB

Data Swap + Post-Match Debrief

The teacher writes one sentence on the board: "Every team's dashboard is going to a different team. That team has 10 minutes to find one pattern in your data." Underneath she writes the distinction: pattern, not result · analysis, not advice. She explains it with one example. "'They lost Match 3' is a result. 'Their goal rate dropped each match day despite constant Speed' is a pattern. One is news. The other is data." The pairings go up on the board. Patience gets Magnetic Raptors #22's dashboard. Cobalt Wolves #14 gets Patience's. The team that drew Patience on Day 7 R3 is the team that's going to read Patience's eight matches of data. Maya doesn't notice the symmetry until she sees Cobalt's table pull up Patience's dashboard across the room.

Explore — what Patience finds about Magnetic Raptors. Maya opens Raptors' dashboard on her screen. Eight matches. The first three from Day 7 are losses; Raptors went 0 − 0 − 3. The Day 8 row is a loss to Patience. The Day 9 rows aren't filled in yet because the dashboard updates are still running. Maya scrolls to the stat allocation panel: S = 4, E = 6, T = 6, P = 4. Turning is 6 — that's the highest in the league. She scrolls to the goal counts across all 8 matches: 0, 0, 0, 1, 0, ?, ?, ?. Total scored: 1 goal. Across 5 completed matches. Iris reads over her shoulder. Maya, slowly: "Their Turning is the highest in the league and they almost never score." Iris: "Card?" Devon picks up the index card and writes a first try, copying Maya's framing: "Raptors' Turning didn't help them." Iris reads it and shakes her head. "That's not a pattern. That's a result. The teacher said specific." Devon crosses it out. Then, looking at the dashboard Maya is still scrolling: "Look at the opponents in the matches where they scored zero. Their Power." Patience's finding card — about Magnetic Raptors
"We noticed Magnetic Raptors' Turning = 6 is the highest in the league but they scored 0 goals in every Day 7 match against opponents with Power ≥ 7 (Cobalt, Iron Ravens, Steady Hawks). The bottleneck is Power, not Turning."

— Patient Foxes #23

Card exchange. Dashboards go back to their owners. The teacher gives each team 60 seconds to read their finding card aloud to the team whose dashboard they analyzed. What Cobalt Wolves found in Patience's data
"We noticed Patient Foxes' goal count drops to 0 or 1 every time you play opponents with Power ≥ 7 (Kinetic Falcons twice, Steady Hawks twice — that's 4 of your 8 matches). Against Magnetic Raptors (Power = 4) you scored 2 goals. The pattern: your goal count is bottlenecked by your opponent's Power. Your gap sentences across Day 7 and Day 9 keep naming Endurance, but the data shows the constraint is your own Power — only 5, which gives chargePower 25. The bottleneck is your Power, not your Endurance."

— Cobalt Wolves #14

Maya doesn't write anything down while Cobalt reads. She is still listening when Cobalt's Analyst sits back down.

Summarize. The teacher picks three teams to share their finding cards out loud — Cobalt's card about Patience is the third one she picks because it's the cross-metric comparison she wants on the board. She names the practice for the whole room after Cobalt reads: "What Cobalt just did — holding two variables in mind at once, in this case opponent Power and Patience's own Power, and asking how they constrain each other — is called controlling variables. That's one of the most powerful moves in math and in modeling. You'll use that term by name when we get to systems of inequalities in Week 3." Maya copies controlling variables into her journal. Underneath the phrase she writes one line of her own: "Cobalt said it in one sentence. I've been writing around it for nine days."

Exit ticket. Three minutes. The teacher hands out half-sheets. Two questions: write your team's finding in one sentence, and write a Track A hypothesis for tomorrow. Maya writes:

Patience's exit ticket — finding + hypothesis (Devon's official copy, Maya wrote her own)

Q1 — Data Swap finding (about Magnetic Raptors):

"Magnetic Raptors' Turning = 6 is the highest in the league but their goal count is 1 across 5 completed matches. Across all the matches where they faced an opponent with Power ≥ 7, they scored 0. The pattern: high Turning doesn't translate to goals when their chargePower (16) is below their opponent's chargePower. The bottleneck is Power."

Q2 — Track A hypothesis (for Patience's own Season 2 reallocation):

"If we change Power from 5 to 7, we expect Patience's chargePower to go from 25 to 49 because chargePower = P². We expect to score more against high-Power opponents because our Match Day 3 Round 2 sentence said we never had the ball when Falcons had chargePower 49 — at chargePower 49 ourselves, we'd match them on first contact instead of losing it every time. We'd have to drop 2 points somewhere else. Probably Speed from 6 to 4 because our Endurance and Turning are already where we need them, and Cobalt's finding said our Power is the bottleneck, not our Speed."
Cobalt's Analyst said it in one sentence. The bottleneck is your Power, not your Endurance. Maya has been writing around that sentence for three days — the Day 8 Open Lab question, the Day 9 R2 loss recovery, every gap sentence she wrote this afternoon. She had every piece of it. She just couldn't see past her own target formula. Endurance was the lens she'd been told was hers in the morning's Formula Relay, so Endurance was the variable she kept naming when something else was the actual constraint. Cobalt wasn't using Endurance. Cobalt was looking at the dashboard cold. And Cobalt saw it. Maya is going to think about that on the walk home. What she wrote on the exit ticket isn't a guess. It is a hypothesis another team's analysis just confirmed. Tomorrow she takes 2 points off Speed and puts them on Power. She's been writing that hypothesis for nine days. Today is the first day she's allowed to write it as a hypothesis instead of a complaint.

CHALLENGE

Distance Formula: How Far Did Your Ball Travel?

Worksheet on every desk, but it's also an interactive lesson. Three fixed positions on the 8 × 4 field: A = (4, 2) at tip-off, G = (8, 2) at opponent's goal. B is yours — where your ball landed after your team's best Season 1 charge. The driving question on the board: "Can your bot move the ball far enough to score?" The distance formula sits next to it: d = √((x₂ − x₁)² + (y₂ − y₁)²). The teacher reads one sentence: "Subtract first. Square each difference separately. Then add. Then take the square root." Hard stop before any calculation: "Mark Position B on your field diagram. Read all ten steps before computing anything. Including the Big Idea at the bottom."

Picking B. Maya, Iris, and Devon huddle. The best match was Day 8 R2 vs Magnetic Raptors — they kept the Gallery Walk card on their table as reference. Patience's bot launched from (0, 4), ran the L-path to (4, 2), and kicked. The ball ended up… somewhere east of (4, 2), but slightly off the straight line because Patience's Turning = 5 didn't square the bot perfectly. Maya estimates: "Six on x, maybe three on y. The kick went east and slightly up." Devon agrees. Iris writes B = (6, 3) on the worksheet.

Step 2 — A → G. The worksheet does this one for them: A and G share y = 2, so it's a horizontal distance. 8 − 4 = 4 units. Maya writes 4 in the blank without thinking — she's looked at the field for ten days.

Step 3 — A → B — and the algebra error. Devon has the marker. "x difference: 6 minus 4 is 2. y difference: 3 minus 2 is 1. Add: 2 plus 1 is 3. Square: 9. Square root: 3." He writes d = 3 and reaches for Iris's sheet to copy across. Iris reads what he wrote, frowns, and points: "We added before we squared. The formula says square each piece first, then add." Devon stares. Iris does the small-number test out loud: "3 plus 4, squared, is 49. 3 squared plus 4 squared is 9 plus 16 is 25. Those aren't the same." Devon's face does the thing it does when he realizes a small problem. He recomputes: (2)² + (1)² = 4 + 1 = 5. d = √5 ≈ 2.24 units. Iris writes the corrected work on her own sheet. Devon writes it on the teacher's circulation sheet, in case the teacher asks.

Step 4 — B → G — estimate first. The worksheet asks: before calculating, should B → G be greater than or less than 4? Maya looks at the field. B is between A and G visually, but it's not on the straight line — it's one unit north. She estimates "less than 4 but not by much." Calculation: x difference 8 − 6 = 2, square 4. y difference 2 − 3 = −1, square 1. Sum 5. √5 ≈ 2.24 units. Same number as A → B. Maya stops. "Both legs are √5. That's because the angles are symmetric across the center of the kick." She doesn't say it aloud but she thinks it. She writes it in the margin.

Steps 5–7 — progress, efficiency. Distance to goal before: 4. After: 2.24. Progress = 4 − 2.24 = 1.76 units. Maya writes the sentence on Step 6: "The ball moved 2.24 units total, but only 1.76 units reduced the distance to the goal." Step 7 — efficiency. Devon does it first and writes 2.24 / 1.76 = 1.27. Iris reads it and pauses. "That's bigger than 1. Efficiency should be less than 1, right? Because some of the movement was wasted." Maya re-reads the worksheet on the dashboard: "useful progress ÷ total movement." Devon flips the ratio: 1.76 / 2.24 ≈ 0.79. 79% efficient. Devon crosses out the 1.27 and writes 0.79. 21% of the kick was wasted on the wrong direction.

Step 8 — minimum kicks. Useful progress per kick = 1.76. Distance from A to G = 4. 4 ÷ 1.76 ≈ 2.27. Round up: 3 kicks to score, theoretically. Patience scored twice in the actual match (at 0:52 and at 2:38). Two kicks, two goals. Devon: "Our actual was better than the theoretical minimum." Iris: "Because Step 8 assumes every kick is identical to this one. Some of our kicks were straighter than this one."

Patience's Distance Formula worksheet — Steps 6–10 + Big Idea 
STEP 6 — Movement vs. Progress
  Distance ball traveled (A → B):  2.24 units
  Distance toward goal:            1.76 units
  "The ball moved 2.24 units total, but only 1.76 units
   reduced the distance to the goal."

STEP 7 — Efficiency
  1.76 ÷ 2.24 = 0.79  (79%)
  21% of the kick was wasted on direction.

STEP 8 — Minimum kicks to score
  4 ÷ 1.76 = 2.27   →  round up: 3 kicks

STEP 9 — Why?
  [✓] The ball went off-angle.
  [ ] Too much Turning — bot over-corrected.
  [✓] Power was low so we needed multiple kicks.

STEP 10 — Strategy for Season 2 (must include one number)
  "For Season 2 we need either more Turning so the bot squares
   up and we don't lose 21% to angle, OR more Power so the kick
   reaches farther — chargePower 49 instead of 25. I'm not sure
   which. Cobalt said the bottleneck is Power. The 21% on this
   card says Turning matters too. Pick tomorrow."

BIG IDEA
  "Hitting the ball farther does NOT always help because my
   ball moved 2.24 units but only 1.76 units reduced the
   distance to the goal."

  This means: 21% of every kick at our current Turning value
  goes to direction, not progress.

Summary. The teacher walks the WHY of Step 2 first — A and G share y = 2, so the y-term zeroes out — then picks a team where B → G came out greater than A → G (Kinetic Falcons, whose ball flew sideways). The Falcons Analyst reads: "Our B was (3, 4). B → G came out about 5.4 — bigger than A → G of 4. The kick moved the ball away from the goal." The teacher names the practice: "What Falcons just did — naming a specific coordinate where the general claim (more movement equals more progress) breaks — is called counterexample reasoning. It's one of the most precise moves in math." Then the Big Idea round. Maya reads hers to Devon: "My ball moved 2.24 units but only 1.76 units reduced the distance to the goal." Devon: "Same numbers as mine — we did the math together." They mark agree.

Exit ticket — two problems. Maya solves the first quickly: A = (4, 2), B = (6, 1), G = (8, 2). B → G = √((8−6)² + (2−1)²) = √(4 + 1) = √5 ≈ 2.24 units. Same shape she just worked. Problem 2 asks her to interpret a team that moved 3.6 units and ended 2.2 units from the goal. Maya writes: "A → G was 4 units. After the charge, B → G = 2.2 units. They reduced the distance to the goal by 1.8 of the 3.6 units moved — that's 1.8 / 3.6 = 50% efficient. Moving the ball helped, but only half the movement was useful toward scoring. Same shape as ours — except ours was 79% efficient because our angle was less off."

Maya's hypothesis from yesterday's Open Lab said move 2 Speed points to Power. The Distance Formula data says some of Patience's wasted kick was angle — Turning, not Power. Yesterday's hypothesis is not as clean as it was yesterday. The 21% Maya just calculated is a Turning loss, and Patience already has Turning = 5. If she moves points to Power, the kick is harder but still goes off-angle. If she moves points to Turning, the kick goes straight but is still only chargePower 25. Cobalt's finding said Power is the bottleneck. Her own Distance Formula data says angle is also a constraint. Both are probably true. She doesn't know which one matters more yet. This afternoon she has to pick — Final Round is the last chance to test before reallocation tomorrow.
CHALLENGE

Cold Recall Check

Set up. Five questions on the worksheet. Individual. No notes. No devices. Ten minutes. The teacher says one sentence: "All week you've done these calculations with your bot in front of you. The bot is a memory cue. Today we take the bot away. This tells you — and me — what you've actually kept." Maya reads the room as she picks up her pencil. This is the second recall check. The first one was Day 5, when she rounded √25 ≈ 5.2 out of habit and lost a point on what should have been an automatic answer. The teacher's marginal note that day said: "√25 is exactly 5. No rounding." Maya wrote in her own margin underneath: "Next time, check if √x is a clean number before writing ≈."

The five questions. Q1 is percent change: (45 − 30) ÷ 30 × 100 = 50%. Maya does it in her head and writes the work to be safe. Q2 is slope-intercept: slope 2, point (1, 3), solve for b. 3 = 2(1) + b → b = 1 → y = 2x + 1. Q3 is the distance formula — same shape as this afternoon. (2, 5) to (7, 17). x difference = 5, square = 25. y difference = 12, square = 144. Add: 169. d = √169. Maya's pencil starts to write ≈ 13. She stops. Five days ago she'd already have moved on. Today she pauses and reads what she wrote. 169 — is that a clean square? 13 times 13 is 169. She erases the ≈ and writes the equals sign. d = 13 units exactly.

Q4 — the zero-crossing. The bot's power decays at P(t) = 100 − 12t. When does P reach zero? Maya sets the function equal to zero: 100 − 12t = 0. Rearranges: 12t = 100. Divides: t = 100/12 ≈ 8.33 minutes. Done in 30 seconds. She doesn't notice that 12 is the decay rate for an Endurance value, or that solving for t when P = 0 is the move that's about to matter for Season 2 reallocation tomorrow. She just does the algebra. Q5 is unit rates: 150 cm in 5 sec → 30 cm/sec → 240 cm in 8 sec. Two steps, both shown. Pencil down at 8 minutes 20 seconds.

Maya's Cold Recall Check — Day 10 vs Day 5 comparison 
                            DAY 5            DAY 10
                            ───────         ────────
Q1   Slope                    ✓                —
Q2   Recipe / Slope-int        ✓             ✓
Q3   Distance formula          ✗  (≈ 5.2)    ✓  (= 13 exact)
Q4   Endurance / zero-cross    ✓             ✓
Q5   Distance / unit rates     ✓             ✓
                            ───────         ────────
                              4 / 5           5 / 5

THE DIFFERENCE
  Day 5 — rushed √25, wrote ≈ on a clean square.
  Day 10 — almost did it again on √169. Caught it.
  Both questions: distance formula. Both clean squares.
  Both moments: the same kind of habit.

  My own margin note from Day 5, in my own handwriting:
  "Next time, check if √x is a clean number before writing ≈."

  I checked.

Answer check. The teacher reads the answers aloud, fast, not stopping to re-teach. Maya tracks each one against her paper. Five for five. The teacher pauses only on Q4 to ask the room: "For those who got it — how did you know the move was to set P(t) equal to zero?" A student answers. The teacher names it: "That move — set the function equal to zero and solve for the variable — is called finding a zero of a function. In Season 2, it tells you when your bot's power hits zero." Then she moves on. Then her closing line, the one that turns the whole ten minutes into a bridge: "If Q4 gave you trouble, that's important information before Season 2 planning starts. Come find me before Open Lab. I want to show you one thing — it takes two minutes."

5 out of 5. Maya does not feel triumphant. She feels something quieter: the score is the side effect of one specific habit she changed since Day 5. She caught the ≈ before she wrote it. The score would have been 4 out of 5 — the same as Day 5 — if she hadn't. Five days of distance formula practice between Day 5 and now turned one rushed reflex into one slowed-down half-second of checking. That's the only thing that changed. She puts the paper face-down on her desk and looks at her three documents from earlier today, all sitting next to the Cold Recall sheet. Yesterday's hypothesis. The Gallery Walk card from this morning. The Big Idea from the Distance Formula block right after it. Now today's 5 out of 5. Four pieces of paper about the same bot, in the same handwriting, all written by a kid who five days ago would have rounded √25 wrong. She isn't sure what to do with that observation. She files it next to the question of whether to move points to Power or to Turning. Both are open. The Final Round this afternoon is the last data she gets before she has to choose. She is more ready for Season 2 than she was at the break, and she could not have told you why if you'd asked her.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 1 Final Round + Analysis Block

Pre-match audit. "The matches are not the main event. Your dashboard has been collecting data for three match days. Today you find out what it actually says about your model. Before the first bot runs, you have ten minutes."

The three audit questions — what each one is actually asking

Q1 — What is your average Error across Days 7, 8, and 9?

What the question is asking you to do: Open your dashboard. Find the Error column. Add up every Error value (signs matter — +2 and −2 partially cancel each other out). Divide that sum by the number of matches you played. Write the answer with its sign.

What the answer tells you: On average across the whole season, how far off your prediction was. If your answer is +1.5, your predictions were 1.5 goals too high per match, on average. If your answer is −0.8, your predictions were 0.8 goals too low per match. If your answer is near zero, your over-predictions and under-predictions canceled out — that's balanced, not necessarily accurate (you might have been wildly wrong in both directions; Q3 catches that).

Q2 — Is your average Error positive (over-predicted) or negative (under-predicted)?

What the question is asking you to do: Look at your answer from Q1. Just the sign. Circle one word.

What the answer tells you: Which direction your model is biased. Positive means you consistently over-predicted — you wrote down higher goal counts than actually happened. Negative means you consistently under-predicted. (The reason: Error = Predicted − Actual. If your predictions were bigger than your actuals, the subtraction comes out positive.) A consistent sign across many matches means there's something your model isn't capturing — an input variable that would push the prediction in the opposite direction. You'll figure out which variable later using the interactive lesson, after the matches.

Q3 — Compare your first Error from Day 7 to your most recent Error from Day 9. One word: improving, worsening, or flat.

What the question is asking you to do: Find your first Error from Day 7 R1. Find your most recent Error from Day 9 R3. Compare their sizes (use absolute values — an Error of +3 is bigger than an Error of +1, and an Error of −2 is bigger than an Error of +1). Pick one word.

What the answer tells you: Whether your model is getting better over time. Improving = recent errors are smaller than early errors (your predictions are getting closer to reality as you learn). Worsening = recent errors are bigger (something is throwing off your model — maybe you started facing opponents your model wasn't built for). Flat = the size hasn't changed much (your model is whatever it's going to be — it's not learning OR forgetting).

Round 1 — Magnetic Raptors #22. The team that built their Best Match card on Patience's data swap finding two days ago. Pre-match: Maya reads her prediction. "Equation gives four. Revised for opponent Power: one. Raptors' Power is 4 — chargePower 16. Ours is 25. If we reach the ball first, we score. If they reach it first, their push falls short." Raptors: "Three for us." Three minutes. Patience reaches the ball first at 0:48 — her bot cruises at 30% wheel power, Raptors at 20% — and pushes at chargePower 25. The ball rolls clean over Raptors' goal line. After the reset, both bots head back toward center. Patience gets there first again at 1:36 but the push drifts off-angle and the ball goes wide. Raptors finds the ball at 2:12, pushes at chargePower 16 — the ball stops short of Patience's goal. Final: 1 − 0. Maya enters it. Gap: |1 − 1| ÷ 1 × 100 = 0%. Error 0. Devon writes the one-sentence entry: "R1 fits the pattern — opponent Power below ours, we get there first, we convert. Exact prediction."

Round 2 — Cobalt Wolves #14. The team that named Patience's bottleneck in the Data Swap. Cobalt's Power is 9 — chargePower 81, more than three times Patience's. Patience has never beaten them. Maya goes pessimistic: "Their chargePower is 81. We've never scored against them. Predict zero." Devon hesitates. "Iron Ravens prediction was one and we got one." Maya: "Iron Ravens' Power is 3. Cobalt's is 9. Different problem. Predict zero." Cobalt: "Three for us." Three minutes. Cobalt reaches the ball first at 0:42 and pushes at chargePower 81 — the ball flies clean across the field and over Patience's goal line. Reset. Patience gets to center first this time at 1:24 and pushes at chargePower 25. The ball rolls toward Cobalt's goal. Cobalt's defender bot is positioned wide of the path. The ball crosses the line. Goal. Maya stares at the field. She predicted zero. Patience scored. Reset. Cobalt reaches the ball first at 2:18, pushes at 81, scores again. Final: 1 − 2. Maya enters it. Error −1 — Patience's first under-prediction all season. Devon writes the entry: "R2 doesn't fit. We went too pessimistic. The +1 goal is the one I didn't predict — first time my error sign flipped all season. Cobalt's defender was out of position when we got to the ball. The goal was luck, but my model couldn't see it."

Final Season 1 record: Patience finishes 5 wins · 1 draw · 4 losses across 10 matches. Total goals: 9. Average actual: 0.90 goals per match. Average Error across all 10: (8 + 0 − 1) ÷ 10 = +0.70. Still positive. Smaller than before. The trend held.

Analysis Block — Phase A. Teams open the interactive lesson. The tool opens with three tabs: Calculate, Identify, Build Hypothesis. Phase A asks for the Error sum, count, average, sign, and trend. Devon enters: sum +7, count 10, average +0.70, positive, improving. The page shows the positive tile and trend improving. The team moves to Phase B.

Phase B — find the pattern. Top 3 biggest errors: Day 8 R1 (+3, vs Steady Hawks P = 7), Day 7 R3 (+2, vs Cobalt Wolves P = 9), Day 7 R2 (+1, vs Kinetic Falcons P = 7). Devon types each opponent's dominant stat into the table. All three biggest errors come against opponents with Power ≥ 7. The tool flashes a green hint: "Clear pattern — the same stat appears in all three biggest errors." Devon writes their sentence using the sentence builder: "Our model consistently over-predicts because it does not account for opponent Power." The tool accepts it — green check, not the result-language warning. This is the sentence Cobalt named for them two days ago. Today they're writing it into the official analysis tool, with the dropdown selecting Power as the missing variable.

Phase C — and the equation problem. Devon enters Patience's Day 8 scatter plot equation: y = 0.5x + 1. Current Speed = 6. Proposed Speed = 4 (the move from their Day 9 Open Lab hypothesis: shift 2 points to Power). The tool auto-calculates: Current predicted: 4.00. Proposed predicted: 3.00. Season 1 actual avg: 0.90. Model-predicted difference: 3.00 − 0.90 = +2.10. The equation is telling them that even at Speed 4, they'll still over-predict by 2.10 goals. The tool's Q4 explanation field asks: "Why does this stat change fix the problem?" Maya, Iris, and Devon hesitate. The equation can't see Power. Devon writes:

The team's Phase C explanation — wrestling with the equation's limitation

Q4 — Why does this stat change fix the problem?

"The equation only uses Speed. Power isn't in it. That's exactly the gap our Phase B finding identified. The equation tells us the predicted goals will go DOWN from 4 to 3 when we lower Speed — but that's because the equation can't see that our chargePower will go from 25 to 49 when we move those 2 points to Power. We're betting my actual goals will go UP even though the predicted goals go down. The equation is a Speed-only model. We are about to break it on purpose."

What would prove us wrong?

"Our actual goals stay at 0.90 or drop after we switch to S = 4, P = 7. That would mean Power wasn't the bottleneck Cobalt said it was — or it would mean Turning matters more than Power, which is what our Distance Formula 21% loss this morning suggested."

Peer pressure-test. Patience hands their hypothesis sheet to Iron Ravens. Iron Ravens reads it and writes one objection on the bottom: "Your equation uses Speed. You're lowering Speed. The equation says your predicted goals go DOWN. Your hypothesis assumes the actual will go UP because of Power — but the equation can't verify that. Where's the mathematical evidence that Power matters more than the Speed loss?" Maya reads the objection and feels the friction of it. It's a fair challenge. Devon writes their response next to it: "The mathematical evidence is in our Phase B table. Three biggest errors, all opponents with Power ≥ 7. That's the residual pattern. The equation can't model Power, but the residual shows Power is what's missing. Cobalt's data swap finding confirmed it from outside." Iron Ravens reads, nods, writes back: "OK. The residual pattern is the math you can show. Don't let anyone tell you that's not evidence." Patience doesn't revise their hypothesis. It survives the peer challenge.

Closing + exit. The teacher names the practice for the room: "What you just did — looking at a pattern across an entire dataset and identifying the input variable the model was missing — is called residual analysis. It's the same mathematical move data scientists use to improve predictive models. You did not just play a game. You built and tested a model for ten days." Then the exit prompt: "Write your final Season 2 hypothesis. Stat change. Equation calculation. One sentence connecting to your residual finding." Maya copies the auto-generated hypothesis from the tool onto the half-sheet:

Patience's Season 2 hypothesis — exit slip (Devon's official copy)
"If we change Speed from 6 to 4 (moving 2 points to Power, taking P from 5 to 7), our scatter plot equation y = 0.5x + 1 predicts 3.00 goals per match. Our Season 1 actual average was 0.90 goals per match — model-predicted difference +2.10. This addresses our finding because Phase B showed our three biggest errors all came against opponents with Power ≥ 7, and the equation can't model the chargePower change from 25 to 49 — but the residual pattern says that change is what matters."

— Patient Foxes #23 · Devon, Iris, and Maya
Patience finishes Season 1 at 5 − 1 − 4. Maya finishes the day with one hypothesis written in three different documents: the Open Lab exit ticket from Day 9, the Distance Formula Step 10 sentence from this morning, the Phase C field. All three say move 2 Speed points to Power. All three for slightly different reasons. The Day 9 hypothesis was about chargePower. The Distance Formula hypothesis split between Power and Turning. The Analysis Block hypothesis is residual-based: the math shows Power is what the equation can't see. Three roads, same destination. She doesn't know yet whether that means she's right or whether she's been confirming her own bias for two days straight. Iron Ravens accepted her residual argument. The peer challenge was real, and her answer held. Tomorrow they change the bot. what if the equation is right and the residual is wrong? She does not know how to answer that yet. They have the data to find out tomorrow.

Season 2 Planning

Two tracks, one decision. The bot has no opponent right now. We'd just be moving it around." The Turning question, resolved. The Distance Formula's 21% loss this morning had Maya half-convinced Turning mattered. The matchup column says: not yet. The contingency is written. The primary hypothesis stays. Iris, while Maya was tallying, opens MakeCode and types in the new allocation: S = 4, E = 4, T = 5, P = 7. Sum = 20. She saves it as "patience-s2-draft.mkcd" — draft, not committed — and closes the program. The bot stays at Speed 6 until tomorrow afternoon — Practice Matches is the first time it runs at the new config; Thursday is when the allocation locks after the Commissioner vote.

Pair-share. Adjacent team is Steady Hawks, the Best Prediction leader all season. Devon reads Patience's hypothesis aloud, Big Idea sentence and Power matchup data and contingency line. Close. The teacher names the practice for the room: "What you just did — building a prediction from data before running the test — is exactly how engineers and scientists plan. You didn't guess. You built an argument. That's what Season 2 is: an argument that your data supports, tested against a real match." Then the consolidation line: "Season 2 planning continues tomorrow with Commissioner proposals. When you come in, your hypothesis is the starting point — not a blank slate. Maya's Closing Ritual entry — Day 10
"The mistake: yesterday in Match Day 3 R2 vs Falcons I wrote in the Notes column 'we scored fewer goals because Falcons got the ball first.' The teacher read it and asked which number in the Endurance formula was wrong. I just said what happened in the match instead of what was wrong in my model.

What it led to: I crossed it out and wrote the version that named the decay rate. Today in Phase B I wrote one like it for the whole season — 'our model over-predicts because it doesn't account for opponent Power.' I would not have written that on Tuesday.

What I changed: I stopped writing about what happened. I started writing about what number was wrong. Devon helped me see the difference."
Maya walks out of the room with her hypothesis card, her exit slip, her Big Idea worksheet, and the Closing Ritual entry in her journal. Five pieces of paper. All five about the same bot, in her own handwriting, written by a kid who is not the same kid who walked into Day 5 with a confused look at √25. She is not naming this transformation. She does not have the vocabulary for it and would not use it if she did. She has the matchup data. She has the contingency note. She has the Thursday plan. Iris has the .mkcd file saved as a draft. Maya has the dashboard bookmarked to the Power column. They are not done thinking about this. They are done thinking about this today. Tomorrow they write the Commissioner proposal. Thursday the bot actually changes and the allocation locks. Maya does not know yet whether the change will work. She does know she made the argument as carefully as she could. That is the most honest thing she has done this week, and it is also the smallest one.
DAY 11

Stat Rankings · Proportional Scaling · Commissioner Proposal Prep

Wednesday · Week 3 · Season 2 planning (no matches today)
Maya's arc today: The day opens with a public claim about which stat mattered most for Season 1 wins. Maya stands on POWER. The data check confirms it. For the first time all camp, her instinct is the same as the data — and the gap between those two things, even when it's zero, is what a Commissioner proposal is built on. Then proportional scaling and the proposal writing carry the rest of the day.
Where Maya is nowYesterday closed with five documents pointing at one decision. The hypothesis (move 2 Speed → Power) is written but not committed — Thursday's session locks it. Today she has to make the case for it in a Commissioner proposal that other teams can read. The argument has to leave her notebook.
HOOK

Human Number Line: Stat Rankings

Painter's tape runs the full width of the room at floor level. Sign at the left end: LEAST IMPORTANT FOR WINNING. Sign at the right end: MOST IMPORTANT FOR WINNING. One sticky note and one marker at every seat. The teacher's rule on the board, read aloud: "No dashboards, no data logs — just your memory of Season 1. Write the ONE stat you believe mattered most, then stand where the line matches how strongly you believe it." The comprehension check, said once: "The question is which stat mattered most for winning matches — not which stat you gave the most points to. Those can be different." Thirty seconds.

Maya writes POWER without hesitation — three days of analysis, two outside readers, and yesterday's matchup column all point there. Devon writes POWER next to her — yesterday's Phase B sentence is still in his head, opponent Power is the missing variable. Iris writes SPEED. Maya looks at her sticky note. "Speed?" Iris: "Our equation is built on Speed. Day 8 scatter plot, all season. Speed is the only variable the equation actually sees. Power doesn't even exist in that equation." Maya opens her mouth to argue and stops. It's a fair point. The equation IS Speed. Power isn't in it. She says: "Walk to where you actually believe it. We can argue after." All three stand up.

Maya walks to the right end — far right, certainty, three days of work behind her. Devon stands next to her. Iris walks to the left-middle — moderate confidence in Speed because she knows the equation is incomplete. Across the room: four teams on SPEED clustered around the middle, two teams on POWER on the right (Patience and Cobalt Wolves), one team on ENDURANCE on the right (Steady Hawks — the Best Prediction leader all season). Nobody on Turning. Nobody on Budget. The teacher reads the distribution aloud: "Four on Speed, two on Power, one on Endurance, nobody on Turning or Budget. Speed is the room's claim." She points to one of the middle Speed teams. "You're in the middle of the Speed cluster. What made you uncertain?" That team's Analyst says, "We had high Speed and we won some, but we also lost a lot. So I don't know." The teacher doesn't respond. She doesn't do anything to change the feelings of uncertainty.

Seven minutes in the class is asked to "Look at the full league: all teams, all win records. Find the teams with the most wins. What did they allocate to your stat? Now find the teams with the fewest wins. What did they allocate? Is there a pattern across the league? Does where you're standing still make sense?" Maya, Iris and Devon open the dashboard. They have three minutes to do some research. Devon pulls up the league standings. Top three by record: Kinetic Falcons (7 − 2 − 1), Cobalt Wolves (6 − 1 − 3), Steady Hawks (6 − 2 − 2). Maya checks their stats: Falcons P = 7, Cobalt P = 9, Hawks P = 7. All three top teams allocated Power 7 or higher. Then the bottom: Iron Ravens (3 − 1 − 6), Magnetic Raptors (1 − 0 − 9). Ravens P = 3. Raptors P = 4. The bottom teams all allocated Power 4 or lower. Then she checks Speed. Iron Ravens has the highest Speed in the league at S = 8 — and the second-worst record. Cobalt Wolves has the lowest Speed at S = 3 — and is in the top three. Across the league, Speed doesn't line up with winning at all. Iris reads the screen over Maya's shoulder and looks at the line. She was in the wrong place.

Patience is now together on POWER. Maya doesn't say "told you so." She doesn't say anything. Iris drops her sticky note, picks it up, looks at the marker, and writes POWER over the top of the SPEED she wrote ten minutes ago. "The equation can't see Power. But the league did." The room's claim shifted from Speed to Power in three minutes.

The teacher calls on Patient Foxes #23. Maya reads their numbers: "Top three teams by wins, all with Power 7 or higher. Bottom three teams, all with Power 4 or lower. Speed didn't show a pattern — Iron Ravens had the highest Speed and one of the worst records." The teacher says it cleanly: "So you're saying: across the league, the Power allocation predicts wins. Speed doesn't. Is that what the data shows?" Maya nods. Then the teacher calls on a team that moved, but a different team that originally stood with Speed They explain the same pattern in different words. The teacher names the move: "What that team just did — claim a stat, check the data, revise — is exactly the structure of a Commissioner proposal. 'I believed X. The data showed Y. Therefore I'm proposing Z.'" The closing line: "Did anyone choose Budget?" Nobody. "Budget is a constraint, not a stat that produces wins. Keep that distinction in mind when you write your proposal — Budget is what you have to work inside of, not what you allocate to."

Exit ticket — the opening line of the Commissioner proposal. Ninety seconds. Every team member writes the same sentence on the back of their sticky note. Iris writes it on hers, over the crossed-out SPEED:

Patience's exit ticket — the opening line of the Commissioner proposal
"The stat that mattered most for our Season 1 results was Power because our data shows the three teams with the best win records (Kinetic Falcons 7−2−1, Cobalt Wolves 6−1−3, Steady Hawks 6−2−2) all allocated 7 or more points to Power, while the teams with the fewest wins (Iron Ravens 3−1−6, Magnetic Raptors 1−0−9) all allocated 4 or fewer. The Power threshold appears to sit between 4 and 7. Patience allocated 5 — right in the gap — and finished middle of the league."
Patience stood on Power at the start, and Patience stands on Power at the end. Nothing moved on Maya's end of the line. But Iris moved. Iris is the most quantitatively careful person on the team and she had the wrong sticky note for five minutes — not because she was sloppy, but because the equation she'd been using was the wrong tool to see the answer. That is the moment that stays with Maya, not her own confirmation. She watches Iris drop the marker on the desk and stare at the line. The equation can't see Power. But the league did. Maya writes that sentence into her journal next to her hypothesis card. Three days ago Cobalt's Analyst said something similar from outside Patience's team. Today Iris said the same thing from inside. The argument is no longer just Maya's. The proposal they write in the next block will have all three of their names on it. For the first time, the hypothesis is the team's, not just hers.
CHALLENGE

Proportional Scaling + Commissioner Proposal Prep

The teacher asks, "When you add 50% to a stat, does the bot's output go up by exactly 50%? More? Less? Hold your prediction. We're about to calculate the answer." Maya is asked to take their Season 1 Speed, add 50%, find the percent change in Motor Power. Then take their Season 1 Power, add 50%, find the percent change in Charge Speed. Patience's stats: S = 6, P = 5.. Maya writes them at the top of a page in her journal. Underneath, before any calculation, she writes her prediction in the margin in her own shorthand: "Speed = 50%. Power = more. P² grows faster."

The interactive lesson is open. Before calculations, Maya is asked to match terms with definitions. She starts with % Change. That one is easy. (new-old)/old x 100. The Quadratric is next. That's the POWER formula. Scale by 1.5 actually has the answer right there in the definition. That leaves Proportional. So, that must be Input grows 50% → Output grows 50%. Same rate.

May then enters Speed and Power. And Calculates Speed first.6 × 1.5 = 9. Motor Power old: 6 × 5 = 30%. New: 9 × 5 = 45%. Percent change: (45 − 30) ÷ 30 × 100 = 50%. Proportional. Speed scales 1-to-1 with Motor Power. Then Power. Maya squares 7.5 in her head and gets stuck — seven times seven is forty-nine, plus seven half-times, that's… She writes 7 × 7 = 49, then stares at the half. Devon, watching: "Just write it out. 7.5 times 7.5." She does the longhand: 56.25. Charge Speed old: 5² = 25%. New: 7.5² = 56.25%. Percent change: (56.25 − 25) ÷ 25 × 100 = 125%. Devon reads it aloud: "Hundred-twenty-five percent. We added fifty percent to Power and Charge Speed went up two-and-a-quarter times." Maya checks her margin prediction. Power = more. She underlines it. Then she writes underneath, slower: "(1.5)² = 2.25. So squaring the multiplier IS the multiplier?" She circles it with a question mark. That sentence is the thing she's been circling for ten days without naming. The teacher calls a Strategy A team to share — different team. The Mini-Summarize Bridge names what Maya already wrote in her margin: "Speed is linear. Power is quadratic. Same 50% input. Different output growth."

The team moves onto the Commissioner Proposal interactive lesson which explains how the system works. You can write a CAP rule or a FLOOR rule. Then they have to draft a rule.They pick a stat, a direction (CAP or FLOOR), and a number. Two formula computations to back it up, and name the last-place team and the effect the rule has on them. A fairness statement. They select POWER. Then Maya pulls up the league standings. Iron Ravens (3 − 1 − 6) and Magnetic Raptors (1 − 0 − 9) are at the bottom — and both have Power ≤ 4. The Human Number Line exit ticket they wrote earlier is sitting on the desk: "the Power threshold appears to sit between 4 and 7." Devon, looking at the threshold: "Floor it at 5. Lift the bottom teams to the bottom of the threshold." Iris: "Cap it at 7. Stop the top teams from pulling away." Maya: "A cap at 7 would block our own move to P = 7." Brief silence. Devon: "Floor." They click on Floor, and add their evidence. After reviewing their proposal, they transfer the rule to the Commissioner's worksheet.

Patience's Commissioner Proposal — draft (will be verified in PM Open Lab) 
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PATIENT FOXES #23  ·  Commissioner Proposal · Day 11 draft
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

1 · THE RULE  ·  POWER FLOOR
  Plain English: Every team must allocate at least 5 points to
  Power. Bots with Power below 5 have charge output below 25% —
  too low to drive the ball home after first contact. A floor
  at 5 puts every team at or above the chargePower threshold
  where Season 1 wins start to appear.

  Math notation:  P ≥ 5     Rule type: FLOOR

2 · DATA EVIDENCE  ·  Charge Speed = P²
  Data Point 1 — Iron Ravens #25 (current P = 3, worst record)
    Charge Speed old:  3² = 9%
    Under our rule:    5² = 25%
    Stat % change:    (5 − 3) ÷ 3 × 100 = 66.7%
    Output % change:  (25 − 9) ÷ 9 × 100 = 177.8%
    The floor nearly triples their charge output.

  Data Point 2 — Patience #23 (current P = 5)
    Charge Speed at P = 5:  25%
    Charge Speed at planned S2 P = 7:  49%
    Our planned allocation is above the floor. No change forced.

3 · EFFECT ON LAST-PLACE TEAM  ·  Magnetic Raptors #22 (1 − 0 − 9)
    Current Power: 4    Under our rule: must rise to 5
    Charge Speed old: 4² = 16%    New: 5² = 25%
    Stat % change:   (5 − 4) ÷ 4 × 100 = 25%
    Output % change: (25 − 16) ÷ 16 × 100 = 56.25%
    A 25% stat increase produces a 56% output increase because
    Charge Speed scales quadratically. The floor lifts the
    weakest team most — which is what a floor is for.

4 · FAIRNESS STATEMENT
    Does this rule benefit our own team?  ☐ Yes  ☒ No
    Our planned Season 2 Power = 7 is above the floor, so the
    rule does not change our allocation. The rule lifts teams
    below us. We proposed a rule that helps our rivals catch up
    and does not help us. That is what makes it fair.

                          — Devon, Iris, Maya · Patient Foxes #23
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Misconception correction — correlation vs. causation. "Some proposals cite evidence like this: high-Power teams win more. That's a real pattern. Does that mean Power CAUSES wins?" The room is silent. The teacher gives a second example to make the move visible — one that's not about bots. "Outside this room: in summer, ice cream sales go up. Shark attacks also go up. Does that mean buying ice cream causes shark attacks? Why might that be the case?" Tasha from Magnetic Raptors #22 offers "Both go up because there are more people on the beach?" The teacher picks up there, Exactly! The data shows two things rising together. It doesn't show one causing the other. That third thing — the beach — that's what statisticians call a confound. You can't see it in the ice cream column. You can't see it in the shark column. But it's what's actually driving both." Now Devon reads Patience's proposal back to himself and flags one phrase: "Power below 5 has charge output too low to compete." He squints. "That's almost a causal claim. We're saying low Power makes you lose." Maya looks at the draft. "Falcons have Power 7 AND Speed 4 AND Endurance 6. We can't tell which one made them win — we only see them together." Devon crosses out "too low to compete" and writes above it: "too low to match the chargePower pattern the league winners showed." The new wording describes a pattern instead of asserting a cause. The teacher names the distinction for the room: "Correlation is a pattern. Causation requires holding other variables constant. The rule can still be supported by correlation evidence — but the language has to match what the data can prove." Devon files the wording change. The proposal still stands. The argument got more careful.

The teacher takes two minutes at the front of the room and names what just happened in the order it happened. They calculated that scaling a stat by 50% doesn't always scale the output by 50% — the formula's exponent decides. They turned that calculation into evidence for a Commissioner proposal. That, she tells the room, is what a mathematical argument looks like: a formula, a computation, and a claim about what the result means for the league. Then the teacher says what's coming tomorrow. Commissioner Lobby. The vote. This afternoon's Open Lab is the last chance to check every number on the proposal against the actual dashboard. Maya is half-listening, half-reading the paper on her desk. Three names at the bottom. Devon. Iris. Hers. The order is whoever picked up the marker first. Devon picked it up first. Hers ended up last. She doesn't say anything about it. She slides the proposal next to yesterday's hypothesis card and puts them in a pile. Two papers. Same bot. Same week. Same argument.

The thing that stays with Maya isn't the calculation. It's that her margin prediction was right, and she didn't need the Day 4 insight to make it. The Day 4 insight has stopped being a thing she remembers and started being a thing she knows. The quadratic isn't a fact about Power anymore — it's a fact about how she thinks about Power. She also thinks about Devon catching the causal language. Devon would not have caught that on Day 8. He has been the team's Analyst for three days and the vocabulary of "pattern not cause" is now in his hand. Two members of Patience now speak the language that until last week was only Maya's — and Maya did not teach it to them; the data did, and the role rotation moved both of them into chairs where they had to use it. The proposal will go to a vote tomorrow. Maya does not know how the vote will go. She knows the argument is now hard to dismiss because it was built by three people, not one. That is the first time this week she has felt that her hypothesis is also her team's hypothesis, not just one she persuaded them into.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Underdog Mechanic Announced + Season 2 Stat Reallocation

The teacher walks to the front of the room and points at the Season 1 leaderboard still posted on the wall. "This is where every team finished. Finishing last is data — it tells us something about what happened, and it gives that team a specific mechanical advantage going into Season 2." Then she says the team's name. "Magnetic Raptors #22. You finished Season 1 in last place. That means you get 2 additional stat points right now, before Season 2 begins. You can change any stat by any amount, as long as all four add up to 22. This is the Underdog Mechanic. It's a permanent feature of this league. It's in effect until the end of camp." The room goes quiet. The Raptors' Analyst looks at the Raptors' Coder. Devon leans over from where he's sitting next to Maya. "They're the team our proposal was about." Maya nods. The team that wrote Patience's data swap finding three days ago is the team that just got the floor lifted from underneath them — by the league, not by the proposal Patient Foxes #23 was bringing to a vote tomorrow.

The teacher gives the room two jobs at once. Raptors get to use their 22-point budget to reallocate. Everyone else opens their morning's Commissioner proposal next to their Season 1 stat card and the interactive lesson. The question on the board: Does your own rule even apply to you? Maya pulls up the dashboard. Devon reads their rule out loud — Power has to be at least 5. Patience's Season 1 Power was 5. Their planned Season 2 Power is 7. Both pass the rule. Their own proposal doesn't push them anywhere they weren't already going.

Devon clicks through the lesson. The first screen asks him to pick a track — he picks Regular. The next is a Commissioner Rule explainer Patience already knows by heart (they wrote one this morning), and a timeline showing what's coming: today they think but don't commit, tomorrow they vote, Season 2 enforces what passes. He skims both. The lesson asks for Patience's Season 1 stats. Devon types them in: Speed 6, Endurance 4, Turning 5, Power 5. Sum 20. Green check. Then it asks for the Commissioner proposal. He copies the morning's rule into the box: every team must have Power of at least 5. Click forward.

Four strategy questions on the screen. Step 1: does your loadout satisfy your rule? Devon checks — Patience's Power is 5, the rule says ≥ 5, that passes. He clicks Yes. The lesson confirms: legal under your own rule. Step 2: does the rule help you strategically? Devon stares at the screen for a second. Then he opens the worksheet next to him and writes a note in Section 2 — Maya watches him work it out as he writes. The rule pushes Iron Ravens, because Iron Ravens has Power 3 and no Underdog bonus — they'd have to take 2 points off another stat to climb up to 5. But it doesn't push Raptors anymore. Raptors has 22 points and is probably going to put some of them in Power on their own. The proposal was written about Raptors. The proposal is going to mostly affect Iron Ravens. Maya re-reads Devon's note. She didn't see that this morning. She gets it now. Devon clicks Yes on the lesson — the rule does help Patience, but not for the reason they thought when they wrote it.

Step 3 asks which stat Patience would consider changing. Devon clicks Power. Step 4 asks for a Season 2 direction note. He types one sentence — our planned S2 Power is already 7, so the rule doesn't force us to rebuild — it forces Iron Ravens to climb up to where we already are — and clicks Lock In. The lesson shows a strategy summary on his screen: the proposal, the constraint check, the stat to watch, the direction note.

While Devon was working on the screen, Iris was copying their reallocation onto the worksheet from yesterday's draft. Speed goes from 6 to 4. Power goes from 5 to 7. Endurance and Turning don't move. The total still adds to 20. She works through the formula outputs — Motor Power 20%, Charge Speed 49% — and files them for the exit ticket. While she's writing, Maya looks across the room. Raptors is working at their own table. From her seat she can see Raptors' Power column go from 4 to 6. Not 5 — six. The floor Patience proposed sits at 5, and Raptors went straight past it. They didn't need the rule to push them up. They were going to land above it anyway.

The teacher invites Raptors to read their new allocation to the room. Their Coder reads it off: Speed 4, Endurance 6, Turning 6, Power 6. Sum 22. The class checks the math. Two points went into Power, and the bonus is accounted for. Raptors' Analyst adds something Maya didn't expect — their Charge Speed went from 16 to 36. A 125% increase. Same percentage as this morning's calculation. The teacher writes it on the board: a 50% bump in Power produced a 125% bump in Charge Speed. The quadratic from this morning, showing up in a real team's reallocation.

Then the teacher asks the room a question. Raise your hand if your Season 1 allocation already passes your own proposal's rule. Patience raises. Most teams raise. Kinetic Falcons doesn't. Their rule was a Speed floor at 5, and their Speed is 4. The teacher asks them why. Falcons' Analyst: "We wrote it last and we were hurrying. The floor would force our own team to raise Speed by 1." The teacher names the move: writing a rule that constrains other teams but also constrains your own. Engineers do the same check when they design something. Does our own design pass our own requirements? Falcons writes it down. Maya does too.

Exit ticket. Three minutes. Section 3 of the worksheet — fill in the blanks for the Performance Hypothesis using Patience's new numbers:

Patience's Performance Hypothesis — Section 3 of the Season 2 Loadout Capture 
SECTION 3 — PERFORMANCE HYPOTHESIS

  With Speed = 4 and Power = 7, my Motor Power will be
  4 × 5 = 20% and my Charge Speed will be 7² = 49%.

  I expect to be stronger at first-contact kicks against
  high-Power opponents (chargePower 49 matches Falcons'
  and Hawks' 49), and weaker at outrunning opponents
  to the ball (Motor Power dropped from 30% to 20% —
  −33% change).

  In Season 2 I will win by getting in front of the ball
  on contact and not losing the chargePower exchange when
  I do. The cost is I will have to defend more, because
  fast opponents will reach the ball first.

                  — Devon, Iris, Maya · Patient Foxes #23
Two things from this block stay with Maya. Neither is what she expected when she walked in.

The first is Devon's note. The proposal Patience wrote was about Raptors. Devon noticed it was actually going to affect Iron Ravens. Maya read his note and got it on the second pass — Raptors got a bonus, so they don't need the rule to push them up. Iron Ravens didn't get a bonus, so the rule pushes them. The thing Patience wrote three hours ago wasn't aimed at the team it's going to land on. Maya didn't see that this morning. Devon did.

The second is Raptors going to Power 6. The proposal said the rule would push them to 5. Raptors went past 5 to 6 on their own. They didn't need the rule. They saw the same thing Patience saw and made the same call without anyone telling them to. Maya doesn't say anything about it. She files it.

Tomorrow they vote. Then they play. The Performance Hypothesis is on the table with three names on it. The Motor Power dropped 33%. The Charge Speed went up 96%. Both numbers are there in the same sentence — the cost and the gain in their own handwriting. Not a wish. A hypothesis.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Practice Matches: Test New Loadout Hypotheses

Bots are reprogrammed, the field is open, and the teacher gives them fifty-five minutes. Her rule, said twice: write the hypothesis first, or the run doesn't count. Before any team plays, someone reads the hypothesis out loud. If they can't say it, they're not running. Every desk has a worksheet with the Commissioner proposal stat circled at the top. Patience's circled stat is Power. Iris loads the .mkcd draft from yesterday onto the bot — Speed 4, Endurance 4, Turning 5, Power 7. The bot isn't a Season 1 bot anymore. It's the hypothesis.

Run 1. Devon writes the hypothesis on the worksheet before they walk to the field. Their guess: move Power from 5 to 7 (and lose 2 Speed) and Patience will finally score against high-Power opponents, because chargePower jumps from 25 to 49 — same as Falcons. Their evidence: in Season 1 they beat 4 out of 5 low-chargePower teams, and they scored exactly 1 goal across 6 matches against high-chargePower teams. The teacher reads it at their table. "Specific. You can run." Iris places the ball at center. They press A, then B, B, B to calibrate, then A to launch. Maya starts the timer on her phone. The bot runs its launch sequence — turn, drive, turn — and the front headlights flash white when its sonar locks the ball at 40 centimeters. Time to ball: 5.1 seconds. The bot aligns on the ball and pushes forward at chargePower 49 — both wheels driving at 49% of max, follow-through 365 ms. The ball rolls clean across the field and over the far goal line. Goal. The bot keeps running. It re-acquires the ball mid-field, pushes again. Goal. Three minutes in, the bot has scored twice. Every push at chargePower 49 reached the goal line. In Season 1 at chargePower 25, these pushes would have stopped short — that's the comparison they came here to make. Maya writes the result on the worksheet: "Time to ball 5.1 sec. Two solo goals in 3 minutes. Every push at chargePower 49 reached the goal line. ChargePower 49 was enough to drive the ball home." Devon circles Improved on the Conclusion line. Under "Supports Commissioner proposal?" he circles Yes.

Run 2. Maya pulls her hypothesis worksheet out. The fallback line she wrote at the end of Day 9: if after Season 2 Round 1 our actual goals don't go up, look at Turning next — not back at Power. She reads it aloud. Iris: "We have a practice run right here. We don't have to wait for the real R1 to test it." Iris reprograms — Speed 4, Endurance 4, Turning 7, Power 5. Turning instead of Power. Same total. Devon writes the new hypothesis: if they trade Turning for Power, they align on the ball a little faster but get no chargePower improvement. He predicts every push will fall short of the goal line because chargePower stays at 25. Evidence: every Season 1 match against a Falcons-level team, they scored zero — and chargePower 25 is why. Iris places the ball at center. They press the buttons again. Time to ball: 5.0 seconds — Speed is the same as Run 1, so the launch and the cruise take the same time. The headlights flash slightly sooner than Run 1; the extra Turning sharpens the alignment step by a hair. The bot pushes the ball forward at chargePower 25 — wheels at 25% of max, follow-through 295 ms (almost a second shorter than Run 1). The ball rolls maybe halfway across the field. Stops. The bot loses contact, re-acquires the ball, pushes again. Stops short again. Three minutes in, the bot has scored zero times. Every push at chargePower 25 stopped short of the goal line — same as every Season 1 match against a Falcons-class opponent. Maya writes the result: "Time to ball 5.0 sec. Zero solo goals in 3 minutes. Every push at chargePower 25 stopped short. Power matters more than Turning — chargePower is the variable." Devon circles Worse on the Conclusion line. Under "Supports Commissioner proposal?" he circles Yes — Run 2 confirms what Run 1 alone couldn't.

Two solo runs, three minutes each. Two goals in Run 1; zero in Run 2. Devon writes the comparison: same Speed in both runs, so time-to-ball was nearly identical (5.1 vs 5.0). The thing that changed was chargePower at the push — 49 versus 25 — and that's what determined whether the ball reached the goal line at all. Power was the right call. Turning is not the move. Maya re-reads what Devon wrote. This is the first time her hypothesis has been tested against the question she planted at the end of Day 9 — what if Turning matters more? — and survived.
