The teacher calls one team to read their best conclusion sentence aloud. She picks a different team — Magnetic Raptors. Raptors' Analyst reads: "With +2 Underdog budget allocated to Power, our chargePower went from 16 to 36. In solo testing, the push reached the goal in 3 of 4 trials. At chargePower 16 it stopped short every time. Hypothesis confirmed: the Underdog Mechanic gave us a real mechanical advantage, not a cosmetic one." The teacher echoes the claim back: "So you're saying — the 2 bonus points actually changed the math, not just the budget. Is that what the data shows?" Raptors confirms. The closing line: "A hypothesis that was not confirmed is not a failure. It is evidence that your proposal needs to be more specific. That is more useful than a convenient hypothesis that was never tested." Patience's hypothesis was confirmed. Twice. Maya files the worksheet next to the morning's proposal. Five papers now, not four.

For three days the Power hypothesis sat in writing in three different documents and Maya was carrying it on faith — Cobalt's outside reading, Iris's Day 11 morning move, Raptors going past the floor. Today the bot tested it for the first time. Run 1 at chargePower 49: three solo goals. Run 2 at chargePower 25: zero. Three goals Patience would not have scored at the old allocation, and zero confirms it was the Power, not the Turning. The Day 9 contingency is closed. "If actual goals don't go up, look at Turning" — well, actual goals went from zero to three, and the Turning test scored zero at the equivalent push. Turning is not the move. Maya does not cross out the fallback line in her notebook. She draws one careful line under it and writes: "Answered Run 2 — solo push at chargePower 25 stopped short. Power, not Turning." The hypothesis card is now closed on three sides: cross-team validated (Cobalt, Raptors), team-internal validated (Iris), bot-tested (today's two runs). The only thing left open is whether the proposal passes tomorrow. She is not worried about that anymore. Even if the proposal fails the vote, the data she has now is hers, and the bot will keep its new allocation regardless of the league rule.
OPEN LAB

Commissioner Proposal Refinement

Verification Standard. Two checks go up on the board. Check 1: are both cited numbers accurate against the actual dashboard? Check 2: does your rule actually help the last-place team — percent change, not "it helps them." The teacher's launch line: "Open your proposal, open your dashboard. Go." Maya opens the dashboard. Devon takes the proposal sheet and the Section 4 worksheet. Iris pulls up the bot's loaded .mkcd as a sanity-check. Nobody discusses the split — it happens in three seconds.

Check 1. Maya works the cited numbers row by row. Falcons P = 7. Confirmed. Cobalt P = 9. Confirmed. Hawks P = 7. Confirmed. Iron Ravens P = 3. Confirmed. Magnetic Raptors P = 4 … The dashboard says P = 6. Underdog reallocation, three hours ago. Her stomach drops. "Our proposal cites Raptors at P = 4. That was true this morning. It is not true now." Devon looks up. Their Section 3 calculation uses a number that doesn't exist in the dashboard anymore.

Check 2. Devon runs the percent change on Raptors anyway, knowing it'll be wrong. Stat change forced by the rule = 0%. Output change = 0%. The proposal fails Check 2 as written. Maya, eyes still on the dashboard: "Iron Ravens. P = 3. No Underdog bonus. They're the team this actually binds." Devon re-runs the math. Stat change 66.7%. Output change 177.8%. The revision helps Iron Ravens almost twice as much as the original would have helped Raptors. Devon writes the parenthetical into Section 4: "Magnetic Raptors finished last in Season 1 but no longer constrained after Underdog reallocation. Iron Ravens, P = 3, is the team the rule binds."

Patience's Commissioner Proposal — verification revision

The teacher picks Steady Hawks to share — clean Endurance numbers, no Underdog complication. Hawks reads their decay-rate math. The Commissioner's Meeting tomorrow will require it. You don't argue for your proposal. You show the math." Maya is listening. She is not called on. Patience's proposal needed a revision; the revision made it stronger; the teacher knows it.

Two-minute close. "Some numbers held. Some needed adjusting. That's verification — it's what separates a credible proposal from a strong opinion." Preview for tomorrow: Lobby (10 min), revise (15 min), formal meeting. "Know your two numbers cold." Maya knows hers: Iron Ravens 177.8% chargePower increase. Patience 0% change. The conflict-of-interest math passes by the largest margin in the room.

Their proposal was almost wrong by the end of the morning. Three hours after they wrote it, the Underdog Mechanic moved their named team off the floor. If they had walked into the Meeting tomorrow with the original numbers, an examiner could have pulled up the dashboard and broken the case study cold. The verification didn't break the proposal — it made it sharper. Iron Ravens at 177.8% is more dramatic than Raptors at 56% would have been, and the conflict-of-interest math is overwhelming. Maya caught the staleness. Devon did the math. Iris had the bot's live allocation as a cross-check. None of the three of them needed a teacher to tell them what to do. Six papers now, in the same handwriting, about the same bot. Tomorrow she finds out if the league agrees with her data, and Patience plays its first official Season 2 match either way.
DAY 12

Commissioner Lobby · Season 2 Begins · The Question Closes

Thursday · Week 3 · Season 2 first match · SKETCH
Maya's arc today: Patience's POWER FLOOR proposal goes through the morning Lobby and verification block, the Commissioner vote at lunch, and the post-vote allocation verification — the moment the math she's been doing all week gets a name: system of inequalities. The bot — already reallocated yesterday to S=4, P=7 — plays its first Season 2 match in the afternoon. Maya tries to compute the Patience-specific slope she filed on Day 8 using the new data point, and discovers she can't, because two variables changed at once. The question closes with the answer "you can't get one yet."
Where Maya is nowDays 8–11 ended with a question Maya couldn't answer: what would a Patience-specific equation look like? Today she finally has the second data point she needed. She tries to use it. It doesn't work. The reason it doesn't work is the next thing she learns.
HOOK

Lobby the Commissioners

Lobby Rules are up o the wall before Maya walks in the classroom in the morning Maya reads them with her team: conversation not presentation, talk to at least two teams, feedback is optional to use, prepare for objections. At start time: "Two sentences. Show them your number. Ask what they think. Timer starts now. Go." Maya picks up Patience's worksheet. Iris goes with her — there's no coding to do this block, so she's the second voice if a team asks a question Maya doesn't have an answer ready for. Devon stays at the table to field teams that come to Patience.

First stop: Iron Ravens. Maya reads them their own data. "Our rule forces P ≥ 5. You're at P = 3 with no Underdog bonus. That's a 177.8% chargePower increase for you." Iron Ravens reads back: "That's our math. We're voting yes." Two-second exchange. Maya moves.

Second stop: Steady Hawks. Endurance floor at E ≥ 4. "Decay rate drops from 27% to 24% per minute for the worst-Endurance team — 11% improvement in retained power at minute 1." Clean numbers. Maya nods. Strong proposal. Moves on.

Meanwhile at Patience's table. While Maya and Iris are out, two teams come by. Steady Hawks checks whether the POWER FLOOR conflicts with their ENDURANCE FLOOR — Devon answers in three words: "Different stats. No conflict." Steady Hawks nods and moves on. Magnetic Raptors comes over a minute later — Patience's framing (name the team the rule helps, calculate the percent change for that team) is the same move Raptors used in their Underdog extension proposal. Devon makes the connection: "We both named the team the rule helps. That's why." Raptors writes it down. Two visits Devon handles solo while Maya and Iris are at Cobalt's table.

Third stop: Cobalt Wolves. Their proposal: a Power cap at 7. Maya stops. Cobalt has P = 9 — the cap forces them down to 7. It would also leave Iron Ravens at P = 3 with no fix. That's a rule that doesn't help the team in last place. Neither Maya nor Iris says it out loud. They walk back to their table with five minutes still on the Lobby clock. "Cobalt's proposal fails Check 2," Maya says quietly to Devon. Iris adds: "They're not naming Iron Ravens. They're capping themselves down to Falcons-level." Devon writes flag for objection in the worksheet's margin.

Finalize. Patient Foxes #23 verified yesterday. Maya rechecks Iron Ravens against the dashboard one more time — P = 3 still. Confirmed. Devon doesn't rewrite anything; he adds one line at the bottom of the worksheet: "If Cobalt's Power-7 cap passes, it leaves Iron Ravens at P = 3 — our rule still applies and is still the only one that helps them." The proposal is locked in three minutes. The remaining eight minutes Maya spends prepping the objection math for Cobalt's rule: percent change Cobalt's cap would force on Patience. Patience is already at P = 7. Cap at 7 = 0% change. Patient Foxes #23 has nothing to lose by opposing it.

Exit check. The teacher calls teams in order. "One phrase: what math concept does your proposal use?" Maya knows Devon is about to say "proportional scaling" — that was Day 11 morning. She catches his eye and shakes her head once. Devon pauses. "Ratio," he says. "Percent change ratio. 177.8% versus 0%." The teacher nods. Patience's concept label is locked: ratio.

Patience walked into the Lobby with a verified proposal. They walked out with two new things to track. The first is easy: Cobalt's Power cap is a competing rule, but the math against it writes itself. Maya has the two sentences ready: "Patience is at P = 7 — Cobalt's cap doesn't move us. 0% change." "Iron Ravens is at P = 3 — Cobalt's cap doesn't move them either. The rule has no last-place beneficiary. It fails Check 2." The bigger thing was the math-concept catch on the exit check. Devon was about to say "proportional scaling" — that was Day 11 morning's lesson, and it's the label most teams will pick today. But Patience's math doesn't fit that label. Proportional scaling means a constant multiplier: double the input, double the output. Power isn't like that — chargePower is P squared, so doubling Power from 3 to 6 quadruples chargePower from 9 to 36. Not double. Quadruple. And the argument Patience is making — 177.8% for Iron Ravens versus 0% for Patience — is a ratio of two percent changes, not a proportional scale. Maya wrote quadratic ≠ proportional in her data log. She doesn't fully know what to do with the distinction yet, but it felt important to name.
CHALLENGE

Final Proposal Verification

Verification worksheet. 35 minutes. Four sections. The teacher's launch: "Check your two numbers against the dashboard, calculate how your rule helps the last-place team, confirm there's no conflict of interest, name the math concept. Before lunch I'll ask one person per team to explain one calculation from memory. Go." Patience splits the work the same way as yesterday: Maya on the dashboard, Devon on the math, Iris on the bot's .mkcd as a sanity-check.

Section 1 — data points. Maya looks at the cited numbers row by row. Iron Ravens P = 3. Confirmed. Magnetic Raptors P = 6 (post-Underdog). Confirmed. Patience S = 4, P = 7 (loaded yesterday). Confirmed. All three numbers still match the dashboard a full day after the proposal was locked.

Section 2 — percent change. Devon re-runs the Iron Ravens math without looking at yesterday's work — stat change (5 − 3) ÷ 3 × 100 = 66.7%; output change (25 − 9) ÷ 9 × 100 = 177.8%. Same as yesterday. Devon also runs the calculation for Patience's own team under the rule: P = 7 is already above the floor, so the stat change forced by the rule = 0%. Section 3 — conflict-of-interest check. 177.8% for Iron Ravens versus 0% for Patience. The rule helps the constrained team infinitely more than it helps Patience. COI passes by the largest margin Maya has seen in the room.

Section 4 — concept naming. Devon's pen hovers over proportional scaling. Maya: "Chargepower is P squared. That's quadratic — not proportional. Our math is a ratio comparison: 177.8 to 0." Devon circles ratio instead. Underneath he writes the one-sentence definition off the worksheet: "a part-to-part relationship between two quantities." Patience's two percent changes — 177.8% and 0% — are that ratio.

Accountability check. The teacher points at Maya. "How did you calculate the 177.8%?" Maya, without looking at the worksheet: "Old chargePower was 9 — that's 3 squared. New chargePower is 25 — that's 5 squared. 25 minus 9 is 16. 16 divided by 9, times 100, is 177.8 percent." The teacher nods. "And what concept type?" "Ratio," Maya says. "Not proportional scaling — Power is quadratic." The teacher writes Patience's name on the verified list.

Yesterday's Open Lab did most of the work. Today was confirmation. The 0% / 177.8% conflict-of-interest math still passes by the largest margin in the room — and Maya can say it from memory now, including the squared step. The accountability check was the easiest math she has done in front of the teacher all camp. Six papers in the same handwriting, all in front of her. The Commissioner's Meeting is right after lunch. Patience is locked.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Commissioner's Meeting: Formal Presentations + Vote

The room is set up: a blank section of wall, permanent markers on the front table, a Vote Record sheet on the teacher's clipboard. The teacher's opening line: "Each team presents for exactly 3 minutes. Two data points. The rule. The effect on the last-place team. After each presentation, the whole room verifies the math before we vote. Mathematical objections only — name the number and the calculation." Iris distributes the Math Verification Sheet to every team. One per team. Two data points per presenter. Run the percent change yourself. Vote yes or no with a one-sentence reason. Patience presents fifth in the order.

Presentations 1–4. Maya works through the Math Verification Sheet as each team presents. Presentation 1 — Steady Hawks: Endurance floor at 4. Their math: decay rate drops from 27% to 24% per minute — Maya runs (24 − 27) ÷ 27 × 100 = −11.1%. Math checks. Vote yes. Presentation 2 — Magnetic Raptors: extend the Underdog bonus to next-to-last place. Math checks. Vote yes. Presentation 3 — Kinetic Falcons: Turning floor at 4. Math checks but the rule helps no team in the bottom three. Maya votes no. Presentation 4 — Iron Ravens: Speed floor at 4. Maya catches a math error on the percent change — Iron Ravens wrote (4−3)÷4 instead of (4−3)÷3. *Divided by the new value, not the old.* When the teacher asks for verification, Maya names the error. Iron Ravens corrects, the corrected math still supports their argument, the vote proceeds. Vote: fails 1–4. At this point, two passed rules in marker: ENDURANCE FLOOR 4 and UNDERDOG BONUS EXTENDS TO 5TH PLACE.

Presentation 5 — Patient Foxes #23. Maya stands. She has the worksheet in front of her and her two numbers memorized. "Our rule: POWER FLOOR at 5. Two data points. Iron Ravens current Power: 3. Patience current Power: 7. Under our rule, Iron Ravens' Power goes from 3 to 5 — stat change (5 − 3) ÷ 3 × 100 = 66.7%. Output change: chargePower = P², so 9 to 25 — that's (25 − 9) ÷ 9 × 100 = 177.8%. Patience is already above the floor, so our rule changes Patience by 0%. The rule helps Iron Ravens 177.8% in chargePower output and helps Patience 0%. Math concept: ratio — not proportional scaling, because chargePower is quadratic." She sits down. The teacher: "Does anyone verify the math? Can the 177.8% be confirmed?" Magnetic Raptors raises a hand. "Confirmed. 25 minus 9 is 16. 16 divided by 9 is 1.778. Times 100 is 177.8." The vote opens. Five non-proposing teams. 5 yes, 0 no. Rule passes. The teacher walks to the wall and writes in permanent marker: POWER FLOOR — every team must allocate at least 5 points to Power. The objection window opens. No team is hit at 10 percentage points above median. No objections filed. The rule stands.

Presentation 6 — Cobalt Wolves. Their rule: Power cap at 7. Cobalt's spokesperson presents the math: Cobalt at P = 9, cap at 7, stat change −22.2%. "Levels the playing field for non-Power-dominant teams." The teacher: "Effect on the last-place team — Iron Ravens?" The Cobalt spokesperson pauses. "Iron Ravens is at P = 3. Our rule doesn't change them." The room hears it. Math verification opens — the percent change is correct. The vote opens. 1 yes, 4 no. Rule fails. The teacher: "Not enough votes. No rule written. Next." Maya looks at the objection math she prepped during yesterday morning's Lobby — Patience at P = 7, cap at 7, 0% change for Patience. She doesn't need it. The room's math-vote did the work.

Rule Consolidation. The teacher reads every rule on the wall back to the room. "Three rules: ENDURANCE FLOOR 4. UNDERDOG BONUS EXTENDS TO 5TH PLACE. POWER FLOOR 5." Then: "Every team revises their Season 2 allocation to satisfy every rule. Total must equal 20 — or 22 for the Underdog team. No one leaves until your revised sheet adds up and follows every rule." Maya checks Patience's current allocation: Speed 4, Endurance 4, Turning 5, Power 7. Power floor 5? P = 7 ✓. Endurance floor 4? E = 4 ✓. Total 20? ✓. Patience already satisfies all three rules. Iris pulls up the bot's loaded .mkcd — it matches. Sign-off in two minutes.

Iron Ravens at the next table. Their current allocation puts them at P = 3. Under the new POWER FLOOR 5 rule, they have to move 2 points into Power from somewhere. Devon walks over to verify their work. They land at Speed 5, Endurance 5, Turning 5, Power 5. Total 20, all three rules satisfied. They figured out the constraint satisfaction problem on their own. Devon initials their sheet as a neighbor-verifier.

Exit Ticket. Two problems, five minutes. Question 1: A rule passes that caps Speed at a maximum of 6. Your current Speed is 9. Calculate the percent change in your Speed. Maya writes: (6 − 9) ÷ 9 × 100 = −33.3%. Question 2: Write a valid Season 2 allocation that satisfies every rule written on the wall today. Maya writes Patience's current loadout: S = 4, E = 4, T = 5, P = 7. Below it she shows each rule satisfied: Power floor 5 ✓ (P = 7). Endurance floor 4 ✓ (E = 4). Total 20 ✓. She hands the sheet in. The teacher initials it.

This morning Patience walked into verification with a proposal that was already solid. During the meeting the room voted on it and wrote it on the wall in permanent marker. Maya saw her own math become a Season 2 rule in real time. The objection she prepped for Cobalt's Power cap didn't get used — but watching the room kill it 4-to-1 was its own lesson. Math arguments don't always need a hero. Sometimes the room runs the percent change and votes. The exit ticket math came easy — both answers were numbers Patience had been carrying for three days. She handed the sheet in without re-checking. Three rules on the wall in permanent marker. Patience's name is on one of them. The bot is already configured for tomorrow's Season 2 first match. She is more ready than she expected to be.
OPEN LAB

Season 2 Allocation Verification

The teacher points at the three steps on the board: Step 1 — verify your loadout in the What-If tool. Step 2 — program it into MakeCode. Step 3 — one test drive. "35 minutes. No one leaves until all three are done. Go." Maya, Devon, and Iris open the What-If tool on Maya's screen. The Commissioner rules from the wall are already loaded as constraints.

Step 1 — Verify. Maya enters Patience's allocation: S = 4, E = 4, T = 5, P = 7. The three rule boxes turn green one after the other. Power floor 5 — green. Endurance floor 4 — green. Total 20 — green. No red anywhere. "All green," Maya says. "Total 20. No blanks." The teacher walks past, glances at the screen, nods. "Step 2." Patience took two minutes. They look up. Iron Ravens is still in Step 1 at the next table — they have to find a new allocation that satisfies the POWER FLOOR 5 rule. Maya watches Devon walk over to coach them through it.

Step 2 — Program. Maya pulls up MakeCode on the bot's loaded file. The .mkcd from yesterday is already at S = 4, E = 4, T = 5, P = 7. She reads each value out loud as Iris checks them against the What-If tool. "Speed four. Endurance four. Turning five. Power seven." Match. Maya saves and re-loads to the bot to be safe. The teacher's pass-through: "Show me one stat in the code." Iris points at POWER = 7. The teacher initials the monitoring chart.

Step 3 — Test drive. One run. Iris picks up the bot, calibrates the bot, and places it at the corner of the field, then presses A. The teacher walks over as the launch sequence starts. "What should look different from Season 1, based on your new loadout?" Maya, watching the bot turn-drive-turn: "Slower to the ball — Speed dropped from 6 to 4, so motor power is at 20% instead of 30%. Stronger push — chargePower is 49 now, not 25. The launch sequence might overshoot slightly because we calibrated it at Speed 6." The bot's headlights flash white at the ball. Time to ball: 5.2 seconds. The push at chargePower 49 drives the ball clean across to the far goal. The bot does what Maya said it would. Devon writes the one line in the data log: "Run 1, Season 2 config. Time to ball 5.2 sec — Speed 4 cost us about 1.5 sec versus Season 1's Speed 6. Push at chargePower 49 reached the goal. Launch geometry held — no overshoot at the reduced Speed."

Wrap mini-share. The teacher calls Iron Ravens to share their trade-off. They had to move 2 points into Power. "We took 1 from Speed and 1 from Turning. Power went from 3 to 5." The teacher: "So meeting the rule meant taking points off two other stats." Iron Ravens: "Right." Magnetic Raptors restates it. The teacher names the practice: "Checking that every rule holds at the same time, not one at a time, is called checking a system for consistency. That is the same thing you do when you solve a system of inequalities in algebra. You have done it three times this week — Day 5 budget constraint, Day 11 proposal verification, today." Maya looks up. The thing she has been doing all week has a name.

Exit ticket. Two questions, two minutes. Question 1: Write the rule that was hardest to meet as an inequality. Maya writes E ≥ 4 with a note: "Hardest because Patience is exactly at E = 4 — no room to move points out of Endurance into Power without breaking it." Question 2: Does your final loadout meet every rule? Circle YES / NO. Maya circles YES. She hands the sheet in.

The verification was easy because Patience's allocation already worked — the morning's proposal-writing forced them to think in constraints. What Maya wasn't expecting was the teacher saying "system of inequalities" out loud. She has been doing this math since Day 5, when the room first wrote down S + E + T + P = 20. Today there are three more constraints stacked on top of that one, and the bot is loaded under all of them. "System of inequalities" goes in her data log with a date next to it. Tomorrow her bot plays under those constraints for real. She doesn't know how it will go. What she does know is that the math she's holding is the same math she'll use to solve for variables in algebra next year. The teacher just told her so.
OPEN LAB

The Day 8 Question Closes — SKETCH

(This is a sketch. Full scene goes here when Day 12 gets built out.)

After Patience's first Season 2 match, Maya opens her data log to the page she marked on Day 8: "Day 11 — change Speed, take a new data point, see if the equation gets sharper." She has the new data point. Speed = 4, Goals scored = 3. She writes it underneath the five Season 1 rows.

She tries to compute the Patience-specific slope. Two points: (6, 1) and (4, 3). m = (3 − 1) ÷ (4 − 6) = 2 ÷ (−2) = −1. Negative slope. She stops. The class equation has a positive slope. "That can't be right," she says out loud.

Iris looks over her shoulder. "We changed Power too. That's not just a Speed change." Maya stares at her data log for a second, then nods slowly. "Oh. Right." She crosses out the −1 with one careful line and writes underneath: "Can't fit a slope from these two points. Power changed too. Need to change ONE thing at a time."

The teacher walks by. Maya tells her what she just figured out. The teacher writes it on the board for the room: "If you change two variables at once, you can't tell which one made the difference. That's why scientists run controlled experiments." Maya copies the sentence into her data log next to the crossed-out −1.

The question Maya filed on Day 8 has an answer. It's not the answer she wanted. She wanted a Patience-specific equation. What she got was a reason she can't have one yet — Patience would have to keep Power constant and change only Speed, and they don't have the budget for that. The closure is honest, not triumphant. Maya writes it down anyway. Three weeks ago she would have crumpled the page. Today she keeps it.
DAY 13

Season 2 Opens · Two Predictions, One Equation

Week 4 · Season 2 + Exhibition
Maya's arc today: As Fabricator she gets Patience ready for its first Season 2 match. The team predicts their win count twice — once from gut + Season 1 win rate on a Prediction Market card, once from a class-wide linear equation Iris builds against six teams of data. Both methods point to the same number: 5 wins of 8. Iris's residual analysis says Patience over-performed Power-alone in Season 1 by 17 percentage points. Maya watches the math land on her bot's allocation — the residual is the first hint that the Power upgrade story she's been carrying for three weeks may not be the only thing driving Patience's wins. The math is layering up.
Where Maya is nowSeason 1 closed at 5 − 1 − 4 across 10 matches — 50% win rate. The bot is loaded at S = 4, E = 4, T = 5, P = 7 under the new POWER FLOOR 5 and ENDURANCE FLOOR 4 rules. New week, new role: Maya is the Fabricator — bot allocation, programming, physical bot setup. Devon takes the dashboard. Iris runs the math.
HOOK

The Prediction Market

The teacher writes on the board: 8 matches in Season 2. How many will you win? Index cards go around. The rule is read once: "One number — not a range. Base it on something real from Season 1. You defend it out loud." Then: "Silent writing. Three minutes. Cards stay down." Iris — Analyst this week — picks up the pen for Patience.

Quiet writing. Iris writes Patience's Season 1 record on the card in pencil: 5 − 1 − 4 across 10 matches. Win rate 5 ÷ 10 = 50%. Eight Season 2 matches × 50% = 4 wins as a baseline. But Patience moved Power from 5 to 7 — Maya's whole hypothesis was that this would let them score against high-Power opponents. In Season 1 they went 0-for-4 against teams with chargePower ≥ 49. *If even one of those losses flips to a win, that's +1.* Iris writes the rough math under the prediction. Devon, leaning over the table: "Power floor rule helps Iron Ravens, too. They're at chargePower 9 — almost where we used to be. We'll probably still beat them." Iris circles 5 as her final number and writes underneath: Season 1 win rate 50% + 1 win from Power 7 against Falcons-class opponents. Iris nods. That's the defense.

Post all at once. The teacher counts. "One. Two. Three." Every team holds their card up at the same moment. Maya turns to read the wall. Six cards. The room is quiet for thirty seconds while everyone scans. Iron Ravens: 2. Magnetic Raptors: 5. Steady Hawks: 6. Kinetic Falcons: 8. Cobalt Wolves: 6. Patient Foxes: 5. Falcons predicted 8 — undefeated in Season 1, doubling down. Iron Ravens predicted 2 — half their Season 1 win count, even though Patience's POWER FLOOR rule was supposed to help them. They're being honest about Power 5 still being a Power gap against Falcons-class teams.

Go around. The teacher calls Patient Foxes fourth in the order. "What Season 1 number led to that prediction? One sentence." Iris stands. "Our Season 1 win rate was 5 out of 10, so 50% — baseline 4 wins on 8 matches. Plus 1 win expected from the Power 7 upgrade against Falcons-class opponents, where we went 0 for 4 in Season 1. So 5." The teacher echoes the structure back: "Specific Season 1 win rate plus an adjustment for a stat change you can name." Iris nods and sits. Twenty seconds of defense.

Name it. Two more teams defend. After Magnetic Raptors cites their win rate cleanly, the teacher names the move for the room. "What Raptors just did — taking a real Season 1 number and using it to predict — is called quantitative reasoning. The equation you build in the next block does the same thing, more precisely." Maya writes quantitative reasoning in her journal. The cards stay on the wall for the rest of the week.

The number on the wall is 5. Maya picked it. Devon agreed. Iris agreed. Some part of Maya wants the number to be higher — she believes the Power upgrade is the right call and 5 is what safe looks like. But she defended 5 with a calculation and a hypothesis, and the teacher named the move out loud. The card stays up all week. By Day 15 she'll know whether 5 was the right number.
CHALLENGE

Building and Solving Linear Equations

The teacher posts the class data table on the board — every team's stat values and Season 1 Win%. "x is a bot's stat value. y is that bot's Win%. You'll draw a line that fits the pattern across bots in this class. Two bots set the line — Build Bots A and B. A third bot — Test Bot C — is your team. You'll use the equation to predict your own Win%, then compare to your actual." Iris opens the interactive lesson on her screen. "Why not test on Build Bot A or B? Tell your partner." Maya, to Devon: "Because the answer is already baked in. They built the line — of course they fit it." Devon nods.

Pick the stat. "Before you pick bots, check the spread. Which stat has the widest variation across the class?" Maya scans the table. Speed ranges 3 to 7 (spread of 4). Endurance 2 to 7 (spread of 5). Turning 2 to 7 (spread of 5). Power 3 to 9 (spread of 6). Power. *And it's the stat we changed. Two reasons to pick it.* Maya circles Power.

Build Bots A and B. Maya picks the two extremes: Build Bot A — Iron Ravens (P = 3, Win% = 10). Build Bot B — Cobalt Wolves (P = 9, Win% = 80). Maximum spread. Sharpest slope. The interactive page plots the two dots and draws a line. The slope math falls out under Maya's pencil: m = (80 − 10) ÷ (9 − 3) = 70 ÷ 6 ≈ 11.67. Every 1-point increase in Power is associated with about 11.67 percentage points of Win% across the class. Then the y-intercept: b = 10 − 11.67 × 3 = −25. Equation: y ≈ 11.67x − 25. Maya pauses on the negative y-intercept. The line predicts negative Win% for P ≤ 2. That's not real — no team can have a negative win rate. She writes that observation next to the equation: the line works in our actual data range but breaks at the extremes. The model is local, not universal.

Test Bot C. Maya enters Patience's Season 1 Power value — P = 5, the old value. The test asks: did the model predict Patience's actual Season 1 Win% from Power alone? The page calculates: y = 11.67(5) − 25 = 58.3 − 25 = 33.3. Predicted Win% for Patience at P = 5: 33.3%. Patience's actual Season 1 Win% was 50%. Residual = Predicted − Actual = 33.3 − 50 = −16.7. The page shades the residual red — the model UNDER-predicted Patience by almost 17 percentage points. Maya stares at the number. We outperformed what Power alone would predict. Devon: "Either Power isn't the only thing that matters, or we're an outlier." Iris: "Or both." Maya writes the residual down with one sentence underneath: Power alone doesn't capture Patience's Season 1 performance — the model misses by 17 points in our favor.

Season 2 prediction. Now the new Power value: Patience's Season 2 P = 7. "Plug it in," Iris says. y = 11.67(7) − 25 = 81.7 − 25 = 56.7. Predicted Win% at P = 7: approximately 57%. Maya writes the sentence the script demands, exact wording: "Based on the Season 1 class data, changing Power from 5 to 7 predicts approximately 57% win rate." In an 8-match Season 2, that's 0.57 × 8 = 4.56 → roughly 5 wins. Same as the index card. Two roads, same destination. *Except the equation also gave her the residual — and that's the new piece.*

The teacher calls three teams. Maya's gets called for the residual. "Your prediction was 33.3%. Patience's actual was 50%. What does the residual mean?" Iris says,"It means Power didn't fully predict our performance. We did 17 points better than the linear-Power model said we should. Either there's a Speed effect, or a Turning effect, or we played well in a way the model can't see. And the line was built from only two points — Iron Ravens and Cobalt are extremes. The middle teams might pull the slope down." The teacher restates what Iris just said: "So the model captured part of the pattern, but not all of it." Iris nods. The teacher names the practice: "That move — using the gap between predicted and actual to evaluate how much to trust the model — is called residual analysis. That's what data scientists do." Same term Day 7 used — different meaning now. Maya writes it again with a note: (Day 7 named it; Day 13 made it specific.)

Match Gate. Every team writes their Season 2 prediction on the wall in the required format. Maya's team writes: "Based on the Season 1 class data, changing Power from 5 to 7 predicts approximately 57% win rate for Patience." The teacher scans the boards. Predicts approximately. All six boards have the qualifying language. Patience walks to the afternoon matches with two predictions on record.

Two predictions for Season 2 now, by different methods, both pointing at 5 wins of 8. The numbers agree. What doesn't agree is the residual. The equation said Patience should have won 33% in Season 1, but they actually won 50% — the model missed by 17 points in the team's favor. If the residual pattern holds in Season 2, Patience might over-perform the equation again. Or the residual might shrink once the Power change kicks in — that's the test the next two days will run. Quantitative reasoning was the morning's move. Residual analysis is the move that matters going forward. Maya wrote both terms in her journal. Day 8 had six papers about the same bot. Day 11 had seven. Today's is eight. The math is layering up.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 2 Match Day 1 — Three Rounds, Three Ball Modes

The teacher names the four things that are different from Season 1. "One: your equations are live — you tested them this morning, now they predict real outcomes. Two: ball modes — I'll announce the ball and what stat it rewards before each round. Your equation doesn't know about ball modes. Three: commissioner rules are binding — POWER FLOOR 5, ENDURANCE FLOOR 4, UNDERDOG BONUS through 5th place. Four: loss recovery has three diagnoses now — wrong slope, wrong x estimate, ball mode changed the relationship." She moves on. Iris, eyes on the board, frowns: "Wait — what's the difference between wrong slope and wrong x estimate?" Maya was about to ask the same thing. The Magnetic Raptors Analyst, two tables over, raises a hand: same question. The teacher pauses. "Yeah. I went too fast on that one." She walks back to the board. "Wrong slope: you said win% changes by 11.67 per Power point. The actual rate is different — maybe it's only 8. Whole line is the wrong steepness. Wrong x estimate: your slope is fine but you put in the wrong opponent Power. Say the opponent is P = 7 but you predicted off P = 5. Slope's right; the number you plugged in was wrong." Iris nods. Maya writes both definitions next to the diagnoses. The first explanation was the formal one. The second one is the one she'll actually use.

Round 1 — Standard ball. The teacher: "Foam ball. Standard Mode. Equations apply as built." Patience draws Magnetic Raptors. Raptors' Power = 6 (post-Underdog). Iris runs the equation. Raptors at P = 6 → predicted Win% = 11.67(6) − 25 = 45. Patience at P = 7 → predicted 57. The gap is 12 points; Patience is favored. Maya writes 62% Patience on the data log — equation prediction adjusted for the head-to-head margin. The match plays. Patience reaches the ball first at 0:38 — Speed 4 cost them a second on first contact but Power 7's chargePower 49 drove the ball clean over the line. Raptors comes back at 1:50; Final: 1 − 0. Win. Residual = 0.62 − 1.00 = −0.38. Under-predicted, in Patience's favor. Iris writes the diagnosis: "Diagnosis 2: opponent x estimate may have been slightly low — Raptors at P = 6 is even more Patience-favored than the equation captured, because chargePower scales quadratically and our P advantage compounds. Equation works directionally; magnitude is off."

Between R1–R2 window. The teacher says, "Compare your Season 1 residual from Day 8 to today's Round 1 residual. Magnitude similar, larger, or smaller?" Iris and Maya check. Day 8 Season 1 residual against the Power-only model: −17 points (model under-predicted Patience). Today's R1 residual: −38 points (model under-predicted Patience even more). Similar direction. Larger magnitude. Iris writes: "Residual is still negative — same direction as Season 1. We're still over-performing the linear-Power model. The Power 7 upgrade may be widening the gap, not closing it."

Round 2 — Speed Ball (dog ball, 27.8 g, 2.76"). The teacher reads the sentence twice: "Dog ball. Speed Ball Mode. This rewards Speed — not a broken slope." Patience draws Kinetic Falcons. Falcons' Power = 7 — equal to Patience. Falcons' Speed = 5 — one above Patience's 4. Maya runs the equation. Falcons at P = 7 → predicted 57. Same as Patience. Equation says 50-50. Maya writes 50% Patience on the data log. But she also writes a margin note: "Ball mode rewards Speed. Falcons S = 5, ours S = 4. The equation doesn't see this. Watch for residual." The match plays. The dog ball is light and rolls easily — bots that cover ground faster win the repositioning battle. Falcons' motorPower of 25% beats Patience's 20% on first contact every reset; Falcons reaches the ball half a second sooner each time. Falcons scores at 0:42 and again at 2:11. Patience never wins a possession battle. Final: 0 − 2. Loss. Residual = 0.50 − 0.00 = +0.50. Big over-prediction. Maya writes the diagnosis, fast: "Diagnosis 3: ball mode changed the relationship. The Speed Ball rewards Speed, and Falcons' S = 5 vs our S = 4 turned a Power-equal matchup into a Speed loss. The slope wasn't wrong — the equation just doesn't see Speed." The teacher walks past, reads it, nods. "That's the move. Don't blame the slope when the ball changed the rules."

Between R2–R3 window. Maya goes to the Skills Arena side field. Corner Kick challenge. The teacher's rule: the prediction has to come from the equation. Maya plugs in: at chargePower 49 (P = 7), follow-through 365 ms, what's the predicted ball travel distance? She uses the Day 10 distance formula work as a back-of-envelope. Predicts 4 of 4 corner kicks reach the goal. Actual: 3 of 4 — one veers because it hits the scoop funny. Residual: +1. Maya writes the sentence and earns a bronze badge. Faster Skills round than she expected.

Round 3 — Endurance Ball (rubber ball, 41.8 g, 2"). The teacher: "Rubber ball. Endurance Ball Mode. This rewards Endurance — not a broken slope." Patience draws Magnetic Raptors. Raptors' Power = 6, Endurance = 6. Maya runs the equation. Raptors at P = 6 → predicted 45. Patience at P = 7 → predicted 57. Patience favored by 12 points on Power. Maya writes 62% Patience. But she also writes a margin note: "Ball mode rewards Endurance. Raptors E = 6, ours E = 4. Decay rate matters more now. Watch for late-match collapse." The match plays. The rubber ball is heavier and rolls straight — robots that maintain motor power late control the ball longer. Minute 1: Patience scores at 1:08 — chargePower 49 drives the rubber ball clean across at full power. Minute 2: endurance decay kicks in. Patience at E = 4 drops to 19% motor power (lowPowerActive — red strip lights up); Raptors at E = 6 holds at 27%. Raptors recovers the centered ball at 2:18, pushes it home at 2:32. Then again at 2:55. Final: 1 − 2. Loss. Residual = 0.62 − 0.00 = +0.62. Bigger over-prediction than R2. Maya writes the diagnosis: "Diagnosis 3: ball mode changed the relationship. Endurance Ball rewards low decay. Patience E = 4 collapsed in minute 3; Raptors E = 6 held. Equation was right that Power gap favored us — but ball mode meant Endurance gap mattered more. Two Diagnosis 3s in a row."

Hard data check. The teacher walks every table. Three rows in every data log: prediction, actual, residual, diagnosis. Patience's row: R1 won, Diagnosis 2, residual −0.38. R2 lost, Diagnosis 3 (Speed Ball, Falcons' Speed advantage), residual +0.50. R3 lost, Diagnosis 3 (Endurance Ball, Raptors' Endurance advantage), residual +0.62. No blanks. The dashboard updates: Patient Foxes Season 2 record: 1 − 2. Two Diagnosis 3s in a row. Five matches left.

Prediction Market readout. The teacher reads each team's index card aloud. "Patient Foxes — predicted 5 wins. Day 1 record: 1 − 2. Pace projects 2.67 wins of 8." Maya does the math in her head before the teacher finishes saying it. Off pace. Need 4 wins from 5 remaining to hit the card. 80% win rate the rest of the way. The teacher's tone is level — neither encouraging nor discouraging. "The card stays up. Day 14 you check whether your equation needs adjusting based on today's residuals."

Exit ticket. "My Round 3 residual was +0.62 and I diagnosed it as Diagnosis 3 because the Endurance Ball rewarded low decay, which my equation doesn't see. Raptors' Endurance 6 held when our Endurance 4 collapsed at minute 2." Maya hands it in. The teacher reads it once and initials the bottom.

Maya predicted 5 wins this morning on an index card. The equation also predicted 5 wins. By the end of the afternoon she's at 1 − 2 with three diagnoses written down. The diagnoses are not a defense — they don't make the losses sting less. But they make the losses readable. The Falcons loss wasn't a bad prediction; it was a Speed-Ball loss the equation can't see. The Raptors loss was an Endurance-Ball loss the equation also can't see. The R1 Raptors win was a model under-prediction, same direction as Season 1. Three matches, three different stories. None of them say "equation is wrong." What two Diagnosis 3s in a row do say: ball modes matter more than her equation can capture. Maya writes one line under the day's data: "The model is doing what a linear model can do. The ball mode is a variable I have to layer on top — by hand, until the equation gets smarter. Two Diagnosis 3s in a row — tomorrow's auction has to address a ball-mode vulnerability, not Power." Day 14 morning is when she gets to try.
OPEN LAB

Residual Logging and Match Review

The teacher posts four steps on the board. 1. Predictions and actuals. 2. Residuals + direction. 3. Pattern sentence. 4. Specific question for tomorrow. Then the rule: "No equation changes today. Three matches isn't enough to know which value to change, and the change locks in. Write down what you saw. The revision comes tomorrow. Today's matches gave you three data points. That is not enough to know what to fix yet. So today's job is not to fix anything. It is to write down what you saw, calculate the gaps, and form one question that tomorrow's matches can actually answer."

Steps 1 and 2 — log the residuals. Iris pulls out the data log. Devon reads the predictions and actuals from this afternoon. Iris writes the residuals in clean columns: R1 predicted 0.62, actual 1.00, residual −0.38. R2 predicted 0.50, actual 0.00, residual +0.50. R3 predicted 0.62, actual 0.00, residual +0.62. Then the directions: R1 too low. R2 too high. R3 too high. Maya, watching from the bot's side of the table: "Two too high and one too low. The two highs are ball-mode rounds." Iris underlines the +0.50 and +0.62. Devon: "And the too-low is the only Standard round we played." The split is on the page in three lines.

Step 3 — pattern sentence. Iris drafts: "Our equation over-predicts in Speed Ball and Endurance Ball modes and under-predicts in Standard mode." She reads it aloud. Maya thinks. "That's three matches. We can't actually call that a pattern yet." Iris: "The script says if you can't call it a pattern, say so." She rewrites: "My three residuals were −0.38, +0.50, +0.62. The two over-predictions both came in ball-mode rounds; the under-prediction came in the only Standard round. With three matches I cannot yet call this a pattern, but the direction is suggestive." Devon: "That's the more honest version." He copies it into the data log.

Step 4 — specific question. Iris's first draft: "Does the ball-mode pattern hold?" The teacher walks past, reads it, shakes her head once. "Too vague. Make it specific enough that one more match result could change your answer." Iris stares at the page. Devon: "Specific how?" Maya: "I don't know — like a number?" Iris tries again, out loud this time: "Will our residual go down tomorrow?" She doesn't even write it. "That's still vague." Two minutes go by. The team is stuck. The teacher comes back. She asks: "If tomorrow's match gave you the same residual, what would you conclude? If it gave you the opposite, what would you conclude?" She walks away. Maya, slowly: "Wait. She's asking us to write both possibilities?" Iris: "Two conditions. Two conclusions." Devon picks up the marker. "OK. If tomorrow's Standard round gives us another negative residual around −0.4..." He stops. Maya: "Then the equation is consistently under-predicting Standard mode. Not a fluke." Iris writes it down. Devon: "And if it gives us a positive residual instead?" Maya: "Then R1 today was the fluke." Iris writes that one too. They work the ball-mode condition through the same way — back and forth, three drafts before the wording seems right. Iris reads the final version: "If tomorrow's Standard round gives us another negative residual around −0.4, the equation systematically under-predicts our Standard-mode performance. If the next ball-mode round gives us a residual under +0.30 after we win the auction, the ball-mode gap shrinks." The teacher returns, reads it. "Yes. That question can be answered by one more result. Cleared."

Wrap. Round-robin, one sentence each. Iris reads Patience's question. The teacher: "Good — two conditions, two different conclusions." Magnetic Raptors reads theirs: "If tomorrow gives us a positive residual on the Endurance Ball, our equation needs more Endurance weight." Steady Hawks: "My residuals were all under 0.05. My question is whether tomorrow's match gives me a residual over 0.10." The teacher's bridge: "Tomorrow you'll have six results. That is enough to look for a pattern and make one change. Hold the question you just wrote — it is the first thing we check."

Maya walks out of the Open Lab with three residuals, one pattern sentence, and one question — and exactly zero changes she's allowed to make to the equation. That should bother her. It doesn't. The teacher said it twice and didn't budge: three matches isn't enough to know what to fix. Don't fit to noise. Iris's pattern sentence was honest about the sample size — "I cannot yet call this a pattern." Maya's question for tomorrow is specific enough that one more result will move it. The discipline is to wait. To not act today. To watch what tomorrow shows. That's a math practice the camp hasn't named yet, but the teacher held the room to it for thirty minutes and didn't compromise. Maya writes one line under her question: "The Endurance Ball weakness is real, but the data is too thin to be sure. Tomorrow." Tomorrow she gets the auction — but not the equation. The equation gets six matches first.
DAY 14

Stat Auction · Acting on the Diagnosis · Match Day 2

Week 4 · Season 2 + Exhibition
Maya's arc today: Spends three tokens at the Stat Auction on Package 1 (+2 Speed) — the lever the Day 13 R2 Diagnosis 3 told her to pull. She has two Diagnosis 3s to choose between (Speed Ball and Endurance Ball) and picks Speed because restoring Season 1 Speed level addresses both the R2 loss and her ongoing first-contact disadvantage in Standard mode. To pay for it she gives up 2 points of Turning (T = 5 → 3), preserving Power 7 because the Power upgrade is still the central hypothesis. She walks to the Prediction Market wall and revises the card from 5 wins → 4 wins, citing the +0.50 R2 residual as the data point. The auction was the first time the math she's been collecting actually rewrote the bot.
Where Maya is nowPatience is 1 − 2 in Season 2 with five matches left. Three diagnoses from yesterday: R1 Diagnosis 2 (x estimate), R2 Diagnosis 3 (Speed Ball rewarded Speed; Falcons S=5 vs Patience S=4), R3 Diagnosis 3 (Endurance Ball rewarded Endurance; Raptors E=6 vs Patience E=4). Two Diagnosis 3s in a row — only one is exploitable today. The Stat Auction is the moment she gets to act on it.
HOOK

Stat Auction + Prediction Check

The teacher orients everyone to the webpage that explains the activity. Five auction packages, in order: +2 Speed, +2 Endurance, +2 Turning, +2 Power, +3 Wild. Token distribution: three per team, four for the last-place team. Patience has three — they're 1 − 2, not last (Iron Ravens is at 0 − 3 and gets four). If you can't, the bid goes to the next team."

Maya opens her data log to yesterday's three diagnoses. "R1 Diagnosis 2 — x estimate. Nothing to bid for. R2 Diagnosis 3 — Speed Ball rewarded Speed. R3 Diagnosis 3 — Endurance Ball rewarded Endurance. Two ball-mode losses. We can only pull one lever." Maya, in her Fabricator role, runs the allocation math both ways. "+2 Endurance: E goes 4 → 6, take from where? Power going to 5 erases the hypothesis. Speed going to 2 is brutal. Turning going to 3 is the cleanest hit. +2 Speed: S goes 4 → 6, restores Season 1 level. Take 2 from Turning, same cost." Devon weighs it out loud. "Endurance Ball only matters in matches that go past minute 2. Speed Ball matters from the first contact onward — every match. And restoring Speed 6 also fixes the first-contact disadvantage we had in Standard mode." Iris nods. "Pick the one that helps in more situations." They commit to Package 1, three tokens, take from Turning.

"Fists closed. Bid." Six teams open at once. Patience opens 3. Magnetic Raptors opens 2. Iron Ravens opens 1. Three others open 0. Patience wins. The teacher: "Patient Foxes — say your new stat and formula result." Maya, looking at Iris's screen: "Speed 6. motorPower% = 6 × 5 = 30%." The teacher nods. "Update your stat sheet now."

Allocation update. Devon pulls out the bot allocation card. S: 4 → 6. E: 4 (unchanged). T: 5 → 3. P: 7 (unchanged). Total: 6 + 4 + 3 + 7 = 20. ✓ Iris cross-checks against the commissioner rules on the wall. P ≥ 5? P = 7 ✓. E ≥ 4? E = 4 ✓. The teacher passes: "Show me your new total." Devon turns the card. Maya recognizes the term from yesterday morning's Prediction Market. Same term, different application. The auction was a market; quantitative reasoning was the move that won it.

Exit check. The teacher goes around the room. Each team states one formula fact. Patience's turn. Maya: "motorPower% went from 20 to 30 when our Speed moved from 4 to 6." The teacher writes it on her clipboard and moves to the next team.

Which means the three-week hypothesis stays alive AND first-contact is no longer a fight Patience loses by default. The card on the wall now reads 4 wins, not 5. Closer to honest, less ambitious. And revised with a number — the R2 residual — not a feeling. What the revision sentence also names, out loud, is the diagnosis Maya didn't fix: R3 Endurance Ball. "You pick the one the data is loudest about and you pull that one." Maya writes it in her data log. The Endurance Ball is going to come back.
CHALLENGE

Percent Change: Season 1 to Season 2 Configurations

"Auction is over. Your Season 2 configuration is locked. Before you can know whether your changes were good ones, you need to know how big they were. Percent change gives you that number." The teacher writes the formula on the board: Percent Change = (Season 2 − Season 1) ÷ Season 1 × 100. Then the worked example. Speed 4 → 6. Subtract: 2. Divide by Season 1 (4): 0.5. Multiply by 100: 50%. "Denominator is always Season 1. Always where you started. Apply this to all four of your stats. Thirty minutes."

Maya and Iris work through the four stats from the allocation card. Speed: Season 1 was 6, Season 2 is 6. (6 − 6) ÷ 6 × 100 = 0%. No change net — they restored at the auction what they sacrificed at the Season 2 reallocation. Endurance: Season 1 was 4, Season 2 is 4. 0%. Same story — at the floor both times. Turning: Season 1 was 5, Season 2 is 3. (3 − 5) ÷ 5 × 100 = −40%. Down forty. Power: Season 1 was 5, Season 2 is 7. (7 − 5) ÷ 5 × 100 = +40%. Up forty. Maya circles the pattern. Turning −40. Power +40. Same magnitude, opposite directions. Speed and Endurance at zero. Two stats moved. Both by the same size. Devon, doing his own version: "That's the trade in one sentence." The teacher passes. "Denominator?" Maya: "Season 1. Both Power and Turning. (S2 − S1) ÷ S1." The teacher initials the page.

The teacher reads the spread aloud. "Largest single-stat percent change in the room: Iron Ravens, Power +66.7% — POWER FLOOR plus Underdog +2. Most teams have at least one stat at 0%, including Patience on Speed and Endurance. The unusual pattern: Patience is the only team with a perfectly mirrored trade — Power +40%, Turning −40%, both other stats unchanged." The room turns to look at Maya, Devon, and Iris. That's accidental, Maya wants to say. The auction picked Speed; they paid in Turning. The math came out symmetric on its own.

Proportionality. "Patience increased Power by 40%. Should their wins go up by 40% this afternoon?" A student from another team says "yes, proportional." Another: "no, because Power is squared." The teacher turns to Maya. "Test it. Your Power went from 5 to 7. ChargePower is Power squared. What was your chargePower in Season 1? What is it now?" Maya, doing the math on her worksheet: "5 squared was 25. 7 squared is 49." The teacher: "Percent change." Maya: "Right. (49 − 25) ÷ 25 × 100 = 96%." A 40% stat change produced a 96% output change. "Maya's Power went up by 40 percent. Her chargePower went up by 96. Almost two-and-a-half times the size of the stat change. That's what non-proportional looks like." She names the practice: "What Maya just did — testing the claim with a specific formula to find out where it breaks down — is called reasoning from a counterexample. That's a powerful move in mathematics."

Exit ticket. Two problems on the board. Problem 1: A team's Endurance went from 6 to 3. Percent change. Maya writes: (3 − 6) ÷ 6 × 100 = −50%. Problem 2: A team increased Power by 40% from a Season 1 Power of 5. By how much did charge speed increase? Is that a 40% increase? Maya writes: New Power = 5 × 1.4 = 7. Charge speed at 5: 25. Charge speed at 7: 49. (49 − 25) ÷ 25 × 100 = 96%. Not 40%. Power scales quadratically, so a 40% Power increase produces a 96% charge speed increase. Non-proportional. She hands it in. The teacher reads it once. "Same answer you just got out loud, but written down." Initials the bottom.

On Day 11 Maya did one percent change calculation by hand and made it stick — 177.8% chargePower change for Iron Ravens — and defended it from memory at the Commissioner's Meeting. Today she did four in a row on her own bot. Speed 0. Endurance 0. Turning −40. Power +40. Symmetric. Clean. And then the Power +40% became chargePower +96% in two lines of math. The hypothesis she's been carrying since Day 11 is bigger on the inside than it looks from the outside. The chargePower jump for Iron Ravens at the auction (P=3 → P=5) was 177.8%. The chargePower jump for Patience (P=5 → P=7) is 96%. Both larger than the stat percent change. Power² is doing real work and she can read it now — not just trust it.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 2 Match Day 2 — Three Rounds, One Ball

"30 seconds. Look at your Match Day 1 Prediction Tracker. Find your Round 3 residual. Positive or negative?" Maya finds it. +62. The biggest one she had. "Hold that number. Today you find out if your adjustments closed the gap."

Ball mode + Best Model framing. The teacher holds up a squeaky ball and sets one on each field. "Yesterday the environment changed every round. Today it stays fixed. Squeaky ball, all three matches. Same ball. Round 1 to Round 3. If your residuals change today, that change came from your prediction strategy — not the ball." Then the framing Maya hasn't heard before: "The Best Model badge does not go to the team with the most wins. It goes to the team whose final record is closest to their prediction card. Your job today is not to win matches. Your job is to predict accurately." Maya writes Best Model = closest to card at the top of her tracker.

Round 1 — Kinetic Falcons. Step 0: MD1 residual against Falcons. Maya writes +50 (R2 Speed Ball — predicted too high; we lost). Step 0.5: Maya pauses. "My MD1 residual was +50 because Patience S=4 vs Falcons S=5 on Speed Ball. We had a Speed disadvantage. Today our Speed is 6 — we have the Speed advantage now. The auction flipped the matchup." She circles UP. Step 2: plug opponent's Power into equation. Falcons post-auction: P=9. y = 11.67(9) − 25 = 80. Patience predicted 57. Falcons favored by 23 points on Power. But Patience has Speed 6 vs Falcons' Speed 5 — squeaky ball rewards Speed. Maya adjusts up from the equation's ~30: 40%. They play. The squeaky ball is light and rolls fast; Patience's motorPower 30% beats Falcons' 25% on first contact every reset. Patience reaches the ball first three times in three minutes — scores at 1:34. Falcons' chargePower 81 punches through anyway, scoring at 0:48 and 2:25. Final: 1 − 2. Loss. Residual = 40 − 0 = +40. Maya circles TOO HIGH. Shrank by 10 points from MD1 — Speed advantage helped, but Power 9 was still too much.

Round 2 — Magnetic Raptors. Step 0: MD1 residual against Raptors. Maya writes +62 (R3, Endurance Ball — different ball, but it's the only Raptors anchor she has). Step 0.5: she pauses. "MD1 was Endurance Ball — different mechanics. Today's squeaky ball rewards Speed. Patience S=6 vs Raptors S=4 — we have Speed advantage." She circles UP. Equation says Raptors P=6 → predicted 45, Patience P=7 → predicted 57. Patience favored on Power by 12 points. Plus the Speed advantage on squeaky ball. Maya adjusts up: 65%. They play. Patience's motorPower 30% reaches every loose ball before Raptors' 20% can; chargePower 49 pushes the squeaky ball clean across the field. Patience scores at 0:42 and again at 2:12. Raptors scores once at 1:48. Final: 2 − 1. Win. Residual = 65 − 100 = −35. Maya circles TOO LOW. Yesterday's same matchup was +62 and a loss. Today it's −35 and a win — the auction worked.

Round 3 — Iron Ravens. No MD1 comparison. Step 0.5: Maya circles SAME with a note: "no MD1 anchor; equation alone, plus Speed advantage." Iron Ravens post-floor: P=5, S=4. y = 11.67(5) − 25 = 33. Patience predicted 57. Favored by 24 points on Power. Speed advantage on the squeaky ball too (6 vs 4). Maya predicts 75%. They play. Patience reaches the ball first at 0:38 (Speed 6 dominant on the lightweight squeaky ball), pushes at chargePower 49. Iron Ravens' chargePower 25 push falls short every time. Patience scores at 1:02 and again at 1:48. Iron Ravens scores once at 2:42. Final: 2 − 1. Win. Residual = 75 − 100 = −25. Maya circles TOO LOW.

Hard data check. Three rows filled. Residuals: +40, −35, −25. All under 50 in magnitude. The two same-opponent comparisons told different stories: vs Falcons shrank from +50 to +40 (Power gap still dominant); vs Raptors flipped from +62 loss to −35 win (Speed advantage decisive). Patience MD2 record: 2 − 1. Season 2 total: 3 − 3. Two matches left tomorrow.

Wrap. The teacher calls two teams. Patience gets called as Pattern 1 — residuals shrinking AND a matchup flipped. "What changed between yesterday and today?" Maya: "Two things. My Speed went from 4 to 6 at the auction — that flipped my Speed Ball matchups. AND how I read the equation changed: I adjusted UP for matchups where I now have a Speed advantage, even though my equation doesn't see Speed. My MD1 residual against Raptors was +62 and a loss. Today it was −35 and a win." The teacher echoes: "Same model. Different reading. Different bot."

Prediction Market pace check. The teacher reads every team's row. "Patient Foxes — predicted 4. Season 2 record: 3 − 3. One more win needed. Two matches available tomorrow." Maya writes 1 of 2 at the bottom of the tracker. On track. The teacher names the closing practice: "Today the environment was constant. Whatever moved in your residuals came from your model, your bot, or your reading. That is controlled-variable thinking — hold the environment constant, isolate what you changed, measure what moved." Maya writes the term in her data log.

Exit ticket — End of Day. Maya fills in the fields. W: 2. L: 1. On track? YES. Residuals compared to MD1: SMALLER on average. Round where equation was most wrong: Round 2 vs Raptors — residual −35 on a win. The equation under-predicted our win rate even after I adjusted UP. Then the load-bearing field: For tomorrow's Open Lab, the ONE thing I want to change: my slope (m), from 11.67 to roughly 8. Both my biggest negative residuals on wins — Day 13 R1 against Raptors (−38) and today against Raptors (−35) — suggest the equation systematically under-predicts our wins. I built the slope from two extreme data points (Iron Ravens P=3 and Cobalt P=9). I want to try a middle data point instead. The adjustment cards: Slope: change from 11.67 to ~8. X variable: keep Power. She hands it in. The teacher reads it once and writes one word in the margin: "Yes."

Two wins and a loss on the wall. Patience is 3 − 3. And the auction worked. The Raptors matchup that lost yesterday at +62 won today at −35 — same opponent, same equation, but Patience's Speed 6 reached the ball first every reset on a ball that rewards exactly that. The Falcons matchup stayed a loss because chargePower 81 is bigger than any Speed advantage can close — but the residual shrank. Same equation. Same Power slope. Same y-intercept. New bot, new reading. The teacher's name for it lands in her data log — controlled-variable thinking. But the line that surprised her was her own, on the exit ticket: slope from 11.67 to roughly 8. She doesn't quite know where the 8 came from yet — and she didn't change the x variable, only the slope — but the direction is clear. Her biggest negative residuals are on wins. The equation under-predicts how often Patience wins. The slope she picked from the extremes is too steep. She'll find out tomorrow if the hypothesis holds.
OPEN LAB

Residual Analysis and Stat Auction Testing

"You now have six match results across two different ball modes. Before you touch your equation, there is one question you have to answer first: did your prediction errors point in the same direction in both modes, or did they flip? The answer to that question changes everything about what you do next." Five steps go up on the board. Maya pulls out her data log and yesterday's exit ticket — the one where she wrote slope from 11.67 to roughly 8.

Step 1 — fill the residual table. Maya copies her six matches into the table. Day 13 R1 vs Raptors (Standard): predicted 62, actual 100, residual −38, L, too low. Day 13 R2 vs Falcons (Speed Ball): predicted 50, actual 0, residual +50, L, too high. Day 13 R3 vs Raptors (Endurance Ball): predicted 62, actual 0, residual +62, L, too high. Day 14 R1 vs Falcons (Speed Ball): predicted 40, actual 0, residual +40, L, too high. Day 14 R2 vs Raptors (Speed Ball): predicted 65, actual 100, residual −35, L, too low. Day 14 R3 vs Iron Ravens (Speed Ball): predicted 75, actual 100, residual −25, L, too low. Six rows, all sizes L. Residuals range from 25 to 62.

Step 2 — sorting question. Maya looks at the directions column. Day 13: 1 too low, 2 too high — mostly too high. Day 14: 1 too high, 2 too low — mostly too low. She writes the answer: "Yes, direction flipped between Day 13 and Day 14." Then she stops and reads the answer back. "But Day 13 had three different ball modes. The script assumes Day 13 was one mode. Mine wasn't." Iris reads over her shoulder. "The flip isn't necessarily Day 13 vs Day 14. It might be Speed Ball vs the other modes." Maya nods. She adds a note next to her Yes: "Flip may be a ball-mode confound — Day 13 had three modes; Day 14 had one."Did she use confound right?

Speed Ball isolation. Maya isolates the four Speed Ball matches across both days. Day 13 R2 vs Falcons (P=7): +50. Day 14 R1 vs Falcons (P=9): +40. Day 14 R2 vs Raptors (P=6): −35. Day 14 R3 vs Iron Ravens (P=5): −25. She sorts by opponent Power. P=5: −25. P=6: −35. P=7: +50. P=9: +40. Maya stares at the column. "The direction changes with opponent Power. Low Power → too low. High Power → too high." Devon: "That's not flat. And it's not a clean fanning either — sizes don't strictly grow." Maya: "Low-P residuals are sizes 25 and 35. High-P are 50 and 40. Sizes grow with opponent Power, but the direction also flips around P = 6 or 7. That's a mix."

The teacher passes. "Tell me in one sentence: does this point to m, b, or neither?" Maya thinks. "Neither cleanly. A wrong slope would keep direction consistent and grow the size with opponent Power. A wrong intercept would keep direction consistent and keep size flat. Mine doesn't do either — the direction changes around opponent Power 6−7. That's not Path 2 or Path 3 — neither a slope problem nor an intercept problem." The teacher nods. "What's your call?" Maya pauses. She thinks about her exit ticket from yesterday — slope from 11.67 to roughly 8 — and now reads it with the residual table next to it. "My exit ticket said the slope is too steep. But the residual table shows the direction flips with opponent Power, not just the size. Steepening or flattening the slope won't fix a direction flip. I want to hold the equation. The data is too mixed-mode and too noisy to confidently diagnose." The teacher: "That's Path 4. That's a defensible call. Write that down."

Maya writes the two sentences. "Within Speed Ball, the residual direction flips with opponent Power — negative for low-Power opponents (Iron Ravens, Raptors), positive for high-Power opponents (Falcons). Sizes are all L (25 to 50)." Then: "This is not a slope problem (which would keep direction consistent) or an intercept problem (which would keep size flat). The pattern is consistent with my equation being miscalibrated around opponent Power 6–7 — possibly because my slope was built from extreme data points (Iron Ravens P=3 and Cobalt P=9). Six noisy matches across two ball modes don't give me enough evidence to choose a confident change. Holding the equation." "Using the original equation y = 11.67x − 25. For an opponent with P=7: y = 57, with manual UP adjustment for Speed advantage. This prediction carries more uncertainty than yesterday's did because the residual pattern is genuinely unclear."

Wrap. The teacher calls Patience as a held-equation team. "What did you see, and why did that tell you not to change anything?" Maya: "The direction of the residual changed with opponent Power instead of staying consistent. Neither Path 2 nor Path 3 fits. Yesterday, I wrote a hypothesis that the slope was too steep. The table today showed it's more complicated than that — the equation may be miscalibrated, but not in a way one change can fix." The teacher names the practice for the room: "What Maya just did — looking at where her prediction went wrong and asking whether the model is broken or whether something outside the model changed — is residual analysis. Yesterday she had a hypothesis. Today she had data, and she updated."

Maya walked into Open Lab with a hypothesis and a number — slope from 11.67 to roughly 8. She walked out with the same equation she walked in with. That's also calibration. Not of the equation — of her understanding of what the data could actually support. The exit ticket sentence she was proud of yesterday turned out to be a hypothesis she couldn't defend once she did the table. The Path 4 finding the teacher named — not enough evidence to diagnose — is a real finding, not a failure. Maya writes one line under her held equation: "My exit ticket was a guess. The table didn't back it up. Maybe MD3 gives me enough data to actually tell." Yesterday's calibration was the bot reading the equation. Today's calibration was Maya reading her own conclusion.
DAY 15

Bot Lore · Whose Equation Tracked Closest

Week 4 · Season 2 + Exhibition
