The day closes with the silent Post-Task Diagnostic and Open Lab exhibition prep, ending on the Skill Bridge sentence Maya says to a stranger: "I know that quadratic isn't the same as proportional."
Where Maya is nowPatience is 3 − 3 in Season 2 after Day 14 afternoon (1 − 2 on Match Day 1 at the old allocation, 2 − 1 on Match Day 2 at the new S=6/T=3 allocation in Speed Ball mode — the Raptors matchup flipped from a +62 loss to a −35 win). Two matches left. The Prediction Market card on the wall reads 4 wins. On pace for 4 − 4 if Patience wins one of the last two. The Gallery Walk is the last data Maya sees before the Market closes.
HOOK

Bot Lore Gallery Walk

Six elements on a wall sheet: bot name, Season 1 stat allocation, Season 2 stat allocation, linear equation with labels, Season 2 match record, one defining moment. Maya, Devon, and Iris split the work. Devon writes the two configs side by side: S=6, E=4, T=5, P=5 → S=6, E=4, T=3, P=7. Iris writes the match record: MD1: 1 − 2. MD2: 2 − 1. Total: 3 − 3. Maya writes the linear equation: y = 11.67x − 25, where x = opponent's Power, y = Patience's predicted Win%. Slope labeled. Y-intercept labeled. X-variable labeled. The defining moment is Iris's call: "Day 14 Speed Ball matchup against Raptors flipped from a +62 loss to a −35 win — same opponent, same equation, but Speed 6 reached the ball first every reset. The auction trade worked."

The teacher reads Q1 through Q4 aloud. On Q2 she does the partner check: "Which team's linear equation would you trust most? What would you actually need to look at on a team's wall to answer that?" A kid from Kinetic Falcons answers first, confident: "The team with the most wins. If they won, the equation has to be right." The teacher doesn't say yes or no. "Walk me through that. What does the equation actually predict?" Falcons kid, slower: "Win… probability." Teacher: "And what does the win column on the wall say?" "Actual wins." Teacher: "So if I want to check how good a prediction is, what am I comparing?" An Iron Ravens kid, getting there: "You'd compare the predicted number to the actual number." Teacher: "What's the word for that gap?" Three seconds of room silence. Maya, looking up from her data log: "Residuals. Smallest gap between predicted and actual is the most trustworthy equation." The teacher nods once. "That's the question. Walk." Maya picks up her data log.

Station 1 — Magnetic Raptors. Their equation: y = 8x + 10, x = Endurance. Their match record: MD1: 2 − 1. MD2: 2 − 1. Total 4 − 2. Maya does the residual check in her head. Raptors at E = 6: predicted Win% = 8(6) + 10 = 58. Actual Win% across 6 matches: 4/6 = 67%. Residual: 58 − 67 = −9 points. That's smaller than her own −17 residual on Patience. She writes the number down in her data log, underlined.

Station 2 — Steady Hawks. Their equation: y = 6x + 8, x = Endurance. Their record: 3 − 3. Hawks at E = 7: predicted 8(7) + 8 = ahem wait — Maya re-reads — 6(7) + 8 = 50. Actual: 3/6 = 50%. Residual: 0. Maya stops moving for a second. Zero residual. Hawks' equation predicted their exact win rate. Hawks' slope (6) is more modest than my slope (11.67), and their intercept (8) is positive — the line doesn't dive below zero at the extremes the way mine does.

Station 3 — Kinetic Falcons. Their equation: y = 9x + 20, x = Power. Their record: 5 − 1. Falcons at P = 9 (post-auction): predicted 9(9) + 20 = 101. Capped at 100. They didn't write a cap; the equation predicts a number that can't exist. Actual: 5/6 = 83%. Residual: ~17. Big. Their equation lets Power explode at the top, just like Maya's does at the bottom (negative y-intercept). Both her equation and Falcons' break at the boundary. Hawks' doesn't.

Back at her station. Q1 (biggest config change): Iron Ravens — Power forced from 3 to 5 (POWER FLOOR rule), plus Underdog +2 added a third allocation move. Total absolute change of 5. Patience moved 4 (Power up from 5 to 7, Turning down from 5 to 3). Cobalt only moved 1. Iron Ravens is the most-changed bot in the room — and Patience's own POWER FLOOR rule is the reason. Q2 (most trustworthy equation): Steady Hawks. Residual of 0 across 6 matches; modest slope; no boundary breakdown. Q3 (commissioner rule with most effect): POWER FLOOR 5. Forced Iron Ravens to revamp; Patience's whole proposal was built on it; visible on every team's S2 display where Power changed.

Maya writes about Magnetic Raptors, using the scaffold from the worksheet. She rereads the scaffold twice — the prompt has three blanks and she keeps mixing up started and changed. Iris taps the worksheet: "Started is Day 1. Changed is the auction." Maya nods and writes: "Magnetic Raptors started last-place, took the Underdog bonus, moved Power 4 to 6, and ended with an Endurance equation that fit their data better than mine fit ours." She reads it back. "Last part says our equation is worse." Devon: "Is that what you mean?" Maya, slower: "It's what the residuals say. I think." She leaves it.

On her way back, Maya leaves one at Steady Hawks: "Your residual was zero across six matches. That's the only equation in the room that didn't break at a boundary." Hawks finds it when they return. Maya finds two on her own wall — Magnetic Raptors wrote: "Patience changed more stats than any team. Three of four." Iron Ravens wrote: "Without your POWER FLOOR rule, we wouldn't be here." She tapes both inside her data log.

Share-out. The teacher calls Iron Ravens first — narrative complexity, a season story that's all reallocation under a rule someone else wrote. Iron Ravens reads: "Iron Ravens started at Power 3, the worst record in the league, changed by the POWER FLOOR rule forcing them to Power 5 plus the Underdog +2 budget bonus, and ended as the team Patience's whole proposal was built around." The teacher echoes the key data point and asks the room what makes the sentence provable. "Power went from 3 to 5 is on their wall. Underdog +2 is on their wall. Patience's proposal cited them — on Patience's wall." Then the teacher calls Patient Foxes second for the equation reference. Maya stands and reads her Q4 sentence. The teacher: "Why does naming the equation as less trustworthy tell the story better than naming the wins?" Maya: "Because the wins came from the trade we made at the auction, not from the equation. The equation never knew about Turning. The trade told us to act on Turning. They're different sources of evidence." The teacher names the practice: "What Maya just did — moving from a number on the wall to a sentence that explains what that number means for that team's whole season — is called interpreting data in context. That is what every mathematician does when the calculation is done." Maya writes interpreting data in context in her data log.

Synthesis + exit ticket. The teacher reads her three patterns: "One. Every team that won a Day 14 auction package changed their highest Season 1 stat or their lowest — never a middle one. Two. Three of six equations predicted values that fell outside 0–100 at the boundaries; only three stayed inside the possible range. Three. Every defining moment written on the wall was a match decision, not a season win." She stops. Maya writes the exit-ticket annotation: "I'd bet on Steady Hawks. Their MD2 residual was 0 — smallest in the room. Their slope and intercept both keep the equation inside 0–100. Mine doesn't." Hands it in.

Three weeks ago Maya would have answered Q2 with "the team that won the most." Today she answered it with a residual she calculated in her head while standing in front of someone else's wall. That's the move. What she didn't expect was where the residual would point — not at Patience. Her own equation broke at the boundary (y = −25 at x = 0 — negative win probability isn't a thing). Falcons' broke at the top (y = 101 at x = 9 — over 100% isn't a thing). Hawks' equation kept its predictions inside the real range and matched their actual win rate exactly. Hawks picked a modest slope from middle-of-the-data points. Maya picked the steepest possible slope from extremes. The lesson that lands: extreme data points produce dramatic slopes, but the line breaks at the edges. Magnetic Raptors knew this on Day 13 and didn't say it. Hawks knew it and did say it on their wall — in their match record, which matched their equation to the percentage point. Maya wrote it in her data log on Day 15. Better late than not at all. She also wrote one more line underneath: "For Season 3, use a middle data point and a modest slope. Don't reach for the steepest line just because the spread is widest." That sentence is now her tomorrow.
CHALLENGE

Teach the Math — Defend Every Claim with a Number

"In 24 hours a visitor is going to walk in and not know what a stat card is. Today you pick one formula you can explain to that person — formula, real numbers from your Season 2 bot record, plain language. No jargon." She points at the board where the nine formula options are written: Budget. Speed. Turning. Endurance. Power. Percent Change. Distance. Linear Equation. Proportional Scaling. The rule: "Every student picks one. No two students in the same team pick the same one. Write your name next to it." Devon walks up first and writes his name next to Budget Constraint — "It's addition. I want to start with the floor." Iris writes her name next to Linear Equation — "I already did this one for the residual page. Same equation." Maya stands at the board for ten seconds. She has three real candidates — Power (her Day 11 insight), Endurance (the formula behind her bot's actual loss pattern), and Percent Change (the calculation she defended at the commissioner meeting on Day 12). She writes her name next to Percent Change. The 177.8% from Iron Ravens' chargePower is still in her data log.

Maya opens the interactive lesson. Picks Percent Change. The tool asks her to enter an Old value and a New value from her Season 2 bot record. She doesn't enter a stat. She enters a stat-squared. Season 1 Power was 5 → chargePower = 25. Season 2 Power is 7 → chargePower = 49. She types those into the tool. The tool walks her through: (49 − 25) ÷ 25 × 100. She does the subtraction in her head — 24. Divides — 0.96. Times 100 — 96%. She types into the plain-language box: "The biggest thing Patience changed between seasons wasn't a stat — it was a stat-squared. We added 2 Power points. That looks small. But chargePower is Power squared, so the calculation on the wall is (49 minus 25) divided by 25 times 100, which equals 96%. Almost double the push on the ball, from two stat points." She hits Check My Explanation. The tool flags "jargon: chargePower." She rewrites: "Imagine your grade went from 60 to 78. That's a 30% improvement. Same idea here. Step 1: subtract — 49 − 25= 24. Step 2: divide — 24 ÷ 25 = .96. Step 3: × 100 = 96% change. " Re-checks. Cleared.

Pitch rehearsal. Two-minute timer. Devon plays visitor. Maya: "Our Power stat went from 5 to 7 between Season 1 and Season 2. The formula for push force is Power squared. So our push force went from 25 to 49. Percent change is new minus old, divided by old, times 100. So 49 minus 25 is 24. 24 divided by 25 is 0.96. Times 100 is 96%. Almost double the push, from two stat points." She stops. Devon: "What does 96% more push feel like in the arena?" Maya, after a pause: "It means by Day 14 we could move the squeaky ball from where Magnetic Raptors had stopped it — and we couldn't on Day 12." Devon: "Yes. Open with the match. Then go to the formula. The formula explains the match." Maya re-orders her opening. Iris is next. She pitches her linear equation — y = 11.67x − 25 — and Maya catches one thing: "You said the slope is 11.67. Don't say the number first. Say what the slope means first. 'For every point of opponent Power we lose 11.67 points of predicted Win%.' Then the number." Iris re-orders. Devon pitches Budget. Three minutes. They're ready.

The teacher writes the order on the board: Budget → Speed → Turning → Endurance → Power → Percent Change → Distance → Linear Equation → Proportional Scaling. Devon goes first — clean read of the constraint, 3-minute hold. Endurance — fourth. A student from Kinetic Falcons reads her formula and says: "Higher endurance means we lose power faster." The teacher doesn't correct her. "Plug in E=18 and then E=4. Which one loses more power per minute at minute 3?" The student does the math at the board. E=18: (20−18) × 1.5 × 3 = 9% lost. E=4: (20−4) × 1.5 × 3 = 72% lost. She reads the numbers back. "Oh. Higher endurance loses power slower." The teacher echoes the claim back, clean: "Higher Endurance means a smaller (20 − E), which means less power loss per minute. The formula's direction is opposite from how the word sounds." Maya writes direction-of-formula check into her data log. Endurance 4 means we lose more, not less. Heard it. Wrote it down.

Power — fifth. Iron Ravens' presenter writes P = 6, charge = 12 on the board. The teacher is at the back of the room helping Cobalt with a calculator. She doesn't see it. The presenter keeps going. Two kids in the front row are flipping a card back and forth and not watching. Devon, low, to Maya: "He just did P times 2." Maya glances up. Devon's right. Devon raises his hand. The teacher looks over, walks back, reads the board. Beat. "You said charge equals P times 2. The formula says P squared. What is 6 times 6?" The presenter corrects to 36. The same error Maya made on Day 11 and Devon caught. The teacher names the practice now: "Quadratic does not mean doubled. Doubled is times 2. Squared is times itself." Maya — in the chair this time — hears how it must have sounded to Devon four days ago. Percent Change — sixth, Maya. She stands. Opens with the match: "On Day 12 our bot couldn't move the squeaky ball when Magnetic Raptors stopped it. On Day 14 we did. The reason is on our wall." Then the calculation: chargePower 25 → 49. (49 − 25) ÷ 25 × 100 = 96%. Almost double the push from two stat points. The teacher asks the connecting question: "Magnetic Raptors went from Power 4 to Power 6. What's their chargePower percent change?" Maya does it at the board: chargePower 16 → 36. (36 − 16) ÷ 16 × 100 = 125%. She turns back: "Theirs went up by more than ours. Same +2 Power points — but 16 to 36 is a 125% jump, and 25 to 49 is only 96%, because their starting value was smaller. The denominator matters." The teacher echoes it back to the room: "What Maya just named — that the same change produces a bigger percent when the starting value is smaller — is the reason the formula divides by Old and not by New. The denominator is where the calculation lives." Then she names the practice: "What every student in this block just did — took a formula, plugged in real numbers, got an answer, and then explained what the answer actually does in the arena — is called mathematical modeling. A model is when math describes something real. Your formulas are models of your bots." Maya writes mathematical modeling in her data log. That's three named practices in three days.

Exit ticket — individual. Three minutes. One index card. The teacher: "This is not a team question. I need to know what each of you understands." Maya writes her formula by hand. chargePower percent change = (49 − 25) ÷ 25 × 100 = 96%. Circles 96%. Then the plain-language box. She starts to write "Our push force went up 96% from Season 1 to Season 2" — and stops. That's the formula talking, not the match. She crosses it out and starts over: "By Day 14 we could move the squeaky ball that Magnetic Raptors had stopped — because two extra Power points isn't 'two more.' Power is squared, so push force went from 25 to 49, which is 96% more. That's the formula behind the match." She hands the card in at the door. The teacher reads it once and initials the bottom corner.

Maya wrote a clean Percent Change calculation in three minutes and explained it without jargon to a teacher she'd never spoken to alone before. That happened. What she didn't expect was the connecting question — Magnetic Raptors went from Power 4 to Power 6, same delta, different denominator, 125% versus her 96%. The Day 11 thing that broke her — that 25 chargePower for Iron Ravens' Power 5 wasn't the same as 25 chargePower would have meant from Power 3 — is the same idea she just said out loud about her own bot. The denominator matters. That sentence is now hers. But there's something else underneath. When the Falcons presenter said "higher Endurance means we lose power faster" and the teacher made her plug in E=18 versus E=4, Maya wrote the numbers down for her own bot. E=4: (20−4) × 1.5 × 3 = 72% power lost by minute 3. That number isn't on any wall yet. It explains why the Day 14 Endurance Ball match was the one Patience couldn't pull back. Percent Change is the cleanest formula she has — the one she could teach a stranger today. Endurance is the formula behind the loss she still hasn't explained. Tomorrow there's an exhibition. The formula she'd be proudest to teach isn't necessarily the formula a visitor would learn the most from. She doesn't decide tonight. She just notices.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Season 2 Match Day 3 — Does the Equation Survive Three Balls

Three balls are staged at the edge of the field: foam, squeaky ball, rubber. The ball mode chart is taped to the wall: R1 Standard = Foam. R2 Speed Ball = Dog Ball. R3 Endurance Ball = Rubber Ball. The teacher: "These are not surprises. By Day 15 you already know ball type affects performance. The question today is whether your equation survives changing conditions." Maya opens her Prediction Tracker. The MY EQUATION section gets y = 11.67x − 25, x = opponent's Power. Devon, watching her write it: "Same equation from this morning. Residual was −17 across MD1 and MD2." Maya: "I know. Today we find out if it was −17 because of the slope, the x-variable, the auction, or the ball." She copies the four-cause list off the board into her data log: (1) slope error, (2) x-variable choice, (3) model drift, (4) environmental sensitivity. Her Prediction Market card reads 4 wins. Patience needs 1 win today to hit it.

Round 1 — Foam vs. Iron Ravens (P = 5). Maya plugs in: y = 11.67(5) − 25 = 33.35. She rounds to 33%. Previous residual was negative; she circles UP in the adjusting box and writes a final prediction of 40%. Foam ball, three-minute match. Patience scores first on Speed. Iron Ravens evens it at minute 2. Then the last minute: Endurance 4. (20 − 4) × 1.5 × 3 = 72% motor power lost by minute 3. Patience's bot slows visibly. Iron Ravens drives in the winner. Loss. Actual win% = 0%. Residual: 40 − 0 = +40. Too high by 40 points. Iris, calmly: "Equation watched Power. The Endurance number is what lost us the last minute." Maya writes the sign. She also writes x-variable choice next to the residual.

Round 2 — Dog Ball vs. Magnetic Raptors (P = 4). Speed Ball mode. The ball is bigger, lighter; whoever reaches it first wins the reset. Maya plugs in: y = 11(4) − 25 = 19. She's about to write 19. Iris, quiet: "You dropped the point six seven." Maya looks down at her own number. "Crap." Redoes it. 11.67 times 4 is 46.68. Minus 25 is 21.68. She rounds to 22%. Maya, low: "That's twice this week." Iris shrugs. She circles SAME — her last residual was +40, but that was on foam; she can't tell which way to adjust for a different ball. She writes 22%. Match: Speed 6 reaches the dog ball first every reset. Patience scores on three of four resets. Raptors can't recover. Win. Actual win% = 100%. Residual: 22 − 100 = −78. Maya stares at the number. "Equation predicted 22%. We won 100%. That's not a slope problem. That's the equation not knowing what Speed Ball is." Devon: "Environmental sensitivity. Your equation only knew Power." Maya writes environmental sensitivity next to the residual. Patience: 4-4 with one match left. The Prediction Market card is still live.

Round 3 — Rubber Ball vs. Kinetic Falcons (P = 9). Endurance Ball mode. The ball is heavy, dense, requires push force AND sustained motor power. Maya plugs in: y = 11.67(9) − 25 = 80.03. She rounds to 80%. She circles DOWN — her last residual was −78, so the equation was wildly low; this prediction is wildly high; pull it back. She writes 55%. Match: Falcons E = 5. Patience E = 4. Both bots fade by minute 2 — but Patience fades a full minute earlier. Falcons score three unanswered in the last 90 seconds. Loss. Actual win% = 0%. Residual: 55 − 0 = +55. Three residuals: +40, −78, +55. Spread of 135 points. Maya writes one sentence in her data log: "My equation was wrong in three different directions in 60 minutes." Patience MD3 record: 0 − 3. Season 2 final: 3 − 6.

Summary — Prediction Market reveal. Miss-to-hit order. The teacher reads Falcons first (card 6, record 7-2, MISS), then Iron Ravens (card 2, record 2-7, HIT), down through the rivals. Patience: "Card said 4 wins. Season 2 record is 3-6. MISS by 1 win." Maya writes the number. Steady Hawks last: "Card said 3. Record is 3-3. HIT." Best Prediction goes to Hawks. Tiebreaker: "Lowest average residual across three Season 2 matches. Hawks averaged a residual of 4. Closest in the room." Maya's morning exit-ticket bet was Hawks. It landed.

Retrospective + practice named. Q1: did your equation become more or less accurate across Season 2? Maya writes first, then reads: "My equation had two of the four causes wrong at once. Wrong x-variable choice — I only watched Power, which works for foam but doesn't predict Speed Ball or Endurance Ball. And environmental sensitivity — same equation produced +40, −78, and +55 across three balls. Same opponent stat, different ball, opposite-direction residuals." The teacher echoes it back to the room: "Maya named two of the four causes. What about the other two? Slope error?" Maya: "Slope was steep — the morning Gallery Walk already showed Hawks' modest slope had residual 0 and mine had −17. So slope contributed, but it wasn't the biggest one today." The teacher: "Model drift?" Maya: "No. We played the same bot we predicted with. The drift was the auction trade-off, but the equation already reflected the post-auction stats by Match Day 1." The teacher waits seven seconds. Then names the practice: "What Maya just did — looking at the gap between her prediction and the actual result, and using that gap to identify what was wrong with her model — is called empirical model refinement. Engineers, data scientists, and economists do it every day. Write that phrase in the margin of your Prediction Tracker." Maya writes empirical model refinement in her data log. That's the fourth named practice this week.

Exit ticket + Season 2 close. Maya writes: Season 2 record 3-6. Prediction Market MISS by 1. One number to explain by Day 18: why three residuals had three different signs across three balls — +40, −78, +55. The same equation predicted both too high and too low depending on the ball. Hands it in. The teacher closes Season 2 for the room. Final standings posted; Best Prediction announced; Steady Hawks at the top of the residual board. Tomorrow is the Community Exhibition. Iris, looking at the wall of three-week-old artifacts: "We have a lot to teach." Maya, looking at her data log: "And a 96% chargePower number that's still mine."

Patience finished Season 2 at 3 − 6 with three Match Day 3 residuals that broke in three different directions inside an hour. That sounds like a disaster. What it actually is, sitting in Maya's data log, is a diagnosis: her equation watched the wrong stat (Power) for two out of three ball modes, and it had no variable for environment. The morning Gallery Walk told her Hawks' modest-slope equation was the most trustworthy in the room. The afternoon proved it — by letting Maya's steep, Power-only equation fail in three different ways against three different balls. The morning was the diagnosis. The afternoon was the test that confirmed it. She also won one match — the Speed Ball one — and it was the only one her equation didn't predict at all. The thing the equation missed was the thing she actually built her auction strategy around.
CHALLENGE

Post-Task Diagnostic — Prior Knowledge Reassessment

Framing. The room is quieter than it has been all week. Eight questions on the screen. No bots, no opponents, no ball modes. "On Day 1, you filled out something like this, and most of you did not know most of the answers. Today you do. I am not grading you against anyone else. I am comparing you to yourself from four weeks ago. Every answer you know today that you did not know on Day 1 is evidence of what happened in this room. Fill it out completely." I'll: "Start the timer." 12 minutes on the clock. Maya hears the second sentence land. Today you do. She remembers almost nothing about Day 1 — except the feeling of staring at the screen and scatch paper "Not Yet" in three of the boxes before she got to question 4. The timer starts.

Q1 through Q4 — the formulas she lived with. Q1: runner jogs 8 miles in 2 hours, how far in 5? She does it without writing the steps. 4 mph. 4 × 5 = 20. Q2: printer prints 240 pages in 4 minutes, how many per minute? 60. Q3: 4 apples per 3 oranges, 12 oranges in the basket, how many apples? She writes the ratio in the margin: 4 : 3 = x : 12. 12 ÷ 3 = 4. 4 × 4 = 16. Writes 16. Q4 — linear decay. A candle is 20 cm tall and burns 2 cm per hour. How tall after 3 hours? Maya almost laughs. P(t) = 20 − 2 × 3 = 14. She just lived through a 90-minute match day where her bot lost 24% motor power per minute — (20 − 4) × 1.5 — and the same formula on her exit ticket would have been the candle problem with bot stats inside it. The candle problem is the same problem with smaller numbers and a real-world object. She writes 14 cm in five seconds. Four answers down. She still has plenty of time

Q5 through Q7 — coordinate work. Q5: which quadrant for each of (4, −2), (−5, 6), (−3, −1), (1, 7). Three weeks ago she'd have second-guessed the negatives. Today she writes IV, II, III, I without consulting the gridded image on the screen. Q6: blueprint 4 ft wide × 6 ft long, scale factor 5, real dimensions? 20 × 30 ft. Done. Q7 — the arc problem. A river curves from town A to town B. The student wants to estimate the length along the curve, but the ruler only measures straight lines. Describe one thing the student could do. Maya stops. Three weeks ago she would have written "use a string." Today she writes: "Break the river into smaller straight segments. Measure each one with the ruler. Add them up. For more accuracy, use shorter segments. Or — plot coordinates along the river and use the distance formula on each segment between two points: d = √[(x₂−x₁)² + (y₂−y₁)²]." She reads it back. That's two methods. One of them is the formula she used on Day 11 to predict launch distance from corner to ball. She does not write that part. The diagnostic doesn't need it.

Q8 and the early finish. Q8: line through (1, 2) and (4, 11). Slope, and what it tells you about how y changes as x increases. Maya writes: slope = (11 − 2) ÷ (4 − 1) = 9 ÷ 3 = 3. For every 1 increase in x, y increases by 3. She remembers her Gallery Walk sentence from this morning — "For Season 3, use a middle data point and a modest slope." Three is modest. She starts to write that and crosses it out. This isn't the Gallery Walk. The slope number and what it means are enough. Eight answers down. The timer reads 6:04. She caps her pencil and sits back. She does not look around. She does not ask if she can leave. The teacher stands at the side of the room and does not make eye contact with anyone who has finished. Maya sits with her answer sheet submitted for six full minutes. Day 1 she still had four blanks at the 12-minute mark.

Collection + closing. The timer sounds. The teacher: "Computers closed." Devon finishes one last sentence and submits. Iris was already done. Submissions go in. The teacher waits until the room is silent again, "You just answered the same questions you answered on Day 1. That's it." No "good job." No "that wasn't so bad, right?" No commentary on performance. Maya stands up and notices her hands are not shaking. Day 1 they were shaking by question 3.

Maya doesn't remember her Day 1 answers. She remembers the panic. She remembers clicking "Not Yet three times before she got to question 4 and feeling like the kid who shouldn't have signed up. Today she answered all eight questions in six minutes and used two methods on Q7 — one of them the distance formula she taught herself on Day 11 when she had to predict the diagonal a launch sequence would cover. The candle problem was the same problem as her Endurance 4 bot, scaled down to smaller numbers. The slope problem was a more modest version of the equation her Gallery Walk this morning named as the least trustworthy in the room. The math that left her shaking on Day 1 took six minutes today — and she sat quietly with the extra time instead of filling it with anxiety. The teacher said one sentence about her work — "You just answered the same questions you answered on Day 1. That's it." — and that sentence is now what she leaves the diagnostic with. It is not a score. It is a measurement against herself from four weeks ago. Three weeks ago she didn't believe a measurement like that was possible to make about her. Today she sat in the chair while it was being made. That's the finding. No one says it out loud.
OPEN LAB

Exhibition Teaching Prep — One Conversation, Not Two

The teacher reads: "This morning you walked the room and added to your bot's story. That story is what you're bringing to the exhibition. Real visitors will sit down, pick one of three challenge cards, and ask you to walk them through the math. When they're done with the card, they're going to ask you questions — about your bot, your numbers, your season. Those two things are one conversation, not two." Then: "Write down your four stat values right now. And your Season 1 values next to them. From memory if you can. 30 seconds." Maya writes S = 6, P = 7, E = 4, T = 3 and underneath: Season 1: S = 6, P = 5, E = 4, T = 5. Devon writes his. Iris writes hers. Compute phase — 10 minutes. Maya works the three cards on paper: Motor% = 6 × 5 = 30%. Charge% = 7² = 49. Doubling: S = 12 → Motor% = 60 (doubled). P = 14 → Charge% = 196 (quadrupled). Percent change of her biggest stat move: Power 5 → 7 = (7 − 5) ÷ 5 × 100 = 40%. Devon double-checks the squaring. All four sets of numbers in her data log before the teacher walks past.

Dry-Run Round 1 — Devon plays visitor. Card pick: "3." Maya opens Challenge 3 — Percent Change — and teaches with her own numbers: "My Power went from 5 to 7. Formula is (new minus old) divided by old times 100. (7 minus 5) divided by 5 times 100 = 40%." Devon nods like a visitor would. Then he picks two follow-up questions from the bank. First — "Why did your team choose those numbers?" Maya: "We chose Speed 6 because we needed to reach the ball first when the ball mode rewards Speed. We chose Power 7 because Power squared is 49 push force — almost double from Season 1. We sacrificed Turning to get there. T went from 5 to 3." Second — "If you could change one thing about your allocation right now, what would it be?" Maya stops. Four seconds. Then: "I would move 1 point from Power back to Endurance. Power 6 still gives chargePower 36 — enough to push. Endurance 5 would slow my motor power decay by 1.5% per minute. By minute 3 I'd have 60% motor power left instead of 28%. That trade would have changed the Round 3 Endurance Ball loss from a 100% defeat into something I could actually fight." Devon writes the number down. The teacher walks past, hears the last sentence, and does not say anything. She nods once and keeps moving.

Debrief + Fix. The teacher names what she heard across the room: "Most common gap: when a visitor asks about your biggest prediction gap, you're naming the round but not the actual numbers. Fix that right now. 90 seconds. Write the sentence: round number, predicted value, actual value." Maya writes hers cleanly: "+55 in Round 3 on Match Day 3. Predicted 55% win probability. Actual was 0%." Devon writes his Budget Constraint follow-up. Iris writes her residual line. The room is heads-down for 90 seconds.

Dry-Run Round 2 — Iris plays visitor. Card pick: "2." Maya opens Challenge 2 — Linear vs. Quadratic. Tighter this round: "My Speed is 6. Doubled is 12. Motor% goes from 30 to 60 — doubled. My Power is 7. Doubled is 14. Charge% goes from 49 to 196 — quadrupled. Linear formulas double when you double the input. Quadratic formulas quadruple." Iris asks the gap question. Maya delivers the clean version straight back: "+55 in Round 3 on Match Day 3. Predicted 55% win. Actual was 0%." Iris asks the season change. Maya: "We moved 2 points from Turning to Power because Power² quadruples push force. It worked in Speed Ball. It didn't help in Endurance Ball — that one was about Endurance, not Power." Three minutes. Tight.

Exit Check + Skill Bridge. Two students called by name. The teacher picks Iris first: "Challenge 2. Your Power is 5. A visitor doubles it. Walk me through what happens to Charge%." Iris, without looking: "Power squared is 25. Doubled Power is 10. 10 squared is 100. Quadrupled." Three seconds. Teacher nods. Then she picks a kid from Iron Ravens: "A visitor just asked you — did your robot get better over time? Answer with a number." The Iron Ravens kid: "Our biggest Match Day 1 residual was +42. Our biggest Match Day 3 residual was −12. So yes — we got better. The number got smaller in magnitude." Teacher nods. Then: "Everyone stand up." Chairs scrape. "Today you practiced teaching math that belongs to you — to someone who has never seen it. That is harder than doing the math yourself. You had to know it well enough to explain it AND answer questions about it." Pause. "Skill Bridge: turn to someone not on your team. One sentence — what is one thing you know today that you did not know on Day 1? Say it out loud." Maya turns to a Steady Hawks kid she has never spoken to. Four seconds. Then: "I know that quadratic isn't the same as proportional. On Day 1 I didn't know what either word meant." The Hawks kid, after a beat: "I know how to calculate a residual in my head. On Day 1 I didn't know what one was." They nod at each other. Around the room, twenty-eight kids saying twenty-eight sentences out loud to a person they don't normally talk to. The teacher waits the full 30 seconds. Then: "That's the bridge. What you just said is what the program built. See you at the exhibition."

Patience walks out of Open Lab with three challenge cards computed on paper, two dry-runs in the bank, and two sentences Maya wasn't sure she had access to before today. The first one is the Skill Bridge sentence she said out loud to a stranger: "I know that quadratic isn't the same as proportional." The second one is the answer to Devon's hardest follow-up question: "I would move 1 point from Power back to Endurance. P = 6, E = 5. By minute 3 I'd have 60% motor power left instead of 28%." That's a second Patience that could have existed. Three weeks ago she didn't know how to design a bot. Tonight she designed a second one out loud — not as the bot she'd build over again, but as the answer to a question a visitor would predictably ask tomorrow. Day 1 Maya wrote on her goal card "I want to not feel bad in math class." Tonight she said something she knows. To a kid whose name she does not know. The Hawks kid said the residual sentence back to her. Two people who couldn't have said those sentences four weeks ago just said them to each other and walked away. Tomorrow she teaches actual visitors. Tonight she carries home quadratic isn't the same as proportional, and an alternative-design number she can speak from memory.
DAY 16

Final Day · Walk the Wall · Teach What You Built

Week 4 · Season 2 + Exhibition
Maya's arc today: Walks the room in silence and finds her artifact — the 177.8% chargePower verification on the Commissioner's wall, in her own handwriting. Picks proportional scaling to teach — the formula behind her Day 11 sentence that quadratic isn't the same as linear. Teaches real people real math for ninety minutes, freezes on the hardest-thing question, recovers with the bot reset. Her Skill Bridge card: translating a quadratic into "almost four times more push" instead of saying "P squared." Then the Awards Ceremony: per-team moment names the 177.8%, Arena Champion to Kinetic Falcons (7 − 2), Best Teacher to Maya on three of five ballots, growth named as "before I write a number down now, I check what it would do in the arena." Teacher's closing: "Most adults have never done any one of those three things."
Where Maya is nowSeason 2 closed yesterday afternoon. Patience finished 3 wins of 9 — one short of her revised Prediction Market card (4). Steady Hawks took Best Prediction with the lowest average residual in the room. The card on the wall now reads what actually happened: Patience didn't win the most, didn't predict the closest. What Patience has, walking into Day 16, is four weeks of work on every wall in the room — and visitors arrive at 9:25.
HOOK

Expert Setup — Prepare to Teach

"In 25 minutes a visitor is going to walk through that door and not know anything about this program. Not Day 1. Not Week 4. Nothing. You know more about this than they do. That's not arrogance. It's four weeks of work. Your job today is not to impress them. Your job is to help them understand one thing. Just one." "Stand up. Let's build your station."

Station setup Maya places the charged bot at the edge of the desk. She puts the bot in its current Season 2 config — S=6, E=4, T=3, P=7 — and writes the four numbers on a card next to it in marker. Devon opens the data dashboard and angles it so a standing visitor can read it. Iris opens the residual page from Day 13 and pulls out the formula reference sheet — Motor% = Speed × 5. ChargePower = P². Endurance decay = (20 − E) × 1.5 per minute. All five items in place by minute six.

Teaching goal card. Maya picks up her index card. The sentence frame is pre-written: "I want every visitor to understand that ___ because ___." Three topics on the board: Speed, coordinate plotting, proportional scaling. She picks proportional scaling. It's the formula behind the sentence she said out loud to a Hawks kid last night — quadratic isn't the same as proportional. She fills the card in: "I want every visitor to understand that our Motor% scales proportionally with Speed, but chargePower does not scale proportionally with Power, because Motor% = Speed × 5 (linear) and chargePower = Power² (quadratic). Speed 6 gives Motor% 30 — doubling Speed doubles Motor%. Power 7 gives chargePower 49 — doubling Power quadruples chargePower. That's the formula behind our Season 2 auction trade. Not twice the push from two more Power points — almost four times." She reads it back. Devon: "That's a formula, a match decision, and the Day 11 insight." Maya: "Yes. Three things in one card." She leaves it.

Peer practice — Maya teaches Iris. Iris plays visitor. Task 1 — physical handoff. Maya says the opening line: "This is our bot. It runs at 30% motor power for the first minute, then drops 24% every minute after that. Want to see it move?" She hands Iris the micro:bit. Iris taps the start button. The bot drives across the station on its own. Task 1 takes 40 seconds. Task 2 — coordinate distance. Maya places a dot on the grid at (2, 7) and another at (6, 7). Iris counts squares: "Four across, zero up. Four." Maya shows the formula calculation underneath — d = √[(6−2)² + (7−7)²] = √16 = 4. Same number. Task 3 — teaching goal card. Maya reads her card aloud. Iris paraphrases: "Motor% scales evenly with Speed — every Speed point adds exactly 5. ChargePower doesn't — every Power point adds more than the last. Doubling Speed doubles Motor%. Doubling Power quadruples chargePower. That's linear versus quadratic, and only one of them is proportional." Maya: "Yes. That's the formula behind our Season 2 auction trade. Two more Power points wasn't twice the push. It was almost four times." Iris and Maya switch. Then Devon and Maya switch.

Debrief. The teacher asks: "What was the hardest thing to explain? Don't fix it — just name it." Maya, after thinking: "The word doubled. People kept hearing it and thinking the answer should double too. With Speed it does — 30 to 60. With Power it doesn't. It quadruples. Same word, different thing happening." The teacher names the practice: "What Maya just described — noticing that the same word can describe two different mathematical relationships, and being careful about which one you mean — that is called precision of mathematical language. It is one of the hardest things in math. Not computing the right answer. Naming what the answer means." Maya writes precision of mathematical language in her data log.

Final check. Teaching goal card posted at the front of the station, visible from standing height. Bot accessible. Showing the residual page. Dashboard displaying. Formula reference sheet visible but off-center. The teacher walks the room one last time. "Stay at your station. Remember your opening line. The teaching starts now." She opens the door.

Maya stands at the front of her station with the bot facing out and her card taped where she can see it without turning around. The opening line is in her head: "This is our bot. It runs at 30% motor power for the first minute, then drops 24% every minute after that. Want to see it move?" Three weeks ago she would have planned twenty minutes of explanation. Today the plan is one sentence and a start button. The reset button is the same button. Devon told her about it twice during practice. Iris told her about it once. The teacher named it in the debrief. If she freezes, she goes back to the bot. That's the rule. She breathes once and watches the door.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Community Exhibition Teaching Begins

The door opens. Five visitors walk in — three parents, one school administrator, one person Maya doesn't recognize. The teacher greets them: "Welcome. The students at each station will teach you something they built over four weeks. Your one job: do what they ask you to do. They will explain it. You try it." The administrator heads to Patience's station first.

Visitor 1 — the administrator. Maya delivers her opening line. "This is our bot. It runs at 30% motor power for the first minute, then drops 24% every minute after that. Want to see it move?" The administrator nods. Maya hands her the micro:bit. The administrator presses the start button. The bot crosses the station on its own at full motor speed. The administrator: "What happens after minute 1?" Maya: "The formula says (20 minus Endurance) times 1.5 equals percent we lose per minute. Our Endurance is 4. So (20 minus 4) times 1.5 is 24. We lose 24% every minute. By minute 3 the bot's lost most of its motor power." The administrator: "Why didn't you make Endurance higher?" Maya: "Because of a trade-off. At the auction we had to pick which weakness to fix. I picked Speed instead of Endurance. So my bot is fast for one minute and slow after that. That's the thing I want you to leave knowing." The administrator nods slowly. Three weeks ago Maya would not have known that sentence.

Visitor 2 — a parent — does the coordinate task. Task 1 takes 30 seconds. Task 2 — the parent plots two coordinates from the scouting report: (2, 7) and (6, 7). Maya counts with them. "Four across, zero up. Four squares." Then shows the formula: d = √[(6−2)² + (7−7)²] = 4. Same number. The parent: "So the square root is just the counting?" Maya: "When the two dots are on the same row. When they're not on the same row, the square root is the diagonal — it's shorter than the across-plus-up path." She traces the diagonal with her finger. Iris steps in with the residual page open: "That's why we calculated the diagonal in Season 1 — to predict how long a launch sequence would take from corner to ball."

Visitor 3 — asks about Power, and Maya gets a number wrong in front of him. "Why is one formula times 5 and the other one squared?" Maya hands the visitor the comparison table off the scouting report: "Motor% goes up by 5 every time Speed goes up by 1. ChargePower goes up by more every time Power goes up by 1. At Power 4, chargePower is 16. At Power 8, chargePower is 81 —" Iris, from the other side of the bot, flat: "Sixty-four." Maya stops. Looks at the card. Eight times eight is 64. 81 is 9 squared. She feels her face get warm. Then she just says it. "Sixty-four. Sorry — I was thinking nine. At Power 8, chargePower is 64. Doubled Power, quadrupled chargePower." The visitor smiles a little and waits. "Why does that matter?" Maya — and this answer surprises her because it comes out without planning — "Because for three weeks I argued we should move 2 points from Speed to Power, and the reason is right there. Two more Power points isn't just twice more — it's almost four times more push on the ball." The visitor nods and steps over to look at the dashboard. He never mentioned the 81.

Visitor 4 — Maya freezes, then resets. A teacher from another district asks: "What was the hardest thing you learned?" Maya stops. She has the answer in her data log — quadratic ≠ proportional from Day 11 — but she can't find the sentence. She looks at the bot. The reset button. She says her opening line: "This is our bot. It runs at 30% motor power for the first minute, then drops 24% every minute after that. Want to see it move?" The visitor laughs and takes the micro:bit. She presses the start button. While the bot drives itself across the station, Maya finds the sentence: "The hardest thing was when I almost wrote 'proportional scaling' on a worksheet because that's what the morning lesson was about, and my teammate caught it before I did. Power is quadratic — it doesn't scale proportionally. They look similar from a distance and they're not." The bot completes its run and stops. The visitor looks up. "Say that again."

Visitor 5 — Iris takes the lead. The fifth visitor is a high schooler who asks Iris a residual question. Iris walks her through the Day 15 Gallery Walk insight — Hawks' residual was 0; ours was −17; Hawks picked middle data points and a modest slope; we picked extremes. The high schooler: "So you'd build the model differently next time?" Iris: "Yes. Maya wrote it in her data log on Day 15. For Season 3, use a middle data point and a modest slope. Don't reach for the steepest line just because the spread is widest." Maya hears her own sentence said back to her by Iris to a stranger.

Mid-block check. At the 45-minute mark, the teacher pauses to scan. Patience has had four visitors. Devon hasn't taken the lead with any of them — he's been on the dashboard the whole time. The teacher quietly directs a sixth visitor over and introduces Devon by name: "This is Devon. Ask him about the data dashboard." Devon takes a breath and starts. His opening line is the same one Maya used.

Ballots + exit ticket. Visitors begin to leave at the 75-minute mark. One ballot each at the door. Five ballots collected. None read out loud. When the last visitor walks out, the teacher waits for the room to settle, then saya: "You just taught real people real math. That happened." Then the exit-ticket prompt: the hardest formula to explain, what you'd say differently, and the two math checks." Maya writes: "Hardest: explaining a 24%-per-minute decay so it sounds like a cliff, not a slope. What I'd say differently: lead with 'by minute 3 the bot has lost most of its motor power' before naming the per-minute number, instead of stating the per-minute number first. Motor% at Speed = 7: 35%. ChargePower at Power = 3: 9%. Percent change Speed 4 → 5: 25%." She hands the card in at the door. The teacher reads it once and initials the bottom.

Maya taught five people math today. One number she said wrong out loud. One question she froze on. Three that went the way she practiced. The wrong number is the part she keeps thinking about. She said chargePower 81 in front of a stranger. Iris caught it. The stranger smiled. He asked the next question. Nothing else happened. Day 1 Maya would have replayed that sentence in her head for a week. Today Maya replays it twice, says "8 times 8 is 64" in her head so it sticks, and lets it go. The freeze with Visitor 4 is the other one. The reset worked — opening line, bot moving, visitor laughing — and that bought her the thirty seconds she needed to find the quadratic ≠ proportional sentence she'd written in her own data log on Day 11. She wrote it then for herself. Today she said it out loud to a stranger. The administrator's question about why she didn't fix Endurance took her further than she'd practiced for, and she answered with the auction trade-off she'd actually made — without pausing. Iris repeated Maya's Day 15 sentence to the high schooler. Her own words coming back at her from her teammate's mouth. She doesn't know who got her vote on any of the five ballots. She also doesn't need to. The five people who walked through the door asked five different questions, and the one wrong answer didn't break anything.
HOOK

Walk the Wall Final Gallery

Maya walks in and the room that is set up like a gallery — six zones around the walls, each labeled with an index card. Day 1 Goals. Bot Origin Stories. Rivalry Cards. Leaderboard History. Commissioner Rule Changes. The teacher says, "Look around. Everything on these walls is something you made. You're going to walk through it quietly. Take 15 minutes. Read everything. That is what four weeks of work looks like." Then: "As you walk, find one artifact that shows something you couldn't have made on Day 1. You don't have to tell anyone. Just find it."

Zone 1 — Day 1 Goals. Maya stands in front of her own handwriting from four weeks ago. The card reads: "I want to not feel bad in math class." She stares at it. That sentence has no number in it. Three weeks ago she would have crumpled it. Today, it doesn't bother her, and moves on.

Zone 2 — Bot Origin Stories. Patience's card from Day 1: "We picked Speed 6 because we saw 10 was too fast and 1 was dead. So somewhere in the middle. 14 left for the other three. Probably enough." Maya reads it twice. "Probably enough" isn't a calculation. It's a guess that landed somewhere reasonable because Devon could see three stats coming. That sentence was the first math Patience ever did, and Maya can name every reason it was a guess.

Zone 5 — Commissioner Rule Changes. Three rules on the wall in permanent marker, with vote counts. POWER FLOOR 5: 5 − 0. ENDURANCE FLOOR 4: 4 − 1. UNDERDOG BONUS EXTENDS TO 5TH PLACE: 3 − 2. Underneath POWER FLOOR 5, in smaller handwriting, the case study cited at the formal meeting: "Iron Ravens P = 3 → P = 5. ChargePower (25 − 9) ÷ 9 × 100 = 177.8% increase." Maya stops in front of her own number. She did that calculation on Day 11. She defended it from memory on Day 12. It is on the wall in marker. This is what she couldn't have made on Day 1. She keeps walking.

The teacher waits until everyone is seated. Then: "Find your Day 1 goals. Read what you wrote four weeks ago." Maya looks across the room at Zone 1 — she can still see her own handwriting from this distance. Then: "Now think about the artifact you found — the one that shows something you couldn't have made on Day 1. It might be a formula. It might be an argument you wrote." Maya is looking at the Commissioner's wall now. 177.8% in her own handwriting. 5 − 0 next to it. "The distance between those two things — that's what four weeks of math looks like." Then the closing consolidation: "On Day 1, you wrote a stat allocation based on what felt right. By Week 4, you calculated formula outputs and predicted match results with cited data. Same variables. Completely different level of precision. That's the math this program asked you to do — and you did it. This afternoon, the exhibition is yours. For now: take a seat. We have one more thing to do."

Maya found her artifact. It's the 177.8% on the Commissioner's wall, in her own handwriting, with vote count 5 − 0 next to it. She wouldn't have known what percent change was on Day 1. She wouldn't have known how to cite Iron Ravens specifically. She wouldn't have written a defense in two sentences and survived a verification check on it. The math she's holding now was made by a kid who didn't exist four weeks ago. The teacher named the distance in eight words — same variables, completely different level of precision. Day 1 Maya's goal card on Zone 1 — "I want to not feel bad in math class" — is on the same wall as the 177.8% verification math. Both are hers. Three weeks ago she would not have been able to look at the Day 1 card without flinching. Today she leaves it on the wall.
MATCH-BUILD-MAKE

Awards Ceremony — Name What They Did With the Math

"Four weeks ago you walked in here and most of you had never driven a robot. Most of you had never written a linear equation from a data table. Most of you had never argued in front of a room about whether a math rule should change. Today we are not doing any of that for the first time. Today you already know how to do it." She names the four parts of the ceremony — moments, Arena Champion, awards, reflection. "Let's start with what I saw." Six moments. One per team. She does not put Patience or the Champion last. Iron Ravens first. Cobalt Wolves second. Magnetic Raptors third. Then Patience. "Patient Foxes. On Day 11 you calculated that Iron Ravens' Power change from 3 to 5 produced a 177.8% chargePower increase. You verified the math in writing on Day 12 in front of the Commissioner, and used it to argue for the POWER FLOOR 5 rule that the whole league reallocated under. That math is on the wall right now in marker, vote 5 − 0 next to it. That is percent change applied to a quadratic relationship, applied to a Commissioner argument that changed the league. Three things in one calculation." She moves to Steady Hawks. Then Kinetic Falcons. Maya does not move while the teacher is speaking. Devon nudges her after the pause. Maya finds her hands.

Arena Champion. The teacher steps back. "The Arena Champion goes to the team with the most wins. Every team that made it today had already done something most people in this building could not do: they ran a full season of competitive matches, revised their bot allocations based on data, and predicted the outcomes in advance." She pauses. "The Arena Champion is Kinetic Falcons. Final Season 2 record: 7 − 2. What I want on record is not just that they won. It's what they did to be in a position to win. Falcons moved Power to 9 at the auction — the largest single-stat move in the league. ChargePower at Power 9 is 81. That number won them three matches in Season 2 and pushed their prediction card from 6 to a Season finish of 7. They overshot their own card by one win because the quadratic kept giving." Maya listens. Falcons did it differently and it worked. The acknowledgment is for the math, not the W column. She watches the Falcons table look at each other. Different math. Different bot. Same camp.

Best Teacher Award. "During the Community Exhibition this morning, every visitor who walked through that door was handed a ballot. They were asked one question: who explained the math in a way you actually understood? This is not a vote for who is the nicest or who talked the most. It is a vote for mathematical communication. Someone in this room made a visitor understand something they did not understand before they walked in. The Best Teacher award goes to Maya from Patient Foxes." Maya does not move for a second. Devon nudges her. She stands. The teacher: "Five visitors cast ballots. Three went to Maya. One ballot reads: 'she explained Endurance decay as a cliff, not a slope.' Another: 'she answered the trade-off question without flinching.' A third: 'she froze, used the bot as a reset, and came back with quadratic ≠ proportional.' The teaching was real. The math landed." Maya walks up. No hug, no applause cue. The teacher hands her a card and shakes her hand. Maya walks back. Devon, low: "I voted for you." Maya: "You can't vote. You were teaching." Devon: "I voted in my head."

Verbal Reflection. "For the last part of the ceremony, two things out loud. No notes. First: name one area where you grew as a problem solver. Second: name one specific moment from this program where you saw that growth in action. A day, a match, a calculation. Something that actually happened." She goes around the room. Cobalt, Iron Ravens, Raptors, Hawks, Falcons. Then Patience. Iris first. Growth: "I learned that a number can be exactly right and still be the wrong number to use." Moment: "Day 15 morning Gallery Walk. I read Steady Hawks' equation. Their residual was 0. Ours was −17. We picked the steepest slope. They picked a modest one. The modest one fit the data." The teacher writes Y on her log. Devon next. Growth: "I check the formula on paper before I trust the program output." Moment: "Day 15 Open Lab compute phase. Four MATCHes on paper. The reason I knew the R3 Endurance Ball loss wasn't a bug was that I ran the formula by hand." Y. Then Maya. The teacher: "Maya." Six seconds of thinking. Maya: "I'm less scared of being wrong out loud." Pause. "That probably sounds small. It's the biggest one." The teacher does not nod or say great. She waits for the second thing. Maya: "This morning. Visitor 3. I said chargePower 81 in front of him and it was supposed to be 64. Iris caught it. I fixed it. He smiled and asked the next question. On Day 1 I think I would have stopped talking for the rest of the day." The teacher writes Y next to Maya's name on the log. She moves to the next person.

The teacher waits until the last student has spoken. Then: "That's the ceremony." Pause. "In four weeks you programmed a bot from allocation decisions, you ran it through a full competitive season, and you taught what you know to someone who walked in off the street. That is not a beginner's program. Most adults have never done any one of those three things.".

Maya leaves Day 16 with a card in her hand that reads BEST TEACHER, the 177.8% in her own handwriting still on the Commissioner's wall, and a verbal reflection on a log that reads Y she will never see. The Day 1 goal card with "I want to not feel bad in math class" is two zones away from the Commissioner's wall. Both are still up. Both have her name on them. The Arena Champion is Kinetic Falcons, and Maya is happy for them, and that's also new. Three weeks ago she'd have measured the camp against the W column. Today she leaves the room with three things named — the math she did, the math she taught, and the math she said out loud as the moment she grew. The cliff of motor power decay at Endurance 4, the quadratic formula behind a 177.8% league rule, the modest slope that fit Hawks' data and broke hers — all of those are now sentences she has said. The teacher's last line is one sentence and Maya carries it home: "Most adults have never done any one of those three things." Maya is fourteen. Day 1 she didn't believe she could do math. Today she taught five people math, won an award for it, and got a Y next to her name on a log she will never see. That's the program. It ends.
OPEN LAB

Final Debrief and Prep — Name What Landed, Fix What Didn't

"You taught during the Community Exhibition. You explained math to someone who didn't know it. That's different from solving math. Take 60 seconds — what did it feel like when a visitor actually got it?" Maya thinks about the administrator's slow nod when she named the auction trade-off. Devon thinks about the visitor who picked up the dashboard tablet for the first time. Iris thinks about the high schooler nodding while she paraphrased Maya's Day 15 sentence. Then the teacher reads Q1 and Q2. Q1: "What did visitors understand quickly? Name the specific moment." Q2: "What explanation did not land? Write a revised version. The revision must differ in approach, not just be shorter." The teacher runs the comprehension check: "Before you start — tell your teammate what Q2 is asking you to do. Not Q1." Devon to Maya: "Find a moment that didn't work and write a different explanation. Not shorter — different approach." Maya nods. Hard stop: "Think for 10 seconds about the hardest moment — the visitor who looked the most stuck. Hold that moment. Now start."

Q1 — what landed. Patience picks three moments, one each. Maya writes hers: "The administrator who asked why I didn't make Endurance higher. I answered with the auction trade-off without pausing. She nodded slowly — that meant she got it." Iris writes hers: "The high schooler who asked if we'd build the model differently. I gave her the Day 15 modest-slope sentence and she stopped walking and asked me to say it again." Devon writes: "The visitor who picked up the dashboard. I didn't explain it. I just handed it over and she figured it out before I said anything. The silence worked." Q2 — what didn't. Maya pauses longer on this one. She writes: "Visitor 4 asked what was the hardest thing I learned. I froze. I knew the answer. I couldn't say it. Next time: start with the wrong sentence — the one I almost wrote on a worksheet — and let the visitor watch me catch it. Lead with the mess, not the answer." Iris reads it. "That's a different approach. Not shorter." Maya: "Yeah. Start with what I got wrong." The teacher walks past and listens to Maya read it once. She does not say great. She nods and moves to the next team.

Skill Bridge — individual, silent, two minutes. The teacher: "Put the team conversation down. Individual writing. Two minutes. No sharing aloud." She reads the prompt: "The skill I used today to teach a visitor was [name it], and I know it worked when [describe the specific moment you saw them understand it]. One sentence. Name the skill by name. Name the exact moment." Silence. Maya writes: "The skill I used today to teach a visitor was translating a quadratic formula into 'almost four times more push' instead of saying 'P squared.' I know it worked when Visitor 3 asked why one formula was times 5 and the other was squared — and I said 'two more Power points isn't twice more, it's almost four times more push on the ball' without planning the sentence. He stopped asking questions and looked at the bot." She caps her pen. Hands the card in at the door. The teacher does not read it in front of her. She tucks it into a folder with the others and looks at Maya for one second before turning back to the door.

"Teaching math is different from doing math. Today you did the harder one — and you did it well enough to hand it to someone else. That's the goal." Patience stays at the table for a moment. Devon picks up his Q2 sheet and folds it. Iris caps her pen.

Maya hands in a Skill Bridge card that reads, in her own handwriting: "translating a quadratic formula into 'almost four times more push' instead of saying 'P squared.'" She also leaves a Q2 revision in her data log that names her own freeze and offers a different approach for next time — start with the mistake, not with what I learned. The teacher's closing line is the second time today a teacher has named the same thing in different words. First was at Walk the Wall: "same variables, completely different level of precision." Second was the Skill Bridge close: "You learned it well enough to hand it to someone else." Three weeks ago Maya didn't have a sentence for what the program was about. Today two adults said it back to her in two different rooms.

Maya's Velocity Arena · A student-experience narrative for facilitators. Day 12 afternoon Season 2 first match still to be added. · Companion to the Running the Program guide and the Classroom Prep page.
